# How simplydata chooses methods

simplydata often chooses a method, an option value or a setting for you: Welch's t-test when you compare two
averages, Holm's method when you adjust many p-values, REML and Hartung-Knapp intervals when you combine
studies. This document answers the question a reviewer asks first: *how does simplydata know which method is
right, and what logic does it use?*

The answer is five principles, applied in order, and one entry for each of the 800 choices simplydata
makes. Each entry gives the default, the alternatives (and the option that asks for them), why simplydata
chose it, the published evidence, and what simplydata checks in your data before it uses the method.

## The five principles

1. **P1.** Follow published guidelines and consensus statements where they exist (for example the Cochrane Handbook, the ASA statement on p-values, the SAMPL guidelines, ICH E9 for clinical trials, the GUM for measurement). ([Wasserstein & Lazar 2016](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.2016.1154108); [Lang & Altman 2015](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijnurstu.2014.09.006); [ICH E9 1998](https://database.ich.org/sites/default/files/E9_Guideline.pdf); [Deeks et al. (Cochrane Handbook, ch. 10)](https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-10); [JCGM 100:2008 (GUM)](https://www.bipm.org/documents/20126/2071204/JCGM_100_2008_E.pdf); [Bland & Altman 1994](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.309.6949.248))
2. **P2.** Where no guideline exists, follow published method comparisons (simulation studies), and prefer the method that stays valid under fewer assumptions (Welch's t-test over Student's). ([Delacre et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.5334/irsp.82); [Veroniki et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1002/jrsm.1164))
3. **P3.** When the evidence is balanced, match the field's standard software default (the package is named), so results agree with what reviewers expect. Matching the reference package sometimes takes precedence over a method comparison; each such entry says which method the studies prefer and the option that gives it.
4. **P4.** Check the data where simplydata can: refuse or warn when the chosen method does not fit, and say what to use instead. Whether the rows are independent cannot be seen in the data: the learner says it (matched_by=, grouped_by=). ([Zuur et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2041-210X.2009.00001.x))
5. **P5.** Be open: the printout names the method; every default can be changed with an option; the manual shows the code underneath; the reliability study checks the numbers; automated review passes checked every default and its references (see 'How the defaults were reviewed').

## How to read the tables

- **Word or option**: the simplydata word, or a word with one of its options (`table.combine_studies test_method=`).
- **Default**: what simplydata does when you do not say otherwise.
- **Alternatives**: what else you can ask for, and how.
- **Why**: the principle followed (P1 to P5), the reasoning and the evidence, then **Checks**: what simplydata
  checks in your data, and when it refuses or warns and says what to use instead.
- **References**: short citations; the full list with links is at the end.

The basis of each default is one of three kinds. *published*: published guideline or method comparison; *convention*: the package named does this, so that results agree with it; studies may prefer another method, and when they do the entry says so (and names the option, if there is one); *starting value*: simplydata's own starting value: no published rule fixes it; change it with the option named. Of the 800 defaults, 166 rest on
published guidelines or method comparisons, 496 follow the convention of the package named so that
results match it, and 138 are simplydata's own starting values, marked as such, each changed with the
option named.

Every reference was checked before it was cited: each DOI was looked up in Crossref
(`https://api.crossref.org/works/<doi>`), which is where the authors, year, title and journal below come from;
the few references without a DOI were checked at their URL. The numbers themselves are checked against the
packages underneath in the reliability study (`validation/`), and every default can be changed with an option.

## How the defaults were reviewed

The defaults of the later batches (S3a, S3b and S3c) were set in design briefs, and each brief was read by an
automated review pass before it was built; the defaults of the earlier releases follow the rules of `PLAN.md`
(state every choice; match R and SciPy) and the docstrings. Then, in November 2026, every entry of this document
was read again by automated review passes told to act as hostile statistical referees (AI review, not named human
reviewers). They checked each reason against
the literature, looked every reference up in Crossref to confirm that it exists and supports the claim it is
attached to, and compared about 50 entries with the code. Their findings were applied: misattributed references
were replaced, overstated reasons were toned down, and the kind of basis was corrected. Where the evidence favours
another default than the one simplydata uses, the entry now says so; changing a default is a separate decision.

This document is made from `src/simplydata/_method_basis.py` by `docs/make_method_basis.py`; a test fails when
a word that chooses a method has no entry, or when this file is out of date.

## Start, show and save

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.get_example()` | Date columns are read as dates. | sdata.get_example() lists the tables. | **P5.** Practice data: dates read as dates let the date words work at once. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `sdata.get_example()` `from_r_package=` | R itself loads the dataset of an installed R package (utils::data, from the data file that holds it); groups (factors) become text, R's NA empty cells, and row names that carry information a first column "row name". | Leave out from_r_package= for simplydata's own practice tables. | **P5.** A dataset is used as the R package ships it, so results can be compared with the package's help page; nothing is downloaded or installed, so data never leave the computer (simplydata's privacy promise). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A missing package or dataset is refused with the nearest name and the install line (never run by simplydata). |  |
| `chart.save()` | 300 dots per inch with the empty edges trimmed; a chart is 8 x 6 inches unless set_size() says otherwise; .tif files are packed without loss (LZW). | quality="high" (600 dots per inch), resolution= any value, set_size() for an exact size. | **P5.** Showing results, not a method: 300 dots per inch is the usual lowest sharpness journals ask for line art and photos, and lossless packing keeps every pixel. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** resolution= below 10 and pictures of 65,536 pixels or more on a side are refused. |  |

## Chart types

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.draw_bars()` | Bars of the average with no error bars, unless error= asks for them. | error="standard deviation", "standard error" or "confidence interval"; show= for another summary; draw_all_dots() to show every value. | **P5.** A bar of averages hides the spread and is often misread (Weissgerber et al. 2015; Newman & Scholl 2012), and readers confuse the kinds of error bar (Belia et al. 2005; Cumming et al. 2007). So simplydata draws no interval it was not asked for (seaborn's own default is a bootstrap interval that changes from run to run), and names in a printed line the error bars it does draw. Leaving them out until asked is simplydata's own choice. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** error= works only with show="average"; a printed line says which error bars were drawn. | [Weissgerber et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pbio.1002128); [Newman & Scholl 2012](https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-012-0247-5); [Belia et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.10.4.389); [Cumming et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1083/jcb.200611141) |
| `chart.draw_bars()` `stacked=` | stacked="percent": each group's total as a percent of the stack's total (pandas div and sum), with the axis named "percent of total" the column; a group with no rows at an x counts as 0, with a note. | show="average" for shares of averages (with a note); stacked=True for the values themselves; also on draw_area. | **P5.** Showing results: shares are compared better when every stack has the same height, and a share means a part of a total, so totals are the normal. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Needs colour= a column to stack; values below 0 are refused; a stack whose total is 0 is drawn empty, with a note. |  |
| `chart.draw_lines()` | Each point is the average of its rows, with no band unless error= asks for one; straight lines between the points. | show= for another summary, error= for a band, joined_by= for curves or steps, with_dots=True. | **P5.** As for bars: no interval is drawn that was not asked for (seaborn's own default is a bootstrap interval that changes from run to run), and a printed line names the band that is drawn. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** error= works only with show="average". |  |
| `chart.draw_lines()` `in_row_order=` | Every row, joined in the order of the rows: no average, no sorting by x (seaborn so.Path, lineplot(sort=False, estimator=None)). | Without in_row_order=: the average at each x, joined from the smallest x. | **P3.** Showing results: a path such as a ship's track must follow the rows; an average or a sort would change it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Takes no show=, error= or joined_by=. |  |
| `chart.draw_dots()` `show=` | seaborn pointplot with no line between the dots; caps on error bars; colour groups side by side. | show= as on bars; error="standard deviation", "standard error" or "confidence interval". | **P5.** Showing results: a line between groups would suggest a trend that groups do not have; side by side, error bars do not cover each other. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** show="count" is refused (use draw_count_bars). |  |
| `chart.draw_dots()` `move_layer_by=` | The dots move the number given along x (seaborn so.Shift); on a group axis each group is 1 wide. | draw_lines, draw_all_dots and draw_dashes take move_layer_by= too. | **P3.** Showing results: x units, as seaborn so.Shift, so a second layer sits beside the first. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A number. |  |
| `chart.draw_area()` | Rows at the same x averaged; the space under each line filled down to 0 in its colour, see-through (alpha 0.35). | colour= for one area per group, stacked=True to pile them up, transparency=, thickness=. | **P5.** Showing results: the average follows draw_lines(), and see-through areas let groups show through each other. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** stacked=True needs colour= groups; y must hold numbers. |  |
| `chart.draw_histogram()` | 10 bins of equal width, counts on the y axis. | with_bins= (a number or the edges), bin_width=, show="percent" or "density". | **P5.** Ten bins is a fixed, predictable starting value, so the same data always give the same picture. The published rules choose the number of bins from the data instead (Sturges 1926; Scott 1979; Freedman & Diaconis 1981), as seaborn's own default does. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** with_bins= and bin_width= together are refused; more than 1000 bars are refused. | [Sturges 1926](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1926.10502161); [Scott 1979](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/66.3.605); [Freedman & Diaconis 1981](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01025868) |
| `chart.draw_histogram()` `y=` | 10 bins along each axis, coloured with viridis as heatmaps are, with a colour bar named after what is counted. | with_bins= one number; show="percent" or "density"; use_colour_scale(). | **P5.** Showing results: viridis is readable in grey and by readers with colour blindness. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** stacked=, cumulative=, bin_width= and drawn_as= do not go with y=. |  |
| `chart.draw_boxes()` | Tukey's box plot (seaborn boxplot): the box from the first to the third quartile, whiskers to the last value within 1.5 x IQR of the box, dots beyond. | draw_violins() or draw_all_dots(); there is no option for other whiskers. | **P3.** The 1.5 x IQR whisker is Tukey's own rule (Exploratory Data Analysis, 1977), the one R boxplot(), seaborn and most software draw; McGill, Tukey & Larsen 1978 and Frigge, Hoaglin & Iglewicz 1989 describe the variants in use. *Basis: convention.* | [Mcgill et al. 1978](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1978.10479236); [Frigge et al. 1989](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1989.10475612) |
| `chart.draw_violins()` | A Gaussian density with Scott's bandwidth (seaborn's default), cut at the smallest and largest value, with a small box inside. | draw_boxes() or draw_all_dots(); there is no option for the bandwidth. | **P3.** The violin plot is a box plot with a density trace (Hintze & Nelson 1998); Scott's rule is the default bandwidth of seaborn and SciPy (Scott 1992). Cutting at the data keeps the violin from showing impossible values, such as marks below 0. *Basis: convention.* | [Hintze & Nelson 1998](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1998.10480559); [Scott 1992](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316849) |
| `chart.draw_violins()` `in_halves=` | seaborn violinplot(split=True): the two groups of colour= as the two halves, cut at the smallest and largest value, with a small gap. | in_halves=False for two whole violins side by side. | **P5.** Showing results: two halves put two shapes face to face, so they are easy to compare. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Needs colour= a column. |  |
| `chart.draw_heatmap()` | viridis colours; when the values lie on both sides of 0, a blue-to-red scale centred at 0. Repeated cells are averaged; cluster=True uses average linkage on Euclidean distances. | use_colour_scale() for another scale; cluster=True. | **P2.** viridis shows equal steps in the data as equal steps in lightness and reads in grey and with colour blindness (Nuñez et al. 2018; Crameri et al. 2020); a diverging scale centred on a meaningful middle is the ColorBrewer advice (Harrower & Brewer 2003). The clustering is seaborn clustermap's own choice. **Checks:** cluster=True with empty cells, or with fewer than 2 rows or columns, is refused. | [Nuñez et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0199239); [Crameri et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-020-19160-7); [Harrower & Brewer 2003](https://doi.org/10.1179/000870403235002042) |
| `chart.draw_pie()` | One slice per label (the total of its values, or its number of rows), in the order the labels appear, from 12 o'clock going clockwise, each with its share in whole percents. | values= for totals instead of counts; order_legend() for another order; transparency=. | **P5.** Showing results: a pie read like a clock starts at the top and goes clockwise; whole percents are as precise as a slice can be read. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Values below 0, or all 0, are refused. |  |
| `chart.draw_pie()` `hole=` | A ring whose width is 1 - hole of the radius (matplotlib wedgeprops width), the percents in the middle of the ring. | No hole for a full pie. | **P5.** Showing results: percents inside the ring stay readable whatever the hole. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** hole= between 0 and 0.9. |  |
| `chart.draw_polar()` | Angles in degrees; a compass (0 at the top, clockwise) when the column is named like a direction or full_turn= is given, otherwise from the right, anticlockwise; the legend outside the circle. | full_turn=360, 24, 12 or "radians"; degrees=False (the old way of saying radians). | **P5.** Showing results: wind and current directions are read on a compass, and maths angles from the right; the column's name tells which one is meant. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** angle= and distance= must hold numbers. |  |
| `chart.draw_contour()` | A full grid drawn as it is (scattered points joined onto a 100 x 100 grid by straight-line interpolation); about 10 filled bands in viridis with thin solid white lines between them. | filled=False for lines with their heights written on them; use_colour_scale() for the colours and the bands. | **P5.** Showing results: viridis reads evenly and with colour blindness (as in use_colour_scale); straight-line interpolation invents no hills between the points. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** At least 2 different x and y values; scattered points need 3 not on one line. |  |
| `chart.draw_surface()` | A full grid drawn as it is (scattered points joined onto a 100 x 100 grid by straight-line interpolation), coloured with viridis from the lowest to the highest point. | use_colour_scale() for the colours; transparency=. | **P5.** Showing results: straight-line interpolation invents no hills between the points. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** At least 2 different x and y values. |  |
| `chart.draw_surface()` `with_height_lines=` | Ten height lines on the floor of the box (at the lowest height), in the surface's colours (matplotlib contour with zdir="z"). | as_wires=True for a wire frame instead of a filled surface. | **P5.** Showing results: lines on the floor show the shape without hiding any part of the surface. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.draw_dots_3d()` | One colour per group for words or a number column with 8 values or fewer; a colour scale for more. | use_colour_scale() for the scale; transparency=. | **P5.** Showing results: more than about 8 colours cannot be told apart, so many values get a scale instead. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.draw_band()` | One light band (alpha 0.25) per group in its colour, no edge line, named in the legend; empty cells leave a gap. | colour= one colour, labelled= the legend name, transparency=. | **P5.** Showing results: a light band keeps lines drawn on it readable, and a gap is more honest than a made-up edge. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** The low edge must not be above the high edge; one row per x in each group. |  |
| `chart.draw_map_of_empty_cells()` | Every column, one line per row from the top; an empty cell (missing or only spaces) in vermilion on light grey, with the number of empty cells under each column. | columns= for some columns only. | **P5.** Showing results: the same rule for an empty cell as the table words; the Okabe-Ito vermilion stands out for readers with colour blindness. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.draw_sticks()` | A stick 1.5 points wide from 0 to each value with a small dot on top, and a thin line at 0. | colour= for groups, thickness=. | **P5.** Showing results: a stick from 0 shows each value at its own place without joining places that are apart. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.draw_dashes()` | Each dash is 0.8 wide (width=), centred on its group, as seaborn so.Dash(width=0.8). | width= from above 0 to 1; move_layer_by= to move the dashes along x. | **P3.** Showing results: seaborn's own width leaves a gap between neighbouring groups. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** x= and y=, y of numbers; width= above 0 and at most 1. |  |

## Statistics

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `show=` | The average of each group (show="average"). | show="median", "total", "count" (bars only), "smallest", "biggest", "standard deviation". | **P3.** The average is what seaborn and most papers draw; the other summaries are one word away. A bar of averages alone hides the data (Weissgerber et al. 2015), so the manual pairs it with draw_all_dots(). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** draw_lines refuses show="count"; draw_bars without y= needs show="count". | [Weissgerber et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pbio.1002128) |
| `error=` | No error bars or band unless you ask; when you ask for the confidence interval, it is the 95 % t interval of the average (as R t.test()), not seaborn's bootstrap. | error="standard deviation" (the spread of the values), "standard error", "confidence interval". | **P5.** Researchers misread error bars when the kind is not said (Belia et al. 2005), and every figure should say which bars it shows (Cumming et al. 2007). simplydata prints the kind for the caption, and its t interval does not change from run to run. Having no default kind is simplydata's own choice: you must name one. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Refused with a summary other than the average, on stacked bars, and on lines that are not joined straight. | [Belia et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.10.4.389); [Cumming et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1083/jcb.200611141) |
| `chart.draw_error_bars()` | Bars of the standard deviation (of_kind="standard deviation") around the average at each x, groups moved a little apart. | of_kind="standard error" or "confidence interval" (95 % t interval); column= for your own sizes; dodge=False. | **P5.** The standard deviation describes the spread of the values; the kind is always printed, so the caption can say it (Cumming et al. 2007). error= on draw_bars has no default, while this word draws the standard deviation unless told otherwise. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Needs dots or lines first, at least 2 values per x (else column=), and no negative sizes. | [Cumming et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1083/jcb.200611141); [Belia et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.10.4.389) |
| `chart.fit_line()` | A least-squares straight line over the range of the data; no band unless with_confidence_band=True (then the 95 % band of the average y, as R predict(lm, interval = "confidence")). | with_confidence_band=True, show_equation=True; table.fit_line(test_method=...) for other lines. | **P3.** Least squares answers 'predict y from x', the usual question, and the band is the one R draws. Lines that treat x and y alike (Warton et al. 2006) are in table.fit_line(test_method=). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Only after dots; x and y must be numbers with at least 2 different x. | [Warton et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1017/S1464793106007007) |
| `chart.fit_curve()` | A polynomial of degree 2 by least squares (as R lm(y ~ poly(x, 2, raw = TRUE))). | of_degree= 1 to 10; fit_formula() for any formula. | **P5.** Degree 2 is the smallest curve that bends once. No study fixes the degree: it is a starting value, and table.fit_curve warns from degree 4, where polynomials wiggle at the ends. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Needs at least degree + 1 different x. |  |
| `chart.fit_formula()` | Nonlinear least squares by Levenberg-Marquardt (as minpack.lm::nlsLM(); R nls()'s Gauss-Newton gives the same estimates when both converge), with Wald t intervals for each value, as summary(nls). | As table.fit_formula: test_method=, search="whole range", between=. | **P3.** Least squares is what R nls() fits. The Wald intervals rest on a straight-line approximation; for strongly curved models Bates & Watts 1988 prefer profile-likelihood intervals, which R confint() gives and simplydata does not yet offer. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The left side must be the y of the dots, and only x may appear on the right. | [Bates & Watts 1988](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316757) |
| `chart.fit_distribution()` | The normal distribution fitted by maximum likelihood (as R MASS::fitdistr()), scaled to the histogram. | distribution= any name of sdata.distribution. | **P3.** Maximum likelihood is the standard way to fit a distribution (Venables & Ripley 2002; Delignette-Muller & Dutang 2015); a note says how the curve was scaled to counts. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Only after a histogram or density; at least 2 values. | [Venables & Ripley 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-21706-2); [Delignette-Muller & Dutang 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v064.i04) |
| `chart.fit_dose_response()` | The 4-parameter log-logistic curve (drc's LL.4), as table.fit_dose_response, drawn over 300 doses evenly spaced on a log scale, with the ED50 and its interval in the legend. | of_kind= as table.fit_dose_response. | **P3.** As table.fit_dose_response (Ritz et al. 2015; OECD 2006). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.fit_dose_response; needs R drc. | [Ritz et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0146021); [OECD 2006](https://doi.org/10.1787/9789264085275-en) |
| `chart.draw_curve()` | The curve over the x range of what is drawn, at 200 points; over a histogram scaled to its bars. | between=, steps=, show="cumulative", dashed=, colour=, labelled=, thickness=. | **P5.** Showing results: 200 points draw a smooth curve; scaling to the bars puts the curve on the same scale as the histogram, and a note says how. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.draw_smooth_line()` | LOWESS as R lowess() with its defaults: the nearest 2/3 of the dots, 3 robustness steps, delta = 1 % of the x range. | test_method="loess" (R loess, with a confidence band), "spline" (R smooth.spline), or a moving average and the other window methods. | **P3.** LOWESS is Cleveland's robust local regression (Cleveland 1979) and R's lowess() default; its robustness steps keep a single outlier from pulling the line. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A number for smoothness= with LOWESS is refused; with_confidence_band= needs test_method="loess"; window methods need evenly spaced x. | [Cleveland 1979](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1979.10481038) |
| `chart.draw_smooth_line()` `test_method="loess", with_confidence_band=` | R loess's own defaults: span 0.75, local curves of degree 2, no robustness steps (not lowess). | smoothness= another span. | **P3.** As table.add_smoothed_column(test_method="loess") (Cleveland & Devlin 1988). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 5 different x. | [Cleveland & Devlin 1988](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1988.10478639) |
| `chart.draw_interaction_lines()` | The average of each group joined by lines, with 95 % t intervals (as R t.test()), groups moved a little apart. | table.explain(using=[..., ...]) and table.find_model_averages_of(within_each_group_in=) for the test. | **P3.** seaborn's own default is a bootstrap interval that changes from run to run; the t interval is the one R and most textbooks give. The picture is printed with the reminder to test the interaction, not read it by eye. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** y must hold numbers; x and colour need 2 to 12 groups each (more: add_group_column or fit_line). | [Gelman & Stern 2006](https://doi.org/10.1198/000313006X152649) |
| `chart.draw_richness_curves()` | vegan's rarefaction curves (rarecurve()) for each row, about 50 points on the shortest curve, with a dotted line at the smallest row total. | colour= to colour the rows by a group. | **P3.** Richness can only be compared at the same number of counts (Hurlbert 1971); the dotted line marks the largest such number. The curves are vegan's; 50 points is a starting value for a smooth curve. Coverage-based curves (Chao & Jost 2012) are not yet offered. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Non-whole numbers are refused. | [Hurlbert 1971](https://doi.org/10.2307/1934145); [Chao & Jost 2012](https://doi.org/10.1890/11-1952.1) |
| `chart.draw_cluster_tree()` | Average linkage for Bray-Curtis distances, the same choice as table.add_clusters, so the tree matches the clusters; the cophenetic correlation as a note. | measure=, linkage=. | **P3.** As table.add_clusters(measure=): average linkage is the common choice for ecological distances (Borcard, Gillet & Legendre 2018), and Ward's method does not fit Bray-Curtis (Ward 1963). *Basis: convention.* | [Borcard et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-71404-2); [Ward 1963](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1963.10500845) |
| `chart.draw_variogram()` | The binned semivariances as dots and gstat's fitted curve at 200 distances (gstat::variogramLine). | of_kind= and between= as on find_variogram_of. | **P5.** The same fit as find_variogram_of (Cressie 1985; Pebesma 2004), so the picture and the numbers agree. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A fit find_variogram_of would refuse is refused here too. | [Cressie 1985](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01032109); [Pebesma 2004](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cageo.2004.03.012) |
| `chart.draw_threshold_check()` | Two panels: the mean excess with its 95 % interval (extRemes::mrlplot) and the generalised Pareto shape with its 95 % interval at 10 thresholds (extRemes::threshrange.plot). | between= for the thresholds to look at. | **P1.** Coles (2001, 4.3.1) chooses the threshold where the mean excess becomes linear and the shape stays stable; the two panels are extRemes's own (Gilleland & Katz 2016). **Checks:** Fewer than 10 values above the highest threshold refused; a note when mrlplot's thresholds stop early. | [Coles 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-3675-0); [Gilleland & Katz 2016](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v072.i08) |
| `chart.draw_answer_bars()` | Diverging stacked bars: the answers below the middle to the left of 0, those above it to the right, and the middle answer split across 0; two hues with grey in the middle. | table.count_each(columns=, in_order=) for the numbers. | **P2.** Diverging bars centred on the middle answer make agreement and disagreement comparable across questions (Heiberger & Robbins 2014). **Checks:** As table.count_each(columns=, in_order=); with an even number of answers the bars meet at 0 between the middle two. | [Heiberger & Robbins 2014](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v057.i05) |
| `chart.draw_density()` | A Gaussian kernel density with Scott's bandwidth (seaborn's default); each group's curve has area 1. | draw_histogram(show="density"); there is no option for the bandwidth. | **P3.** Scott's rule is the default of seaborn and SciPy (Scott 1992); R uses Silverman's. Both are rules of thumb made for bell-shaped data (Silverman 1986). The curve runs a little past the data (seaborn's cut=3), unlike draw_violins(). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** x must hold numbers. | [Scott 1992](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316849); [Silverman 1986](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781315140919) |
| `chart.draw_count_bars()` | One bar per group counting its rows, in simplydata's exact colours. | colour= to split the bars, sideways=True, transparency=. | **P5.** Showing results: exact colours keep the legend and the bars the same shade. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.draw_all_dots()` | Every value as a dot: a swarm (no overlap) when the biggest group has 100 values or fewer, else dots spread at random (fixed seed) and half transparent. | Put draw_boxes() or draw_violins() underneath; the dots then turn dark grey. | **P2.** Showing every value is the recommendation of Weissgerber et al. 2015 for small samples; above 100 values a swarm no longer fits. The fixed seed keeps the picture the same each time. | [Weissgerber et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pbio.1002128) |
| `chart.draw_side_histograms()` | Bins from NumPy's 'auto' rule (the larger of Sturges' and Freedman-Diaconis' numbers), at most 50, the same edges for every group. | There is no option for the bins here. | **P3.** The 'auto' rule chooses the number of bins from the data (Sturges 1926; Freedman & Diaconis 1981). *Basis: convention.* | [Sturges 1926](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1926.10502161); [Freedman & Diaconis 1981](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01025868) |
| `chart.draw_side_histograms()` `drawn_as=` | drawn_as="density": seaborn's density curve (kdeplot: Gaussian kernel, Scott's bandwidth) on each edge, filled; with colour groups one outline per group, each with an area of 1. | drawn_as="bars" (normal) for histograms. | **P5.** Showing results: each group's own curve shows its shape whatever its size, as draw_density() does. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** drawn_as= is "bars" or "density". |  |
| `chart.draw_qq_dots()` | Against the normal distribution (SciPy probplot): dots at Filliben's positions, and the line by least squares through the dots. | distribution= any name (fitted by maximum likelihood first); for_each_group_in=. | **P3.** The Q-Q plot is Wilk & Gnanadesikan's way to compare data with a distribution; Filliben's positions are SciPy's (Filliben 1975). R's qqnorm() uses slightly different positions and qqline() a line through the quartiles, which the help says. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 3 values are refused (a group is left out, with a note). | [Wilk & Gnanadesikan 1968](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/55.1.1); [Filliben 1975](https://doi.org/10.1080/00401706.1975.10489279) |
| `chart.draw_cumulative_share()` | Each group's share of values at or below each x, from 0 to 1, with the y-axis named in words. | colour= for groups, thickness=, transparency=. | **P5.** Showing results: the cumulative share shows every value, needs no bins and compares groups of any size. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** x must hold numbers. |  |
| `sdata.draw_all_pairs()` | Dots for each pair of columns; on the diagonal a histogram with seaborn's own bins, or with colour= a density with Scott's bandwidth. | columns= chooses up to 10 columns. | **P3.** seaborn pairplot's own choices (Scott 1992 for the bandwidth). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 2 or more than 10 number columns are refused. | [Scott 1992](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316849) |

## Lines and shading

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.draw_line_at_x()` | A grey dashed line 1.5 points wide; its label standing up along the line, near the top. | dashed=False, colour=, thickness=, labelled=. | **P5.** Showing results: a quiet line marks a reference value without hiding the data. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.draw_line_at_y()` | A grey dashed line 1.5 points wide, above bars and below dots; its label just outside the right edge. | dashed=False, colour=, thickness=, labelled=. | **P5.** Showing results: a quiet line marks a reference value without hiding the data, and a label outside the chart covers nothing. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.draw_line_at_y()` `between=` | From the low to the high value only (matplotlib hlines), grey, dashed and 1.5 points wide as the full line. | between= on draw_line_at_x too (vlines). | **P5.** Showing results: a target that holds for part of the range is drawn only there. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The low end must come first. |  |
| `chart.draw_line_with_slope()` | Through 0, 0, right across the chart: grey, dashed, 1.5 points wide, between bars and dots. | through=, intercept=, dashed=False, colour=, labelled=, thickness=. | **P5.** Showing results: the 1:1 line of observed against predicted values goes through 0, 0; a grey dashed line reads as a guide, not as data. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Ordinary axes only; through= and intercept= not both. |  |
| `chart.shade_between_x()` | A light grey band (alpha 0.2) from the bottom to the top of the chart, behind everything. | colour=; shade_between_y() for a band across. | **P5.** Showing results: a light band behind the data marks a span without hiding anything. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** The two ends must differ. |  |
| `chart.shade_between_y()` | A light grey band (20 % solid) behind everything else on the chart. | colour=; shade_between_x() for a band across. | **P5.** Showing results: a pale band behind the data marks a range without hiding any dot or line. *Basis: starting value.* |  |

## Words on the chart

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.write_title_on_top()` | One chart: the title of its axes. Several panels: one bold title over the whole figure. | label_x(), label_y() and write_caption_at_bottom() for the other words. | **P5.** Showing results: a title over several panels belongs to the figure, so it is not repeated on each panel. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.write_caption_at_bottom()` | Under the x-axis label, in letters 0.8 of the text size and slightly faded. | write_note() for words inside the chart. | **P5.** Showing results: smaller, softer letters mark the caption as a note, not a label, and hanging it from the label keeps it clear of turned tick names. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.label_x()` | One chart: the label of its x-axis. Several panels: their own x labels are cleared and one bold label goes under them all. | label_y() for the other axis. | **P5.** Showing results: one shared label is easier to read than the same words under every panel. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.label_y()` | One chart: the label of its y-axis. Several panels: their own y labels are cleared and one bold label goes beside them all. | label_x() for the other axis. | **P5.** Showing results: one shared label is easier to read than the same words beside every panel. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.write_note()` | Just above the place, on a soft background in the chart's own colour; with_arrow=True moves it 40 points towards the middle with an arrow. | with_arrow=True; on bar charts x can be the name under a bar. | **P5.** Showing results: the soft background keeps the words readable over lines and bars. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.write_numbers_on_bars()` | Each value just past the end of its bar (past its error bar), no decimals and a comma for thousands; stacked bars get the number in the middle of each piece. | with_decimals= from 0 to 6. | **P5.** Showing results: whole numbers keep the chart readable, and a little room is added so the numbers stay inside it. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.write_names_on_dots()` | Each name right of its dot, else left, above, below or in a corner, wherever it covers no other name or dot; a name with no free place is left out and counted. | colour=; a bigger chart (set_size()) makes room. | **P5.** Showing results: names that cover each other cannot be read, so a crowded name is left out and a line says how many. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Only after draw_dots(). |  |
| `chart.use_font_size()` | Every size grows by the same share (the size asked for over the style's text size), so the title stays the biggest. | use_style("presentation") or "school" for bigger words and thicker lines together. | **P5.** Showing results: keeping the steps between title, labels and tick names keeps the order of importance. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Sizes outside 4 to 60 are refused. |  |
| `chart.use_font_size()` `title=` | title= and ticks= replace only those sizes; the other sizes follow the first number as before. | use_font_size(12) alone scales every size together. | **P5.** Showing results: journals often ask for one size for tick words and another for titles. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Sizes from 4 to 60. |  |
| `chart.use_font()` | The font for every word of the chart, used while it is drawn and saved. | Any installed font, or "serif", "sans-serif", "monospace". | **P5.** Showing results: a setting used only while drawing keeps your other charts as they were. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A font that is not installed is refused, with close names. |  |

## Axes

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.set_range_x()` | Both ends as given; None keeps that end where matplotlib put it; a flipped axis stays flipped. | set_range_y() for the other axis. | **P5.** Showing results: fixing one end (often 0) and letting the other choose itself is the common case. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The low end must be below the high end; not 0 or below on a log scale. |  |
| `chart.set_range_y()` | The y-axis runs from low to high; None keeps that end where matplotlib put it, and a flipped axis stays flipped. | set_range_x(); flip_y(). | **P5.** Showing results: the user chooses the range; a range below 0 on a log scale is refused. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** An empty range, or low above high, is refused with the right order written out. |  |
| `chart.use_log_scale_y()` | The log scale is set before drawing, so ranges and ticks are worked out on it. | use_log_scale_x(). | **P5.** Showing results: setting it first stops matplotlib from undoing later ranges and ticks; zero and negative values, which a log scale cannot show, are refused with the count of them. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.put_ticks_x()` | Ticks at the places given; a name is looked up among the names under the bars and keeps its name. | labels=, every=, how_many=, whole_numbers=. | **P5.** Showing results: on bar charts the names are what people know, not the places 0, 1, 2 behind them. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.rotate_labels_x()` | 45 degrees, each name lined up by the end nearest its tick and turned about that end. | Any angle from -360 to 360, such as 90 to stand the names up. | **P5.** Showing results: lined up by its end, a turned name stays under its own bar; matplotlib's centred names drift sideways. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.rotate_labels_y()` | 45 degrees, each name turned about its end next to the axis. | Any angle from -360 to 360. | **P5.** Showing results: turned about its end, a name stays beside its own bar. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.write_ticks_x_as()` | "thousands": a comma between thousands and no decimals unless with_decimals= asks. | "percent" (out_of=), "scientific", "plain", with_decimals=, or how a date should look. | **P5.** Showing results: 12,500 is read faster than 12500.0. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A number format on a date axis, or on names, is refused. |  |
| `chart.write_ticks_y_as()` | "percent" treats the numbers as out of 100; decimals chosen by matplotlib unless with_decimals= says. | out_of=1 for shares, "thousands", "scientific", "plain", or how a date should look. | **P5.** Showing results: the user names the format; only the tick words change, never the data. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A number format on a date axis, or on group names, is refused. |  |
| `chart.add_second_y()` | A second y-axis on the right in the first colour the chart does not use yet; one value per x (the average of its rows); the first chart kept in front. | drawn_as="lines", "bars" or "dots"; colour=; labelled=. | **P5.** Showing results: two y-axes are easy to misread, so the second axis, its label and its tick numbers are all written in the colour of its data, and one legend holds both. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Sideways bars are refused: they have no y numbers to share. |  |

## Colours and look

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.use_colour()` | The exact colour asked for, for every chart type without groups. | use_colours() for groups; colour= on one chart type. | **P5.** Showing results: seaborn dulls bar colours a little by default; simplydata draws the colour named. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A misspelt colour name gets the closest known name as a suggestion. |  |
| `chart.use_colours()` | The Okabe-Ito colours for groups (blue, orange, green, vermilion, reddish purple, sky blue, yellow, black). | use_colours("a", "b", ...) for your own; use_colour() for one colour. | **P1.** Readers with colour blindness can tell the Okabe-Ito colours apart, and they stay distinct in print (Okabe & Ito 2008; Wong 2011). **Checks:** With more groups than colours the colours repeat, and a note says so (once). | [Okabe & Ito 2008](https://jfly.uni-koeln.de/color/); [Wong 2011](https://doi.org/10.1038/nmeth.1618) |
| `chart.use_colour_scale()` | viridis for numbers; with middle=, a blue-to-red scale (RdBu) with the same change of colour per unit on both sides. | Any named scale (cividis, magma, blues ...), your own colours, log=True, steps=, between=. | **P2.** Rainbow scales distort the data and fail with colour blindness; perceptually uniform scales such as viridis and cividis do not (Liu & Heer 2018; Nuñez et al. 2018; Crameri et al. 2020). Diverging scales belong to data with a meaningful middle (Harrower & Brewer 2003). **Checks:** log=True with values at or below 0 is refused; middle= with log= or steps= is refused; an unknown name is refused with a list of colour-blind-safe scales; a rainbow scale (jet, rainbow, hsv ...) gets a note (once). | [Liu & Heer 2018](https://doi.org/10.1145/3173574.3174172); [Nuñez et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0199239); [Crameri et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-020-19160-7); [Harrower & Brewer 2003](https://doi.org/10.1179/000870403235002042) |
| `chart.use_style()` | "paper": 12-point text, no top and right lines, light grid lines across, Okabe-Ito colours. | "school", "presentation" (bigger words, thicker lines) and "dark". | **P5.** Showing results: the style is a set of matplotlib settings used only while the chart is drawn and saved, so other charts are never changed. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.set_size()` | Inches, as matplotlib measures; centimetres and millimetres are turned into inches; a saved file is exactly that size. | unit="cm", "mm" or "pixels". | **P5.** Showing results: journals give sizes in centimetres or millimetres, so they are taken as written. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.show_grid()` | Grid lines in both directions (the paper look draws only the lines across). | hide_grid() for none. | **P5.** Showing results: lines across help to read values off bars and lines; lines both ways help on dots. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.put_legend_at_top()` | One row (up to 6 entries) above the chart, outside it; the chart's title becomes the legend's heading. | put_legend_at_bottom(), put_legend_on_left(), put_legend_on_right(), hide_legend(). | **P5.** Showing results: outside the chart a legend covers no data, and the title as its heading keeps the two from sitting on top of each other. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.put_legend_at_bottom()` | Outside the chart, below the x-axis label, in one row (up to 6 entries in a row). | put_legend_at_top(), put_legend_on_left(), put_legend_on_right(), hide_legend(). | **P5.** Showing results: outside the chart a legend covers no data; entries of every panel are gathered into one. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.put_legend_on_left()` | Outside the chart, beside the y-axis label. | put_legend_at_bottom(), put_legend_at_top(), put_legend_on_right(), hide_legend(). | **P5.** Showing results: outside the chart a legend covers no data. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.put_legend_on_right()` | Outside the chart on the right, entries of every panel gathered in one legend. | put_legend_at_top(), put_legend_at_bottom(), put_legend_on_left(), hide_legend(). | **P5.** Showing results: outside the chart a legend covers no data. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.hide_legend()` | The legends of every panel and of a second y-axis are removed. | show_legend() brings it back; write_names_on_dots() labels groups on the chart. | **P5.** Showing results: the user asks for it; colour is then no longer explained, so it suits charts whose groups are named in another way. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.label_legend()` | The name above every legend of the chart (panels and second y-axis too). | label_legend("") for no name. | **P5.** Showing results: without it the legend is named after the colour= column. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A chart with no legend is refused. |  |

## Order of groups

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.order_x()` | The rows are put in the order named with a stable sort, so every chart type and later step follows it; groups not named come after. | biggest_first=True or False; month and weekday names come in calendar order without asking. | **P5.** Showing results: ordering the rows, not one chart call, keeps bars, colours, lines and dots in the same order, and a stable sort changes nothing else. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Names that are not in the column are refused. |  |
| `chart.order_y()` | The groups in the order named, the first at the top, by a stable sort of the rows; others after them. | biggest_first=True or False. | **P5.** Showing results: read from the top, the first group is the one named first. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.order_legend()` | The colour groups in the order named, by a stable sort of the rows; the first gets the first colour. | Groups left out come after the ones named. | **P5.** Showing results: one order for the legend, the colours and the bars side by side. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Several charts

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.draw_one_chart_for_each_group_in()` | Up to 3 small charts in a row, about 4 x 3.3 inches each, sharing their axis ranges, named by their group. | per_row=, down= and across=, same_x=False, same_y=False. | **P5.** Showing results: small charts on the same scale can be compared at a glance; their own ranges would hide differences in size. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `sdata.put_side_by_side()` | Each chart in a part of the figure of its own, at 3/4 of its size, with its own ranges. | same_x=True, same_y=True, with_letters=True. | **P5.** Showing results: a part of its own keeps each chart's title, labels and legend with it. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `sdata.put_one_above_other()` | Each chart in a part of the figure of its own, at 3/4 of its size, with its own ranges. | same_x=True (suits time series), same_y=True, with_letters=True. | **P5.** Showing results: a part of its own keeps each chart's title, labels and legend with it. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `sdata.put_in_rows_and_columns()` | 2 charts in a row, each in a part of the figure of its own at 3/4 of its size; a page title adds half an inch. | per_row=, same_x=True, same_y=True, with_letters=True. | **P5.** Showing results: a part of its own keeps each chart's title, labels and legend with it. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `chart.add_zoom_box()` | A box in the top right corner, 0.39 x 0.4 of the chart, showing the chart's own drawing again, with lines to the part it shows. | where="top left", "bottom right" or "bottom left". | **P5.** Showing results: drawing the same steps again keeps the box exactly like the chart. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Flat charts only; the two ends of each span must differ. |  |
| `chart.animate()` | One picture for each value, smallest first, 0.8 seconds each, every picture with the axis ranges of the whole data; saved as .gif at 100 dots per inch. | seconds_per_frame=, keep=True. | **P5.** Showing results: fixed ranges keep the bars and dots from jumping about, so the change between pictures is the change in the data. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A column with fewer than 2 values is refused. |  |

## More chart types

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.draw_wind_rose()` | A compass (north at the top, clockwise); bars every 30 degrees centred on their direction, counted by NumPy histogram2d; about five speed bands at round numbers (matplotlib MaxNLocator), stacked slowest first. | every= for the width of the bars; without speed= one count per direction. | **P5.** Showing results: wind and current roses are read as compasses; a bar centred on north holds the winds from 345 to 15 degrees, as in meteorology. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** every= must go into 360 evenly; directions are degrees. |  |
| `chart.draw_arrow()` | A plain arrow 1.5 points wide from start to end, in the text colour; words from labelled= centred on the start. | colour=, thickness=, labelled=. | **P5.** Showing results: an arrow that starts and ends exactly at the places given points at what it means to. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** start= and end= must be two different [x, y] places. |  |
| `chart.put_chart_inside()` | The other chart in the top-left corner, 0.4 of this chart's size, 0.03 in from the edges, with small text. | where= another corner; size= from 0.1 to 0.6. | **P5.** Showing results: a small overview or close-up in a corner keeps the main chart readable. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Only a flat chart with one panel goes inside; a chart cannot go inside itself. |  |

## Breaks and other scales

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.break_between_y()` | The matplotlib gallery's broken axis: the chart drawn twice on two panels of equal height that share x, the lower one up to the first number and the upper one from the second, with short slanted lines at the break. | Leave the break out and use use_log_scale_y() or a second chart when the values are far apart. | **P5.** Showing results: a broken axis hides how far apart the two parts are, so simplydata prints a note to say in the caption what is left out, and a web page refuses it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Both numbers inside the drawn range: something must be drawn below the first and above the second. |  |

## More marks

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `chart.draw_hexagon_bins()` | About 20 hexagons across the x range (matplotlib hexbin gridsize=20); hexagons with no rows left out (mincnt=1); viridis, with a colour bar named "count". | with_bins= another number of hexagons across; use_colour_scale() for other colours. | **P5.** Showing results: viridis is readable in grey and by readers with colour blindness, as on heat maps; empty hexagons drawn in the lowest colour would look like a count of 1. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** with_bins= a whole number from 3 to 200. |  |
| `chart.draw_time_bars()` | Bars 0.8 of a row high, the first row of the table at the top, one colour (or one per group of colour=). | colour= a column for groups; label_x / label_y for the axis names. | **P5.** Showing results: a plan is read from the top down, in the order the rows were written. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** start= and end= both dates or both numbers; no row may end before it starts. |  |
| `chart.draw_rectangle()` | Filled with a quarter of the colour (see-through), with a solid edge 1.5 points wide, in the first colour. | hollow=True for the outline only; colour= and transparency= for another look. | **P5.** Showing results: a see-through fill marks an area without hiding the marks inside it. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** x_between= and y_between= each two numbers (or dates), the low end first. |  |
| `chart.draw_residual_dots()` | seaborn residplot (a straight line by least squares, a dotted line at 0), with the y-axis named "residual of" the column. | fit_line() on the dots themselves to see the line. | **P5.** Showing results: the axis name says the dots are what is left after the line, not the column itself. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** x= and y= columns of numbers. |  |
| `chart.draw_event_lines()` | One row of lines for each group of y=, in the order they first appear; lines 0.8 of a row long and 1.5 points wide (matplotlib eventplot). | Without y= all lines on one row; thickness= for the width. | **P5.** Showing results: rows in the table's order keep the groups where the reader expects them. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** colour= is the column of y= or one colour. |  |
| `chart.draw_bars_3d()` | One bar per row, standing on 0, 0.8 of the smallest gap between two places wide and deep; names along an axis at 0, 1, 2 ... in the order they first appear; a note that a heat map is often clearer. | draw_heatmap() for the same numbers in two dimensions. | **P5.** Showing results: 3D bars hide each other and their heights are hard to read, so simplydata says so once. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Each place (x and y) needs one row. |  |

## Tables: start and look

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.table()` | The separator of a text file is guessed from the file. | separator=, decimal_mark=, thousands_mark=, empty_values=, skip_rows=, encoding=. | **P5.** Reading data: files from other countries use ; or tabs; every guess can be set by hand. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `table.show_first_rows()` | Rows numbered from 1, the way people count; only the display changes. | as_pandas() gives the table with pandas' own row labels (from 0). | **P5.** Showing data, not a statistical method: numbering from 1 matches the row numbers the other position words use (keep_rows_by_position, remove_rows_by_position). *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.show_last_rows()` | Each row numbered with its place in the whole table, counted from 1; only the display changes. | show_first_rows(), show(rows=). | **P5.** Showing data, not a statistical method: the numbers are the ones keep_rows_by_position() and remove_rows_by_position() take. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.show()` | Every row and column (up to 1,000 rows), numbered from 1. | rows= for the first rows only. | **P5.** Showing data: pandas hides the middle of a long table behind "..."; a beginner who asks to see the table wants to see it. The limit keeps a huge table from flooding the screen, and a line says how to see more. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A table of more than 1,000 rows shows its first 1,000 and says so. |  |
| `table.describe_columns()` | Number columns get smallest, biggest and average; text columns the number of different values; empty cells (also blank text) are counted and left out; numbers are shown rounded to 2 decimals. | find_summary_of() for more measures and full precision. | **P5.** A first look at the data, not a statistical method; the rounding is only for reading. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `table.get_row()` | Rows counted from 1; empty cells become None, the same on both engines. | get_row("last"). | **P5.** Data handling: counting from 1 matches the other position words, and None is Python's own word for nothing, where pandas would give nan. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.get_cell_at()` | Rows counted from 1, the column by name; an empty cell gives None with a note. | row="last". | **P5.** Data handling: counting from 1 matches the other position words. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.show_differences_from()` | Rows compared in order, two empty cells the same (pandas compare); differing cells listed as row, column, this, other. | check_if_same_as() for a plain same or not. | **P4.** Data preparation: after cleaning, the cells a step changed should be the ones meant; listing only those makes the check quick. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Tables with other columns or another number of rows are not compared cell by cell: simplydata says how they differ. |  |
| `table.check_if_same_as()` | The same columns in the same order, the same rows, the same values and the same kinds (pandas and Polars equals); two empty cells count as the same. | show_differences_from() lists every differing cell. | **P4.** Data preparation: a strict check (kinds too) catches a column that turned from whole numbers into decimals or text; the sentence names the first difference. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.check_if_all_values_in()` | Empty cells are left out, with a note, as pandas all() and Polars all() leave them out; a column with no values gives True (as pandas all() of an empty column), and the sentence says so. | check_if_any_value_in(); a list of columns checks them all (Polars all_horizontal); for_each_group_in= gives the answer for each group (pandas groupby().all()). | **P3.** Data handling: an empty cell is not known to meet or fail a condition, so it is left out, as both packages do; the sentence says how many values fail and the first row that does. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One condition at a time; a column without True and False needs a condition. |  |
| `table.check_if_any_value_in()` | Empty cells are left out, with a note, as pandas any() and Polars any() leave them out. | check_if_all_values_in(); a list of columns (Polars any_horizontal); for_each_group_in= for each group (pandas groupby().any()). | **P3.** Data handling: as check_if_all_values_in(); the sentence says how many values meet the condition and the first row that does. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One condition at a time; a column without True and False needs a condition. |  |
| `table.check_if_sorted_by()` | Smallest first, values that tie may follow each other (pandas is_monotonic_increasing, Polars is_sorted()); empty cells are left out, with a note. | biggest_first=True checks the other direction (is_monotonic_decreasing, is_sorted(descending=True)). | **P3.** Data handling: pandas says False for a column with an empty cell, which would hide whether the filled values are in order; leaving empty cells out and saying so answers the question asked. The first row out of order is named. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** biggest_first= is True or False; groups in their own order are read in that order. |  |

## Choose columns

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.keep_columns_by_position()` | Columns counted from 1 at the left, both ends kept. | "last", step=, a list of positions. | **P5.** Data preparation: "columns 1 to 3" means three columns, as people (and R) say it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** simplydata names the columns it kept. |  |
| `table.remove_columns_by_position()` | Columns counted from 1 at the left, both ends removed. | "last", step=, a list of positions. | **P5.** Data preparation: the same counting as keep_columns_by_position(). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Removing every column is refused; the removed columns are named. |  |
| `table.clean_column_names()` | R janitor::clean_names(): % becomes percent and # number, letters become plain ASCII, every run of other signs one "_", small letters, an x before a leading digit, _2 for a repeated name; pH becomes ph (janitor would split the capital: p_h). | rename_column() for one name of your own. | **P3.** Data preparation: a fixed published rule (the janitor package, widely used in R) gives the same names every time and on every computer; not splitting capitals inside a word keeps unit and chemistry names (pH, OD600) readable. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Each change is printed, old -> new; nothing changes when the names are already clean. |  |

## Choose rows

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.keep_rows_where()` | is_above= means strictly above (>); rows with an empty cell in the column are left out, as pandas and Polars do. | is_at_least= for >=; the other conditions (is_below=, is_between= ...). | **P3.** The words say exactly which comparison is made; leaving out empty cells is what pandas, Polars and R's subset() do. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.keep_rows_where()` `time_of_day_between=` | Both clock times included, on any day; a start after the end goes past midnight, as pandas between_time (Polars dt.time().is_between()); a part of a second counts. | ["08:00", "08:00"] keeps one clock time (pandas at_time); is_after= and is_before= for whole dates. | **P3.** Data handling: the same rows as pandas between_time, whose default includes both ends. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column of dates with times, or times; two clock times such as "08:00" or "08:00:30"; on its own. |  |
| `table.remove_rows_where()` | A row whose cell is empty is never removed by a comparison on that cell. | is_empty=True removes the empty rows; the same conditions as keep_rows_where(). | **P3.** Data preparation: an empty cell is not known to be below or above anything, so a comparison never removes it; pandas behaves so, and Polars is made to agree. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many rows were removed. |  |
| `table.keep_first_rows()` `for_each_group_in=` | The first rows of each group in the table's present order; the rows keep their places (pandas groupby().head(), Polars int_range().over()); an empty group cell is a group of its own. | sort_by() first to choose which rows come first; keep_last_rows and keep_rows_by_position take for_each_group_in= too. | **P5.** Polars group_by().head() would put each group's rows together; keeping the order makes the result easy to check against the table. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.keep_rows_by_position()` | Positions counted from 1, both ends kept. | step= for every n-th row, a list for chosen rows, to="last". | **P5.** Data preparation: counting from 1 with both ends kept is how people (and R) say "rows 10 to 20". *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.remove_rows_by_position()` | Rows counted from 1; a run keeps both ends. | step=, to=, a list of positions. | **P5.** Data preparation: the numbers are the ones show_first_rows() and show_rows_with_outliers() print. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Removing every row is refused. |  |
| `table.keep_random_rows()` `for_each_group_in=` | The same number of random rows from each group (pandas groupby().sample(random_state=)), rows kept in table order; a group with that many rows or fewer is kept whole, with a note. | percent= for the same share of each group; without for_each_group_in= the rows come from the whole table. | **P5.** A stratified random sample guarantees every group in the sample and is the usual design behind survey weights (Lumley 2010). Keeping a small group whole instead of stopping is simplydata's own choice, said in a note. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** with_seed= must be a whole number; empty group cells form a group of their own. | [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.keep_random_rows()` `percent=` | That share of each group, rounded by pandas (groupby().sample(frac=)), and at least 1 row of every group. | A number of rows for each group. | **P5.** Proportional allocation keeps each group's share of the sample equal to its share of the table (Lumley 2010); at least one row per group keeps every stratum, simplydata's own choice. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** percent= must be above 0 and up to 100; it cannot go with a number of rows. | [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.remove_duplicate_rows()` | The first of the repeated rows is kept, and the rows keep their order. | columns= to look only at some columns. | **P3.** Data preparation: keeping the first, in order, gives the same table every time (Polars' own default keeps any one of them). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many rows were removed. |  |
| `table.keep_biggest_rows()` | Exactly n rows; on a tie at the cut the rows higher up in the table are kept; empty cells never kept; groups in order, biggest first. | for_each_group_in=; keep_smallest_rows(). | **P5.** Data preparation: a fixed number of rows and the table's own order for ties make the result repeat; a note says when tied rows were left out, so nothing is hidden. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Notes for rows left out on a tie and for empty cells. |  |
| `table.keep_smallest_rows()` | Exactly n rows; on a tie at the cut the rows higher up in the table are kept; empty cells never kept; groups in order, smallest first. | for_each_group_in=; keep_biggest_rows(). | **P5.** Data preparation: as keep_biggest_rows(). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Notes for rows left out on a tie and for empty cells. |  |
| `table.show_rows_with_outliers()` | Tukey's rule: values more than 1.5 x IQR below the first or above the third quartile (quartiles as R quantile(type = 7)), within each group when asked. | test_method="3 IQR" (any k IQR), "3 standard deviations" (any k), or a test: "grubbs", "dixon", "rosner" (run in R). | **P3.** Tukey's fences are the box-plot rule and are not pulled by the outliers themselves, unlike a rule of k standard deviations. They flag about 0.7 % of clean bell-shaped values, so in a sample of 100 one value is often flagged (Hoaglin, Iglewicz & Tukey 1986); Hoaglin & Iglewicz 1987 suggest k = 2.2 for labelling, and Leys et al. 2013 the median plus or minus 2.5 MAD. The rule shows rows to look at, not rows to remove. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Refused for non-number columns. A k-SD rule that no value could reach with n values is flagged; the tests warn that they assume bell-shaped data and skip groups with fewer than 3 values. | [Hoaglin et al. 1986](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1986.10478363); [Hoaglin & Iglewicz 1987](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1987.10478551); [Leys et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jesp.2013.03.013) |
| `table.remove_rows_with_outliers()` | The same rule as show_rows_with_outliers: Tukey's 1.5 x IQR fences. | test_method= as show_rows_with_outliers. | **P3.** As show_rows_with_outliers. ICH E9 asks that outlier handling be decided in advance and that results be reported with and without the values; simplydata names every row it removes, so that both analyses can be run. With 1.5 x IQR, clean values are removed too in larger samples (Hoaglin & Iglewicz 1987). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As show_rows_with_outliers; the removed rows are printed. | [Hoaglin et al. 1986](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1986.10478363); [Hoaglin & Iglewicz 1987](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1987.10478551); [ICH E9 1998](https://database.ich.org/sites/default/files/E9_Guideline.pdf) |
| `table.show_duplicate_rows()` | Every copy is shown, the first one too (pandas duplicated(keep=False), Polars is_duplicated()), with its row number counted from 1; two empty cells count as the same; the table does not change. | columns= to compare only some columns; remove_duplicate_rows(keep=) to remove them. | **P5.** Data preparation: looking at the repeats before removing them shows whether they are true repeats (pasted twice) or different measurements that share a key. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many rows repeat and how many extra copies there are. |  |
| `table.keep_groups_where()` | Whole groups stay or go, in their places; empty cells are left out of each summary; a group whose summary cannot be worked out is left out (pandas groupby().filter(), Polars filter(... .over())). | count() for the rows of a group; summaries joined with and / or. | **P4.** Data preparation: a condition on single rows is refused here (keep_rows_where is the word for it), so a group condition cannot be confused with a row condition. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many groups and rows stayed. |  |
| `table.remove_groups_where()` | Whole groups go, the others stay in their places; a group whose summary cannot be worked out is never removed. | keep_groups_where() for the opposite. | **P4.** The same rule as keep_groups_where, turned round: a group is removed only when the condition is surely true. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many groups and rows went. |  |

## Empty cells

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.count_empty_cells_in()` `show="patterns"` | One row for each pattern of empty cells, with how many rows have it and how many cells it leaves empty (as R mice::md.pattern()), sorted by the number of empty cells, and the empty cells of each column printed below. | Without show=, the empty cells of each column; chart.draw_map_of_empty_cells() draws every row. | **P5.** Which cells are empty together is the first step before choosing how to handle them: monotone patterns and rows with many empty cells call for different methods (van Buuren & Groothuis-Oudshoorn 2011). **Checks:** An empty cell is a missing value, NaN or text of only spaces, as everywhere in simplydata. | [van Buuren & Groothuis-Oudshoorn 2011](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v045.i03) |
| `table.count_empty_cells_in()` `columns=` | Counts the empty cells of the columns named, in the order named; an empty cell is a missing value, NaN or text of only spaces, as for every column. | table.count_empty_cells_in() for every column; show="patterns" for which columns are empty together. | **P5.** A learner who wants the empty cells of one column names it, as in pandas df["OD600"].isna().sum(); spaces-only text counts as empty because a spreadsheet shows it as an empty cell. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column not in the table is refused with the nearest name. |  |
| `table.remove_rows_with_empty_cells_in()` | Missing cells, NaN and cells of only spaces all count as empty. | A column name, a list of them, or none for every column. | **P5.** Data preparation: a blank text cell holds no value, so it is treated like a missing one everywhere in simplydata. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many rows were removed. |  |
| `table.fill_empty_cells_in()` | Fill with the value asked for: the average or median of the filled cells, the value above or below, or a line or curve between neighbours. | with_value=, with_the=, sorted_by=, for_each_group_in=. | **P5.** simplydata never fills on its own: the user chooses how, and the sentence says how many cells were filled. Filling with one value makes the spread look smaller; multiple imputation (empty_cells="multiple imputation" on the analysis words) keeps that uncertainty. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.add_is_empty_column()` | True where the cell is missing, NaN or blank text. | Use it before fill_empty_cells_in(). | **P5.** Data preparation: the same meaning of empty as every other simplydata word; marking filled cells lets a report say which values were filled in. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many rows are True. |  |
| `table.remove_rows_that_are_all_empty()` | A row goes only when every cell is empty; an empty cell is a missing value or text of only spaces (pandas dropna(how="all"), Polars all_horizontal(is_null)). | remove_rows_with_empty_cells_in() removes a row for one empty cell. | **P5.** Data preparation: spreadsheet exports leave blank rows between blocks of data; text of only spaces looks empty in a spreadsheet, so it counts as empty here as everywhere in simplydata (pandas and Polars count it as filled). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many rows went. |  |
| `table.remove_columns_that_are_all_empty()` | A column goes only when every cell is empty (a missing value or text of only spaces). | remove_columns() to remove columns by name. | **P5.** Data preparation: a column nobody filled in carries no information; the same definition of an empty cell as everywhere in simplydata. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A table whose every cell is empty is refused; the sentence names the columns that went. |  |
| `table.fill_empty_cells_from()` | Only empty cells take the other table's value (a missing value or text of only spaces); a filled cell never changes (pandas combine_first, Polars join + coalesce). | join_with() to bring the other table's columns in; fill_empty_cells_in(from_column=) to fill from a column of the same row. | **P4.** Data preparation: pandas update() would overwrite filled cells, which loses data typed by hand; a key that is in the other table twice is refused, so it is never unclear which row fills. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Keys of different kinds and columns of different kinds are refused; the sentence counts the cells filled in each column. |  |
| `table.show_rows_with_empty_cells_in()` | A row is shown when any of its cells (or of the columns= named) is empty, with its row number; text of only spaces counts as empty. | columns= for some columns only; remove_rows_with_empty_cells_in() or fill_empty_cells_in() afterwards. | **P4.** Data preparation: looking at the rows with a hole before removing them shows whether they are missing at random or all from one place or time. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Fix columns

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.turn_into_numbers()` | A comma between groups of three digits is a thousands mark, any other single comma a decimal comma; cells that are not numbers become empty and are reported. | sdata.table(..., decimal_mark=",", thousands_mark=".") when reading a file. | **P4.** Data preparation: the rule reads both English and European writing of numbers, and every cell that could not be read is reported, so nothing is lost silently. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Cells that do not read as numbers are counted and up to 3 are shown. |  |
| `table.turn_into_text()` | Dates as year-month-day (with the time only when some date has one); True/False as "True"/"False"; empty cells stay empty. | turn_into_numbers(), turn_into_dates(). | **P5.** Data preparation: ISO 8601 dates sort in time order as text, and an empty cell should not become the word "nan". *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column that is already text is left as it is. |  |
| `table.turn_into_text()` `written_like=` | An example date is turned into strftime codes by a fixed table of its parts: the four-digit year, the number above 12 as the day, the other number as the month, month and weekday names, and a time joined by ":". | % codes may be given directly; turn_into_dates(written_like=) reads the same way. | **P4.** Data preparation: an example is easier to write than % codes, but 03/04/2026 cannot say which number is the day, so an example whose day is 12 or less is refused rather than guessed. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A date that does not exist (31.02.2026) and a two-digit year are refused. |  |
| `table.turn_into_dates()` | 26 ways of writing a date tried in order, day first before month first (03/04/2026 is 3 April); a cell that fits none becomes empty; whole years become 1 January. | order="day month year", "month day year" or "year month day" so nothing is guessed. | **P4.** Data preparation: day first is how most of the world writes dates; month first is used only when day first cannot work, and order= removes the guess. The cells that could not be read are counted and shown. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Decimal numbers and true/false columns are refused. |  |
| `table.turn_into_dates()` `from_unix_seconds=` | Dates and times on the UTC clock, to the microsecond (Polars from_epoch on both engines; pandas to_datetime(unit="s") gives the same times). | Without from_unix_seconds=: dates read from text (order=, written_like=). | **P3.** Unix time counts seconds from 1 January 1970 in UTC, so the times are on that clock. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column of numbers; on its own, without order= or written_like=. |  |
| `table.round_numbers_in()` `significant_figures=` | Each number as it is written, a half to the even digit (5.25 becomes 5.2 at 2 figures), as everywhere in simplydata; that is R signif() in nearly every case (R works on the stored number, so 0.639075 at 5 figures is 0.63907 there). | with_decimals= for decimal places; Polars round_sig_figs would take a half away from 0 (5.3). | **P3.** Rounding halves to even stops a run of halves from always pushing totals up, as R and NumPy round; the number as written is what the reader sees. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A whole number of 1 or more; columns of numbers; not with decimal places. |  |
| `table.change_column()` `in_rows_where=` | The condition is read on the values before the change; a row it cannot check (an empty cell) keeps its value (pandas df.loc[mask, column] = value, Polars when/then/otherwise). | replace_values_in(in_rows_where=) and fill_empty_cells_in(in_rows_where=) work the same way. | **P5.** Data preparation: pandas' chained assignment can fail without a word; one written condition, the same as keep_rows_where's, avoids it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A new value of another kind than the column is refused; a note says how many rows could not be checked. |  |
