| `table.turn_into_categories()` | The values in the order of in_order= (pandas Categorical(ordered=True), Polars Enum); a value not in the list stops the word with its name; without in_order= the order in which the values first appear. | in_order= for your own order. | **P4.** Data preparation: pandas Categorical turns a value it does not know into an empty cell without a word, which hides typing mistakes ("Severe" for "severe"); Polars Enum refuses it, and simplydata does the same on both engines. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Text columns only; values of only spaces become empty cells, with a note. |  |
| `table.turn_into_title_case()` | Each word starts with a capital and its other letters become small; a new word starts after anything that is not a letter ("o'neil" becomes "O'Neil"), pandas str.title() on both engines. | There is no other title-case rule here; turn_into_lowercase() and turn_into_uppercase() for the other cases. | **P3.** Data handling: pandas str.title() and Polars str.to_titlecase() agree except for ligatures and letter pairs, so one rule on both engines gives the same text. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column of text. |  |

## Sort and new columns

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.sort_by()` | A stable sort (rows that tie keep their order), empty cells last in both directions. | biggest_first=True; several columns for ties. | **P3.** Data preparation, not a statistical method: a stable sort makes the result the same every time, and empty cells last keeps them out of the way, as R order() does by default. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.sort_by()` `biggest_first=` | A list gives each column its own direction; rows that tie keep their order. | biggest_first=True or False for every column at once. | **P3.** Data preparation: sorting by class A to Z and marks best first is a common need; pandas ascending= means the opposite, so it points to biggest_first= instead of running. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A list of the wrong length, or with anything but True and False, is refused. |  |
| `table.add_column()` `counted_in=` | Whole units counted towards zero, as for days: 1 hour 59 minutes is 1 hour (Polars total_hours(); pandas (end - start) / pd.Timedelta(hours=1), truncated). | counted_in= "days" (the default), "hours", "minutes" or "weeks". | **P5.** Data preparation: the same rule as the whole days simplydata already counts, so a unit never changes how the count is cut. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Two date columns only. |  |
| `table.add_column()` `floor() ceil() log10() log1p() sign() cbrt()` | Worked out with NumPy (floor, ceil, log10, log1p, sign, cbrt) on both engines; an answer that is not a number (the log of a negative number) gives an empty cell. | There is no other way here; round() for rounding to the nearest number. | **P3.** Data handling: NumPy's functions are the reference ones, so the numbers agree with NumPy and Polars. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Columns of numbers. |  |
| `table.add_column()` `add_working_days() count_working_days()` | add_working_days: a date on a weekend first moves to the Monday (roll="forward"), then Monday to Friday are counted, no holidays (Polars dt.add_business_days, NumPy busday_offset); count_working_days counts them from the start up to the end, the end not counted (Polars business_day_count, NumPy busday_count). | There is no holiday calendar here. | **P3.** The forward roll and the end left out are what NumPy and Polars do, so the dates agree with them. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Columns of dates; a whole number of days or a column of them. |  |
| `table.add_group_column()` | A value exactly on a cut goes into the higher group (a mark of 60 with cut_at=[40, 60, 80] is in the third group). | rules= for groups by conditions. | **P5.** Data preparation: "60 and above" is how grade bands are usually written; pandas cut() and R cut() put the value in the lower group unless told right=FALSE. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `table.add_group_column()` `into_equal_groups=` | Groups numbered 1 (the smallest values) to n, cut at the percentiles of the data (pandas qcut, Polars qcut); a value on a cut goes into the lower group; the cut points are printed. | names= for names of your own; cut_at= for cut points of your own. | **P5.** Learners count from 1, as every position word of simplydata does (pandas labels=False counts from 0). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Too many equal values for n groups is refused with a plain sentence. |  |
| `table.add_rank_column()` | Rank 1 for the biggest value; ties share the lowest rank (1, 2, 2, 4), as in sports. | biggest_first=False. | **P3.** A ranking for reading, not a statistical method; rank tests rank the data themselves (average ranks for ties). *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.add_z_score_column()` | (value - average) / standard deviation with n - 1, as R scale(); within each group when asked. | There is no other kind of standard deviation here. | **P3.** The n - 1 standard deviation is the sample standard deviation that R, pandas and the GUM use. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A group with fewer than 2 values gets empty z-scores. | [JCGM 100:2008 (GUM)](https://www.bipm.org/documents/20126/2071204/JCGM_100_2008_E.pdf) |
| `table.add_row_summary_column()` | Empty cells are left out of each row's summary (pandas skipna, Polars *_horizontal); a row with every cell empty gets an empty cell, also for the total (pandas sum(min_count=1)). | show= "average", "total", "median", "smallest", "biggest", "standard deviation" or "count". | **P4.** Data preparation: an all-empty row has no total, so 0 (Polars' own sum_horizontal) would invent a value; pandas and Polars both skip single empty cells. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Only number columns (any column for count); the sentence says how many rows had an empty cell. |  |
| `table.add_row_number_column()` | Numbers start at 1, as table.show() counts, in the order of sorted_by=, while the rows keep their place (pandas cumcount() + 1, Polars int_range(1, len + 1).over()). | for_each_group_in= to start again in each group. | **P5.** Learners count from 1, and every position word of simplydata counts from 1. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Dates and text

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.add_column_with_part_of_date()` | Weekdays and weeks follow ISO 8601: 1 is Monday ... 7 is Sunday; the week is the ISO week. | part= year, quarter, month, month name, week, day, weekday, weekday name, day of year, hour, minute. | **P5.** Data preparation: ISO 8601 is the international standard for dates; pandas counts Monday as 0. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column that is not dates, or an hour asked of dates without times, is refused. |  |
| `table.add_column_with_part_of_date()` `part="is month start"` | True on the first day of a month, worked out by Polars on both engines; an empty date gives an empty cell (pandas dt.is_month_start gives False). | part="is quarter start", "is quarter end", "is year start", "is year end", "is month end", and "month end" for the date of the month's last day (Polars dt.month_end()). | **P3.** Data handling: an empty date is not known to be a month start, so it stays empty, as every other date part does. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column of dates. |  |
| `table.add_column_with_part_of_date()` `part="is working day"` | Monday to Friday (NumPy is_busday, Polars dt.is_business_day()); no public holidays are left out. | There is no holiday calendar here: remove the holidays with keep_rows_where() afterwards. | **P3.** Public holidays differ from country to country; the weekmask of NumPy and Polars without holidays is their own default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column of dates. |  |
| `table.add_column_with_part_of_date()` `part="unix time"` | Whole seconds since 1 January 1970 on the UTC clock (Polars dt.epoch("s")). | turn_into_dates(from_unix_seconds=True) turns such seconds back into dates. | **P3.** Unix time is counted in seconds from 1970 in UTC, as computers keep it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column of dates. |  |
| `table.add_column_with_part_of_text()` | before= and after= look for the first mark; the part is trimmed of spaces, and a cell without the mark gives an empty cell. | first_characters=, last_characters=, characters=, matches_pattern=. | **P5.** Data preparation: an empty cell where the mark is missing says plainly that there was no part, and the sentence counts those cells. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.add_column_with_part_of_text()` `every_match=` | Every piece that fits the pattern, joined with ";" (pandas str.findall, Polars str.extract_all); a text with none gives an empty cell, and the sentence says how many. | separator= for another mark; without every_match= only the first piece. | **P5.** Data handling: one cell per row keeps the table's shape; split_into_rows() then gives one row for each piece. ";" is simplydata's own starting value. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Goes with matches_pattern=; no capturing brackets ( ) in the pattern. |  |
| `table.add_column_with_part_of_text()` `position_of=` | Where the piece starts, counted from 1, and an empty cell where it is not found (pandas str.find plus 1, which gives -1); characters are counted on both engines (Polars str.find counts bytes). | There is no other way to count here; capital letters matter. | **P5.** Data handling: counting from 1 matches every position in simplydata, and an empty cell cannot be mistaken for a position. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A piece of text in quotes, not empty. |  |
| `table.split_column()` | At most as many pieces as new names; the last keeps the rest; missing pieces are empty; the new columns take the old one's place. | separator=, into=. | **P5.** Data preparation: no text is lost (the rest stays in the last column), and the sentence counts the cells with too few or too many pieces. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Names already in the table are refused. |  |
| `table.replace_text_in()` | The old text is plain text (not a pattern), replaced everywhere it appears; capitals matter. | matches_pattern= for a regular expression. | **P5.** Data preparation: plain text does what a beginner expects (a full stop is a full stop); patterns are one option away. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says in how many cells the text was replaced. |  |
| `table.split_into_rows()` | The separator is plain text, not a pattern; a cell without it stays one row and an empty cell one empty row (pandas str.split + explode, Polars str.split + explode). | split_column() to split into columns instead; a column of lists needs no separator=. | **P5.** Data preparation: one value per cell is what counts, joins and charts need (tidy data); a plain-text separator cannot be misread as a pattern (";" and "\|" mean other things in patterns). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A note says when new cells start or end with a space. |  |
| `table.round_dates_in()` `to_the=` | A time exactly half way goes up (10:30 to the hour is 11:00), as Polars dt.round(); pandas is made to agree with dt.floor() of the time plus half a step. | down_to= (the start of the unit, pandas dt.floor / Polars dt.truncate) and up_to= (pandas dt.ceil). | **P5.** Data preparation: pandas dt.round() sends half way to the even unit, Polars up; one rule on both engines keeps the same table on both; a month or a year has no one length, so to_the= refuses them. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Dates without a time of day are refused for hours, minutes and seconds. |  |
| `table.change_time_zone_in()` | A clock time that never happened (summer time starts) or happened twice (it ends) becomes an empty cell, with a note (pandas tz_localize(nonexistent="NaT", ambiguous="NaT"), Polars replace_time_zone(non_existent="null", ambiguous="null")). | recorded_in= for clock times without a zone; to= for the clock to change to. | **P4.** Data preparation: the parents' default stops the whole column at one such time, and shifting it would invent a time; an empty cell keeps every other time and says what happened. Clock times without a zone are never assumed to be UTC. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Zone names are checked against the IANA list with a did-you-mean; a column that has a zone refuses recorded_in=. |  |
| `table.add_zeros_in_front_of()` | Zeros in front until the text has to_length= characters (pandas and Polars str.zfill); a longer text is never cut, a minus or plus sign stays in front, and an empty cell stays empty. | turn_into_text() first for a column of numbers; other filling characters in pandas str.pad. | **P4.** Data preparation: IDs typed as 7 in one file and 007 in another never match in a join; padding the shorter ones is the usual fix, and cutting a longer one would change an ID without a word. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column that is not text is refused with the step that makes it text; the sentence says how many values were already longer. |  |

## Row after row

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.add_rolling_column()` | A window of this row and the rows just before it, in the order of sorted_by=, within each group; a window that is not full, or has an empty cell, gives an empty cell. | window=, show=, centre=True, window="7 days". | **P3.** As pandas rolling() and R zoo::rollapply(align = "right"): a trailing window uses only what is known at each row. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.add_rolling_column()` `with_column=` | from_column= and with_column= are the same as from_column=[two columns]; the first rows of each group are empty until the window is full (pandas rolling().corr(), Polars rolling_corr). | show="covariance" works the same way (pandas rolling().cov(), Polars rolling_cov). | **P3.** Data handling: a window that is not full gives no value, as pandas rolling() does by default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Goes with show="correlation" or "covariance"; one column name each, both of numbers. |  |
| `table.add_previous_value_column()` | The row before in the order of sorted_by=, within each group; the rows keep their place; the first row of each group is empty. | rows_back=, for_each_group_in=, sorted_by=. | **P5.** Data preparation: the order of the calculation and the order of the table are kept apart, so a later sort never mixes them up. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.add_previous_value_column()` `rows_ahead=` | The value from the row after (a lead: pandas and Polars shift(-1), R dplyr lead()); the last row of each group has no row after it and stays empty. | rows_back= (the default, 1) for the value before. | **P3.** Data handling: the same shift as pandas, Polars and R, within each group and in the order of sorted_by=. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** rows_back= and rows_ahead= are not given together; a whole number of rows. |  |
| `table.add_change_column()` | This value minus the one before (in the order of sorted_by=, within each group); in_percent=True divides by the previous value, and a previous 0 gives an empty cell. | in_percent=, rows_back=, for_each_group_in=, sorted_by=. | **P5.** Data preparation: a change from 0 has no percent, so an empty cell is more honest than infinity. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.add_running_column()` | The running summary follows sorted_by= while the rows keep their place; an empty cell stays empty and is left out of the rows after it (pandas cumsum, Polars cum_sum). | show= "total", "average", "smallest", "biggest", "count" or "product"; for_each_group_in=. | **P5.** Data preparation: pandas' expanding().mean() would carry the average on through an empty cell while its cumsum leaves the cell empty; simplydata treats every show= the same way. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Number columns only (any column for count). |  |
| `table.add_run_number_column()` | The number goes up by one each time the value changes, counted from 1 within each group (Polars rle_id() plus 1); empty cells next to each other are one run, as Polars counts them; the runs follow sorted_by=, but the rows keep their place in the table. | Without for_each_group_in= the whole table is one run of numbers; count_rows(for_each_group_in=[group, name]) then gives the length of each run (Polars rle()). | **P3.** Data handling: counting from 1 matches every position in simplydata; the runs are Polars rle_id()'s and pandas (s != s.shift()).cumsum()'s. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Summaries

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.count_rows()` `after use_survey_design()` | The estimated number in the population: R survey::svytotal() of a column of ones (the sum of the weights); with in_percent=True each group's share, svymean(~factor(group)). | table.use_survey_design() off for the number of rows. | **P1.** Population counts and shares come from the weights and their design-based standard errors (Lumley 2010). **Checks:** in_percent=True needs for_each_group_in=. | [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.count_rows()` `with_totals=` | A "(total)" row and a "(total)" column (named like "(empty)"), each worked out from the rows themselves (pandas crosstab(margins=True), pivot_table(margins=True)); rows with an empty group or across= value stay in the table as "(empty)" and count in the totals; the same on find_total_of and find_average_of. | across= is needed; with_totals= does not go with every= or in_percent=True. | **P5.** Data preparation: pandas margins give the total (or average) of all the rows in a row or column, not of the cells, but leave out rows with an empty key; simplydata cross tables keep them, so the totals do too. Polars has no margins, so simplydata works each total out with group_by().agg() on both engines. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A table that already holds the value "(total)" is refused, with the replace_values_in line that renames it. |  |
| `table.count_each()` | With interval=: Sison and Glaz's simultaneous intervals for all the shares at once (as R DescTools::MultinomCI). | interval= sets the level. | **P2.** Separate intervals for each share would together cover less than 95 %; Sison & Glaz 1995 give intervals that cover all the shares together. May & Johnson 2000 found them good with many groups and small counts, and Goodman's intervals better with few groups and large counts. **Checks:** Fewer than 2 values are refused. | [Sison & Glaz 1995](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1995.10476521); [May & Johnson 2000](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v005.i06) |
| `table.count_each()` `columns=, in_order=` | For answers on a scale, the count and percent of each answer (of the answers given), the percent in the top two answers and the median answer (the inverted CDF, R quantile(type = 1)); no averages. | chart.draw_answer_bars() for the same counts as diverging bars. | **P2.** The steps between answers on a Likert scale need not be equal, so the median and the shares are the safe summaries (Jamieson 2004); averages of many items can work (Norman 2010), but simplydata leaves that choice to you and does not average single items. **Checks:** in_order= is required (at least 3 answers); answers not in it are refused and named; empty cells are left out with a note. | [Jamieson 2004](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1365-2929.2004.02012.x); [Norman 2010](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10459-010-9222-y) |
| `table.find_average_of()` | One row for each group, the groups sorted (an empty group cell is a group of its own, last); empty cells are skipped, as pandas and Polars do. interval= gives the t interval, as R t.test(). | interval= for the interval; weights= for a weighted average; every= for dates by week, month or year. | **P3.** Skipping empty cells and saying how many were skipped is what pandas, Polars and R's mean(na.rm = TRUE) do; NumPy would give nan. The t interval of the average is Student's (Student 1908), the interval of R t.test(). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A note gives how many empty cells were skipped; one value gives no interval. | [Student 1908](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/6.1.1) |
| `table.find_average_of()` `after use_survey_design()` | The population average from R survey::svymean() (svyby() for groups), its standard error from the design, and a t interval on the design's degrees of freedom (degf). | table.use_survey_design() off for the average of the rows. | **P1.** A t interval on the design's degrees of freedom, not survey's own normal-curve confint(): with few clusters or strata the normal curve gives intervals that are too narrow (Korn & Graubard 1999). survey is Lumley's reference implementation (Lumley 2004). **Checks:** Only column= and for_each_group_in= (one column) are taken; empty cells are left out as survey does. | [Lumley 2004](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v009.i08); [Korn & Graubard 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118032619) |
| `table.find_median_of()` | The middle value; interval= gives the exact interval from the order statistics (as R DescTools::MedianCI(method = "exact")). | interval= sets the level. | **P2.** The order-statistic interval of the median needs no assumption about the shape of the data (Campbell & Gardner 1988). **Checks:** With few values one end of the interval can be empty, which is said. | [Campbell & Gardner 1988](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.296.6634.1454) |
| `table.find_median_of()` `weights=` | As find_percentile_of(weights=) at 50 %: frequency weights repeat the rows. | weight_kind="analytic". | **P3.** As table.find_percentile_of weights=. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.find_percentile_of weights=. | [Hyndman & Fan 1996](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1996.10473566) |
| `table.find_median_of()` `after use_survey_design()` | The population median from R survey::svyquantile(qrule = "math") with Woodruff's interval on the design's degrees of freedom. | find_percentile_of for other percents. | **P1.** Woodruff's method turns the interval of the distribution function into an interval for the quantile, which keeps the design (Woodruff 1952; Lumley 2010). **Checks:** As find_average_of under a survey design. | [Woodruff 1952](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1952.10483443); [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.find_total_of()` `after use_survey_design()` | The estimated population total from R survey::svytotal(), with a t interval on the design's degrees of freedom. | table.use_survey_design() off for the total of the rows. | **P1.** A t interval on the design's degrees of freedom, not survey's own normal-curve confint(): with few clusters or strata the normal curve gives intervals that are too narrow (Korn & Graubard 1999). **Checks:** As find_average_of under a survey design. | [Lumley 2004](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v009.i08); [Korn & Graubard 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118032619) |
| `table.find_smallest_of()` | Empty cells skipped; one row for each group, groups sorted. | every=, across=; find_biggest_of(). | **P3.** A summary, not a test: skipping empty cells is what pandas, Polars and R min(na.rm = TRUE) do; sorted groups make the table easy to read. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column that is not numbers is refused. |  |
| `table.find_standard_deviation_of()` | The sample standard deviation, dividing by n - 1, as pandas and R sd(); interval= gives the chi-square interval (R DescTools::VarCI). | weights= with weight_kind="frequency" (default) or "analytic". | **P1.** The n - 1 form is the experimental standard deviation of the GUM and the default of R and pandas; NumPy's default divides by n. The chi-square interval follows DescTools and holds only for bell-shaped values, which the help says; Bonett 2006 gives an interval that does not need a bell shape. **Checks:** Negative weights are refused; a group with total weight of 1 or less gets an empty value. | [JCGM 100:2008 (GUM)](https://www.bipm.org/documents/20126/2071204/JCGM_100_2008_E.pdf); [Bonett 2006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.csda.2004.10.003) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` | Count, average, standard deviation (n - 1), smallest, median, biggest. When asked: skewness G1 and excess kurtosis G2 (the adjusted forms of SAS, SPSS and R e1071 type 2), the MAD scaled by 1.4826 (as R mad()), a 10 % trimmed average, a 20 % winsorised average. | show= chooses the measures (and the trimmed and winsorised shares). | **P3.** Joanes & Gill 1998 compare the skewness and kurtosis formulas in use; the 1.4826 factor makes the MAD estimate the standard deviation of bell-shaped data (Rousseeuw & Croux 1993). The 10 % trimmed share is simplydata's starting value (R's mean() trims nothing unless asked); Wilcox & Keselman 2003 recommend 20 % for both trimmed and winsorised averages. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Skewness needs 3 values and kurtosis 4; a trimmed share of 50 % or more is refused. | [Joanes & Gill 1998](https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-9884.00122); [Rousseeuw & Croux 1993](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1993.10476408); [Wilcox & Keselman 2003](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.8.3.254) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show=` | "first" and "last": the first and last filled value in the order of the rows (empty cells left out, as pandas groupby first() and last()). | sort_by() first for another order; every= for one row per period (open, high, low, close). | **P3.** Data preparation: every other measure leaves empty cells out, so first and last do too; pandas does this by default and Polars after drop_nulls(). *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="product"` | The values multiplied together in decimals (NumPy prod), so a product of whole numbers never wraps round, as pandas prod() of int64 would; empty cells left out, as pandas prod() and Polars product() skip them. | show=["geometric mean"] or ["entropy"], often what is wanted when the product gets big. | **P3.** Data handling: the same product as pandas and Polars, without the silent wrap-round of whole-number arithmetic. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column of numbers. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="entropy"` | The Shannon entropy of the values shared out to 1 (each value over the total), natural log (SciPy entropy, Polars entropy(base=e, normalize=True)). | There is no other base here; divide by log(2) in add_column for bits. | **P3.** Shannon's entropy (Shannon 1948) with the natural log is what SciPy and Polars give by default, so the numbers agree with them. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Negative values, or values that add up to 0, give an empty cell with a note. | [Shannon 1948](https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1948.tb01338.x) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="joined text"` | The texts of each group in the order of the rows, empty cells left out, "; " between them (Polars str.join on both engines, as pandas groupby().agg("; ".join)). | separator= for another mark; split_into_rows() makes one row for each piece again. | **P5.** Data handling: "; " seldom turns up inside a text, so the pieces can be split again; it is simplydata's own starting value, changed with separator=. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** show=["joined text"] goes on its own; separator= is text and goes only with it. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show={column: measures}` | One row for each group, the columns named after the column then the measure ("marks average"), as pandas named aggregation and Polars group_by().agg(). | show= a list of measures for the same measures on every column. | **P3.** Data handling: different measures for different columns in one table, with names that say both. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Leave out the column (the dict names the columns); every key is a column name. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="geometric standard deviation"` | exp of the standard deviation of the logs, dividing by n - 1 (SciPy gstd, its default ddof=1), as R exp(sd(log(x))). | show=["geometric mean"] for the middle; there is no option for n in the bottom. | **P3.** n - 1 is the sample standard deviation used everywhere in simplydata (the GUM's experimental standard deviation, R sd()); it is also SciPy's default here, so SciPy and R agree. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Numbers above 0 and at least 2 values; otherwise the cell is left empty, with the reason. | [JCGM 100:2008 (GUM)](https://www.bipm.org/documents/20126/2071204/JCGM_100_2008_E.pdf) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="cumulant k"` | The k-statistic: the unbiased estimate of the k-th cumulant (SciPy kstat); "cumulant variance k" gives the unbiased estimate of its variance (SciPy kstatvar). | show=["central moment k"] for the plain moment, divided by n. | **P3.** The k-statistics are the usual unbiased estimates of cumulants, and SciPy's kstat and kstatvar give them. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** k from 1 to 4 (1 or 2 for the variance), as SciPy allows; at least k values (2 for the variance), otherwise the cell is left empty, with the reason. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="l-moment k"` | The sample L-moment lambda_k itself (SciPy lmoment(standardize=False)); "l-skewness" and "l-kurtosis" are the ratios tau_3 = lambda_3 / lambda_2 and tau_4 = lambda_4 / lambda_2 (SciPy's standardized default). | show=["l-skewness"] or ["l-kurtosis"] for the ratios. | **P3.** These are Hosking's L-moments and L-moment ratios, which R lmom::samlmu also gives; Hosking 1990 reports lambda_1 and lambda_2 as they are and the higher ones as ratios, which is how simplydata names them. **Checks:** At least k values; the ratios need at least 3 (or 4) values that differ, otherwise the cell is left empty. | [Hosking 1990](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1990.tb01775.x) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="central moment k"` | The average of (value - average)^k, divided by n (SciPy moment, its default center=None: around the average). | show=["cumulant k"] for the unbiased k-statistic; show=["variance"] for the variance with n - 1. | **P3.** Dividing by n is the definition of the sample central moment, as SciPy moment and R moments::moment(central = TRUE) give it. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="expectile p"` | The expectile is written in percent, as "percentile 90" is: "expectile 90" is SciPy expectile(alpha=0.9); "expectile 50" is the average. | None: the percent is part of the name; show=["percentile p"] for the quantile. | **P5.** Expectiles are Newey & Powell's asymmetric least-squares counterpart of quantiles; writing them in percent, as percentiles are, keeps one way of writing for both. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** The percent must be above 0 and below 100, otherwise it is refused with an example. | [Newey & Powell 1987](https://doi.org/10.2307/1911031) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="average between a and b"` | Only the values from a to b, both limits included (SciPy tmean's default inclusive=(True, True)); "standard deviation between", "variance between" and "standard error between" the same, with n - 1 (tstd, tvar, tsem, ddof=1). | show=["trimmed average"] cuts a share from each end instead of fixed limits; limits that leave a value out are not offered. | **P3.** Both limits included is SciPy's default; n - 1 is the sample spread used everywhere in simplydata. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 1 value between the limits (2 for the spreads), otherwise the cell is left empty, with the reason. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="smallest from a"` | The smallest value at or above a: the limit is included (SciPy tmin(lowerlimit=a), its default inclusive=True); "biggest up to b" is SciPy tmax(upperlimit=b), also inclusive. | show=["smallest"] or ["biggest"] for no limit. | **P3.** SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** No value at or above (or at or below) the limit leaves the cell empty, with the reason. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="circular average"` | The unit of the angles is part of the name: "in degrees" (a full turn from 0 to 360), "in radians" (0 to 2 pi) or "in hours" (0 to 24), given to SciPy circmean as high= with low=0; circular standard deviation and circular variance (circstd, circvar) the same. | find_average_direction_of(full_turn=) for the direction with its length and test. | **P4.** The circular average is the direction of the average unit vector (Fisher 1993; Mardia & Jupp 1999), as SciPy circmean works it out; the unit is asked for rather than assumed, because degrees read as radians give a wrong answer without any warning. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Without a unit the measure is refused, naming the three units (as find_average_direction_of needs full_turn=). | [Fisher 1993](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511564345); [Mardia & Jupp 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316979) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="differential entropy"` | In nats, by SciPy differential_entropy's method="auto": the van Es estimator for 10 values or fewer, Ebrahimi's from 11 to 1000 values and Vasicek's above 1000, with SciPy's window length. | None: the method follows SciPy's rule; show=["entropy"] is the entropy of the values as counts. | **P3.** Ebrahimi, Pflughoeft & Soofi 1994 found their estimator less biased than Vasicek's spacing estimator (Vasicek 1976), which SciPy keeps for large samples, where the two agree. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 2 values that differ; when SciPy's window is too small for the values the cell is left empty, with the reason. | [Vasicek 1976](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1976.tb01566.x); [Ebrahimi et al. 1994](https://doi.org/10.1016/0167-7152(94)90046-9) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="percentile rank v"` | The inverse of "percentile p": the percent p whose percentile (R type 7, linear interpolation) is v (SciPy estimated_cdf, times 100). | show=["percent at or below v"] for the plain share of values at or below v. | **P3.** Type 7 is the percentile simplydata, R, NumPy and pandas use by default (Hyndman & Fan 1996), so the percentile rank of the percentile 90 is 90. *Basis: convention.* | [Hyndman & Fan 1996](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1996.10473566) |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="percent at or below v"` | The values at or below v count (SciPy percentileofscore(kind="weak")), so it is the plain share in percent, as R ecdf(x)(v) * 100. | show=["percentile rank v"] for the interpolated inverse of the percentile. | **P5.** "Weak" is the empirical distribution function, the usual meaning of "at or below"; SciPy's default kind="rank" averages ties and would not match the words. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="sigma-clipped average"` | Values more than 4 standard deviations from the average are cut, again and again until none is (SciPy sigmaclip's default low=4, high=4), then the average of the rest. | "sigma-clipped average 3" (or any number above 0) cuts at that many standard deviations. | **P3.** SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The number of standard deviations must be above 0. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `in_bins_of=` | Bins of the width given, from the multiple of the width at or below the smallest value to the first at or above the biggest; a bin holds its lower edge, the last one its upper edge too (as SciPy binned_statistic). Bins with no values are left out, where SciPy gives an empty number (NaN). | A list of edges (in_bins_of={"hours": [0, 2, 5, 10]}) for bins of your own; two columns or more make bins of each (binned_statistic_2d, binned_statistic_dd). | **P5.** Edges on multiples of the width are easy to read (0-2, 2-4 rather than 0.7-2.7); the edge rule is SciPy's, so the counts agree with binned_statistic. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A width above 0 or at least 2 edges, each bigger than the one before; more than 100 000 bins are refused; not with every= or separator=; a column cannot be in both in_bins_of= and for_each_group_in=. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `in_bins_of= two columns` | Each column is cut as with one column (bins of its own width from a multiple of the width); the rows go bin after bin of the first column, then of the second, the empty bins left out. | A list of edges for either column; the order of the columns in in_bins_of= sets the order of the rows. | **P5.** The same rule for each column as for one column (SciPy binned_statistic_2d); leaving out empty bins keeps the table to the bins that hold values. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As in_bins_of= with one column. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="running count"` | The counts of the bins of in_bins_of= added up in the order of the bins (SciPy cumfreq), within each column and group. | show=["share of values"] for each bin's share instead (SciPy relfreq). | **P3.** Adding up in the order of the bins is the cumulative frequency SciPy cumfreq gives, on simplydata's bins. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Needs in_bins_of= with one column; otherwise it is refused, with an example. |  |
| `table.find_summary_of()` `show="share of values"` | Each bin's count divided by all the values in the bins of that column and group (SciPy relfreq), so the shares add up to 1. | show=["running count"] for the counts added up. | **P3.** The relative frequency of SciPy relfreq, on simplydata's bins. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Needs in_bins_of= with one column; otherwise it is refused, with an example. |  |
| `table.find_percent_of_rows_where()` | The exact (Clopper-Pearson) 95 % interval, as R binom.test(); rows with an empty cell are left out of the count, with a note. | interval_by="wilson" or "agresti-coull". | **P3.** The exact interval never covers less than 95 % and is the one R binom.test() prints. Method comparisons find it wider than needed and prefer Wilson's or Agresti-Coull's interval (Agresti & Coull 1998; Brown, Cai & DasGupta 2001), which interval_by= gives. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty cells are counted and left out, with a note. | [Clopper & Pearson 1934](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/26.4.404); [Wilson 1927](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1927.10502953); [Agresti & Coull 1998](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1998.10480550); [Brown et al. 2001](https://doi.org/10.1214/ss/1009213286) |
| `table.find_percent_of_rows_where()` `after use_survey_design()` | The population percent from R survey::svyciprop(method = "logit"), its interval worked out on the log-odds scale and on the design's degrees of freedom. | table.use_survey_design() off for the exact interval of the rows. | **P2.** The logit interval stays between 0 and 100 % and keeps its coverage better than the plain Wald interval for survey proportions near 0 or 100 % (Korn & Graubard 1999; Lumley 2010). **Checks:** One condition and for_each_group_in= are taken; shares of a total go to find_total_of. | [Korn & Graubard 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118032619); [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.count_different_values_in()` | Empty cells not counted (a note says how many); capitals matter; groups sorted. | for_each_group_in=, every=. | **P3.** A count: an empty cell is not a value, as pandas nunique() and R length(unique(na.omit(x))) see it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The note gives the number of empty cells. |  |
| `table.get_different_values_in()` | The different values in the order they first turn up in the table, empty cells left out with a note (pandas Series.unique(), Polars unique(maintain_order=True)); a column of numbers gives sdata.number_list, any other a plain list. | sort_by() first for another order; count_different_values_in() for how many there are. | **P3.** Data handling: the order of first appearance is pandas unique()'s order and gives the same list every time; empty cells are left out, as count_different_values_in leaves them out (pandas unique() would keep one). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One column name; capital letters matter ("Apple" and "apple" are two values). |  |
| `table.find_percentile_of()` | Linear interpolation between the values (test_method="linear", R quantile(type = 7)), the default of R, NumPy and pandas; interval= gives the exact order-statistic interval (SciPy quantile_test, Thompson 1936). | test_method= 1 to 9 (Hyndman & Fan's types) or a NumPy method name; weights= with weight_kind=. | **P3.** Type 7 is the shared default of R, NumPy and pandas, so results match theirs. Hyndman & Fan 1996, who compared the nine definitions, recommend type 8; SPSS and Minitab use type 6. The help names both. The interval is Thompson's, splitting the chance of missing evenly between the ends; R DescTools::QuantileCI(method = "exact") picks, for percents other than 50, the nearby pair of order statistics whose coverage is closest above the level, so the ends can differ; both are exact. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** percent= outside 0 to 100 is refused; interval= works with test_method="linear" only. | [Hyndman & Fan 1996](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1996.10473566); [Thompson 1936](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177732502) |
| `table.find_percentile_of()` `weights=` | Frequency weights (weight_kind="frequency"): each row counts as often as its whole-number weight, the rows repeated and the usual linear rule used, as R quantile(rep(x, w)) and Hmisc::wtd.quantile(normwt = FALSE). | weight_kind="analytic" for sampling weights or precisions; test_method= as without weights. | **P3.** Repeating the rows keeps the one rule of the unweighted word (Hyndman & Fan 1996, type 7), so weights of 1 change nothing; NumPy's own weights= offers only the inverted CDF, a step, which gives another answer even with all weights 1. No study fixes how frequency weights enter a sample quantile; this follows the packages named. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Weights that are not whole numbers are refused (pointing to weight_kind="analytic"), as are negative weights and more than 10 million repeated rows; rows with an empty cell are skipped with a note. | [Hyndman & Fan 1996](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1996.10473566) |
| `table.find_percentile_of()` `weight_kind="analytic"` | The weighted inverted CDF (NumPy percentile(weights=, method="inverted_cdf")), the rule of R survey::svyquantile(qrule = "math"). | weight_kind="frequency" (whole-number weights, any test_method=). | **P3.** For sampling weights the population quantile is read from the weighted distribution function (Lumley 2004, the survey package; Hyndman & Fan 1996, definition 1); it is the only rule NumPy offers with weights. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** test_method= other than the inverted CDF is refused with analytic weights; NumPy older than 2.0 is refused. | [Lumley 2004](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v009.i08); [Hyndman & Fan 1996](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1996.10473566) |
| `table.find_percentile_of()` `after use_survey_design()` | As find_median_of under a survey design, at the percent given. | test_method= and interval= are refused here. | **P1.** As table.find_median_of after use_survey_design(). **Checks:** As find_average_of under a survey design. | [Woodruff 1952](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1952.10483443); [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.find_most_common_of()` | The value that turns up most often; on a tie the smallest (first in order) is given and a note names the others; empty cells skipped. | for_each_group_in= gives the count, percent and number of tied values for each group. | **P5.** A summary: one value has to be given back, so the smallest makes it repeat (as pandas mode() sorts), and the note keeps the tie in view. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column with every cell empty is refused. |  |
| `table.find_interval_of()` | A BCa bootstrap interval (bias-corrected and accelerated) from 9999 resamples, seed 1, as R boot::boot.ci(type = "bca"). | test_method="percentile bootstrap" or "reverse percentile bootstrap"; resamples=, with_seed=. | **P2.** BCa intervals are second-order accurate and correct for bias and skew, where the simple percentile interval is not (Efron 1987; DiCiccio & Efron 1996). The seed makes the answer repeat. **Checks:** Fewer than 2 values per group, a seed that is not a whole number and fewer than 99 resamples are refused; below 20 values a note says the interval is often too narrow (Hesterberg 2015); when BCa cannot be worked out the note names the percentile bootstrap. | [Efron 1987](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1987.10478410); [DiCiccio & Efron 1996](https://doi.org/10.1214/ss/1032280214); [Hesterberg 2015](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.2015.1089789) |
| `table.find_interval_of()` `test_method="bayesian"` | Jeffreys' uninformative prior, as SciPy bayes_mvs; the middle is the posterior mean, and "variance" and "standard deviation" take bayes_mvs's other two parts. The level is interval= (95 % unless given), not SciPy's 90 %. | interval= for the level; leave test_method= out for the BCa bootstrap interval. | **P3.** Oliphant 2006 works out these intervals under the uninformative prior; the average's interval is then the usual t interval. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The "average", "variance" or "standard deviation" only; at least 2 different numbers in each group. | [Oliphant 2006](https://scholarsarchive.byu.edu/facpub/278) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` | Pearson's r with its 95 % interval (Fisher's z, SciPy) and a two-sided p-value; the strength in words (below 0.2 very weak ... 0.8 and above very strong). | test_method="spearman" (Fisher-z interval, DescTools::SpearmanRho), "kendall" (tau-b, asymptotic interval), "somers", "gamma"; allowing_for= (partial correlation); p_value_by="shuffling"; interval_by="bootstrap". | **P3.** Pearson's r is the usual measure of a straight-line link and its Fisher-z interval the usual interval (Fisher 1915 for the distribution of r). Bonett & Wright 2000 propose a better standard error for Spearman's rho than the plain Fisher-z one used here. Spearman's p-value for fewer than 1290 rows without ties is R cor.test()'s default, algorithm AS 89 (Best & Roberts 1975), run in R. The strength words are a textbook scale chosen by simplydata; scales differ between fields (Schober et al. 2018), so the number is always printed with the words. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 3 complete rows and constant columns are refused; with 3 or more columns the p-values are not adjusted, and a note points to add_adjusted_p_value_column. | [Fisher 1915](https://doi.org/10.2307/2331838); [Bonett & Wright 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02294183); [Schober et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1213/ANE.0000000000002864); [Best & Roberts 1975](https://doi.org/10.2307/2347111) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `show="covariance"` | The covariance table from pandas DataFrame.cov(): divide by n - 1, each pair on the rows where both columns have a number, as R cov(use = "pairwise.complete.obs"). | show="r" (the correlation, without units). | **P3.** n - 1 gives the unbiased covariance, the default of pandas and R; pairwise rows follow the correlation table of the same word. R's own default (use = "everything") gives NA with any empty cell. No study is needed for this convention. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 2 rows for a pair is refused; test_method=, sides= and the test options are refused (a covariance has no test). |  |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `empty_cells="multiple imputation"` | The empty cells filled in 20 times by chained equations (statsmodels MICEData, predictive mean matching, as explain()), then Fisher's z of each table pooled with Rubin's rules and taken back to r by R miceadds::micombine.cor(). | empty_cells="leave out" (the default: each pair on the rows where both columns have a number). | **P2.** Correlations are not normal near -1 and 1, so they are pooled on Fisher's z scale (van Buuren 2018, section 5.2.3), with Rubin's rules (Rubin 1987). **Checks:** test_method="pearson" or "spearman" only; show=, sides=, p_value_by=, interval_by= and allowing_for= are refused with it; rows where every column is empty are left out with a note. | [van Buuren 2018](https://stefvanbuuren.name/fimd/); [Rubin 1987](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316696) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `test_method="bayesian"` | The Bayes factor for a correlation from R BayesFactor::correlationBF(), with a stretched beta prior of medium width (1/3), and the 95 % credible interval of the correlation. | prior_width="wide" (0.577), "ultrawide" (1) or a number; leave out test_method= for Pearson's test. | **P3.** A Bayes factor says how much more likely the data are under one hypothesis than under the other, so it can also speak for no difference, which a p-value cannot (Rouder et al. 2009; Wagenmakers et al. 2018). The default prior widths are BayesFactor's (the field's reference software, also used by JASP for t tests); the Bayesian reporting guidelines ask for the prior to be stated and for its influence to be checked (Kruschke 2021), so the width is printed and prior_width= changes it. The interval comes from 10,000 posterior draws with a fixed seed, so it is the same on every run. JASP's default width for correlations is 1, so its Bayes factors differ; the printout says so (Ly, Verhagen & Wagenmakers 2016). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Exactly two columns; The other options of the usual test are refused with test_method="bayesian"; prior_width= without test_method="bayesian" is refused; no p-value and no verdict words are printed. | [Ly, Verhagen & Wagenmakers 2016](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jmp.2015.06.004); [Wagenmakers et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-017-1343-3); [Kruschke 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-021-01177-7) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `show="result table"` | With two columns, one row: r, its 95 % interval (Fisher's z for Pearson, SciPy pearsonr; R DescTools for Spearman and Kendall), the p-value, which directions were tested, the rows used and the strength in words. | p_value_by="shuffling" and interval_by="bootstrap" fill the row with those (and say so); with three or more columns it is show="each pair". | **P3.** The same calls as the printed sentence, so the table and the sentence agree; Fisher's z interval is the one R cor.test and SciPy give (Fisher 1915). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Somers' d and gamma are refused with show="result table". | [Fisher 1915](https://doi.org/10.2307/2331838) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `allowing_for=, show="result table"` | With allowing_for= one row of R ppcor::pcor.test()'s numbers: the partial r, its statistic (t on n - 3 degrees of freedom for Pearson and Spearman, z for Kendall), the two-sided p-value and the rows; no interval, as ppcor gives none. | leave out show= for the number r alone; leave out allowing_for= for the plain correlation with its interval. | **P3.** The same call as the printed sentence, so the table and the sentence agree; every number is ppcor's own, nothing is worked out again. The partial correlation is the correlation of what is left of the two columns after the third is taken out (Fisher 1915 for the distribution of r). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Exactly two columns and one to allow for; sides=, p_value_by= and interval_by= are refused. | [Fisher 1915](https://doi.org/10.2307/2331838) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `test_method="chatterjee xi"` | xi of the second column given the first (it is not symmetric), with the one-sided p-value of the normal approximation; ties in the first column are broken in SciPy's order, y_continuous=False (SciPy chatterjeexi, as R XICOR::xicor()). | Swap the columns for the other direction; test_method="spearman" or "kendall" for a link that goes one way. | **P3.** Chatterjee 2021 defines xi and its p-value; y_continuous=False is the form that allows ties in the second column. Chatterjee suggests breaking ties in the first column at random; SciPy breaks them by its own order, so the answer repeats. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 3 rows with both values. | [Chatterjee 2021](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.2020.1758115) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `test_method="weighted kendall"` | SciPy weightedtau's hyperbolic weights, ranked by both columns (rank=True): the pairs near the top of the ranking count most; no p-value. | test_method="kendall" for the unweighted tau-b, with a p-value. | **P3.** Vigna 2015 defines the weighted tau and the hyperbolic weights SciPy uses by default. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 3 rows with both values. | [Vigna 2015](https://doi.org/10.1145/2736277.2741088) |
| `table.find_correlation_between()` `test_method="multiscale graph"` | 1000 shuffles for the p-value, seed 1 (printed), as SciPy multiscale_graphcorr's default reps=1000 and R mgc::mgc.test(). | with_seed= for another seed. | **P3.** Vogelstein et al. 2019 define the multiscale graph correlation and its permutation p-value; 1000 shuffles is SciPy's default, the seed simplydata's, so the answer repeats. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 5 rows with both values. | [Vogelstein et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.41690) |

## Statistical tests

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.compare_averages_of()` | Two groups: Welch's t-test, which does not assume both groups have the same spread. Three or more: Welch's one-way ANOVA (as R oneway.test()). Effect sizes: Hedges' g with the pooled standard deviation (as R effectsize::hedges_g()), or omega squared from the classic ANOVA. | test_method="student", "anova", "yuen", "brunner-munzel", "alexander-govern", "median", "jonckheere"; based_on="ranks" (Mann-Whitney, Kruskal-Wallis). | **P2.** Welch's test keeps its error rate when the spreads differ and loses almost nothing when they are equal, so it is recommended as the default, without a preliminary test of the spreads (Welch 1947; Zimmerman 2004; Ruxton 2006; Delacre et al. 2017); the same holds for Welch's F against the classic ANOVA (Welch 1951; Delacre et al. 2019). The pooled-SD Hedges' g is approximate when the spreads differ; omega squared is much less biased than eta squared (Okada 2013; Albers & Lakens 2018). **Checks:** Fewer than 2 values in a group or no spread at all is refused; one-sided tests with 3 or more groups are refused. A Shapiro-Wilk note per group suggests based_on="ranks" for small, clearly skewed groups; it never switches the test, because choosing the test from a normality pre-test distorts the error rate (Rochon, Gondan & Kieser 2012). | [Welch 1947](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/34.1-2.28); [Zimmerman 2004](https://doi.org/10.1348/000711004849222); [Ruxton 2006](https://doi.org/10.1093/beheco/ark016); [Delacre et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.5334/irsp.82); [Welch 1951](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/38.3-4.330); [Delacre et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.5334/irsp.198); [Lakens 2013](https://doi.org/10.3389/fpsyg.2013.00863); [Okada 2013](https://doi.org/10.2333/bhmk.40.129); [Albers & Lakens 2018](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jesp.2017.09.004); [Rochon et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-12-81) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `based_on=` | Mann-Whitney U for two groups (below 50 values per group an exact p-value: without ties from SciPy, with tied values the exact conditional one from R wilcox.test(), as R 4.6 gives it; from 50 values, or with ties and no R, the normal approximation with continuity correction), with the Hodges-Lehmann shift; Kruskal-Wallis for three or more. | based_on="averages" (the default, Welch). | **P3.** Rank tests need no bell shape, but they compare more than the medians, and are not robust to unequal spreads (Fagerland & Sandvik 2009; Fay & Proschan 2010): that is why they are an option, not the default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** test_method= must then be a rank test. | [Mann & Whitney 1947](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177730491); [Kruskal & Wallis 1952](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1952.10483441); [Hodges & Lehmann 1963](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177704172); [Fagerland & Sandvik 2009](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.3561); [Fay & Proschan 2010](https://doi.org/10.1214/09-SS051) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `p_value_by=` | The p-value from 9999 random shuffles of the group labels (seed 1), exact when there are fewer arrangements than that; two-sided p twice the smaller side, as R perm::permTS(). | shuffles=, with_seed=. | **P5.** A permutation test needs only that the group labels could be swapped under the null hypothesis (Ernst 2004). 9999 shuffles and seed 1 are simplydata's starting values; the seed makes the answer repeat. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Two groups and the Welch or Student test only; fewer than 99 shuffles are refused. | [Ernst 2004](https://doi.org/10.1214/088342304000000396) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `same_within=` | Two one-sided tests (TOST) with Welch's t, the larger p-value counting, with the 90 % interval (as R TOSTER::t_TOST()). | not_lower= or not_higher= for non-inferiority. | **P2.** 'No evidence of a difference' is not evidence of no difference; the TOST procedure tests equivalence directly (Schuirmann 1987; Lakens 2017), and Welch's form keeps it valid when the spreads differ (statsmodels' own default pools them). **Checks:** The margin must lie on both sides of 0; two groups only. | [Schuirmann 1987](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01068419); [Lakens 2017](https://doi.org/10.1177/1948550617697177) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `matched_by=` | Repeated-measures ANOVA with the Greenhouse-Geisser correction and Mauchly's test (R afex::aov_ez(), whose default this is). | table.explain(grouped_by=) for a mixed model; compare_matched_columns() for a rank test on columns. | **P3.** Repeated measurements on the same subject are not independent, and a sphericity correction keeps the error rate (Greenhouse & Geisser 1959). The Greenhouse-Geisser correction is conservative when sphericity is nearly met; Haverkamp & Beauducel 2017 found the Huynh-Feldt correction (Huynh & Feldt 1976) better, which simplydata does not yet offer. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Refused with based_on=, test_method=, sides=, p_value_by=, same_within=. | [Greenhouse & Geisser 1959](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02289823); [Huynh & Feldt 1976](https://doi.org/10.3102/10769986001001069); [Haverkamp & Beauducel 2017](https://doi.org/10.3389/fpsyg.2017.01841) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `grouped_by=` | Nested ANOVA: the treatment is tested against the variation between the units (Error(rat)), not between readings. | table.explain(grouped_by=) for a mixed model. | **P2.** Readings of one unit are not independent; testing them as if they were is pseudoreplication (Hurlbert 1984). **Checks:** Units that are not nested in one group, units with one row and fewer than 2 units per group are refused; an unbalanced design gets a note pointing to a mixed model. | [Hurlbert 1984](https://doi.org/10.2307/1942661) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `for_each_group_in= with a list of columns` | MANOVA with Pillai's trace first, as R summary(manova()). | The other three statistics stay in the table. | **P2.** Pillai's trace is the most robust of the four MANOVA statistics when groups differ in size or spread (Olson 1974, 1976). **Checks:** Groups with no more rows than columns, and rank deficiency, are refused. | [Olson 1974](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1974.10480224); [Olson 1976](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.83.4.579) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `period=` | The EMA guideline's 2 x 2 crossover analysis with all effects fixed: statsmodels OLS of log(column) on sequence, subject, period and formulation; the ratio of the geometric means with its 90 % interval and the within-subject CV. | same_within=[0.8, 1.25] (or 1.25) for the bioequivalence decision; against= the reference formulation. | **P1.** The 90 % interval on the log scale inside 80-125 % is the regulatory rule (EMA 2010), the same as two one-sided tests at 5 % (Schuirmann 1987). No test for carry-over comes first: the two-stage procedure biases the result (Freeman 1989). The model gives the published answers of the reference datasets (Schütz et al. 2014). **Checks:** Two formulations in two periods only; subjects without both periods are left out and named; values of 0 or less are refused (the log scale); all subjects in one order is refused. | [EMA 2010](https://www.ema.europa.eu/en/documents/scientific-guideline/guideline-investigation-bioequivalence-rev1_en.pdf); [Schuirmann 1987](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01068419); [Freeman 1989](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.4780081202); [Schütz et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1208/s12248-014-9661-0) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `after use_survey_design()` | The design-based t test of R survey::svyttest() for two groups. | table.explain(column, using=[group]) for more than two groups. | **P1.** The difference and its standard error come from the design, so the test keeps its size under clustering and weighting (Lumley 2010). **Checks:** Exactly two groups are needed. | [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `test_method="bayesian"` | The JZS Bayes factor t-test for two groups from R BayesFactor::ttestBF(), with a Cauchy prior of medium width (r = 0.707) on the effect size, and the 95 % credible interval of the difference from BayesFactor::posterior(). | prior_width="wide" (1), "ultrawide" (1.414) or a number; leave out test_method= for Welch's t-test. | **P3.** A Bayes factor says how much more likely the data are under one hypothesis than under the other, so it can also speak for no difference, which a p-value cannot (Rouder et al. 2009; Wagenmakers et al. 2018). The default prior widths are BayesFactor's (the field's reference software, also used by JASP for t tests); the Bayesian reporting guidelines ask for the prior to be stated and for its influence to be checked (Kruschke 2021), so the width is printed and prior_width= changes it. The interval comes from 10,000 posterior draws with a fixed seed, so it is the same on every run. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Exactly two groups; The other options of the usual test are refused with test_method="bayesian"; prior_width= without test_method="bayesian" is refused; no p-value and no verdict words are printed. | [Rouder et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.3758/PBR.16.2.225); [Wagenmakers et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-017-1343-3); [Kruschke 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-021-01177-7) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `test_method="wilcoxon rank sum"` | The rank sum of the first group against the normal curve, with no continuity correction and no allowance for ties (SciPy ranksums), as R wilcox.test(exact = FALSE, correct = FALSE) without ties. | based_on="ranks" (Mann-Whitney U, exact for small groups, the usual choice); sides= for one side. | **P3.** Wilcoxon's rank-sum test and Mann-Whitney's U are the same test (Wilcoxon 1945; Mann & Whitney 1947); this form is SciPy ranksums as it is, for those who need its z; the sentence names the exact test. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Two groups. | [Wilcoxon 1945](https://doi.org/10.2307/3001968); [Mann & Whitney 1947](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177730491) |
| `table.compare_averages_of()` `test_method="baumgartner-weiss-schindler"` | The p-value from shuffling the group labels: every way when there are fewer than 9999, otherwise 9999 random ways with seed 1 (SciPy bws_test with PermutationMethod). | sides= for one side; based_on="ranks" for Mann-Whitney. | **P2.** Baumgartner, Weiß & Schindler 1998 give the test and a large-sample formula (R BWStest's default p-value); a permutation p-value is exact at any size (Ernst 2004). 9999 shuffles and seed 1 are simplydata's usual starting values. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Two groups. | [Baumgartner et al. 1998](https://doi.org/10.2307/2533862); [Ernst 2004](https://doi.org/10.1214/088342304000000396) |
| `table.compare_each_pair_of()` | Each pair by Welch's t-test, the p-values adjusted with Holm's method (as R pairwise.t.test(pool.sd = FALSE, p.adjust.method = "holm")); with based_on="ranks" Mann-Whitney U as R 4.6 pairwise.wilcox.test() (exact below 50 values per group, also with tied values). | test_method="tukey" (Tukey-Kramer), "games-howell", "scheffe", "lsd", "dunn", "conover"; based_on="ranks" or "percents"; against= (Dunnett); show="letters". | **P1.** Holm's method keeps the chance of any false alarm to at most 5 % and is uniformly more powerful than Bonferroni (Holm 1979; Aickin & Gensler 1996); multiplicity should be adjusted for and the method named (Bender & Lange 2001; ICH E9). Welch's test per pair keeps the error rate when spreads differ; it gives no simultaneous intervals, which Games & Howell's procedure does (test_method="games-howell"; Games & Howell 1976). Tukey-Kramer (Kramer 1956) and Dunn's test (Dunn 1964) are options. **Checks:** A pair where both groups have no spread is refused; Tukey prints a note when the largest spread is more than twice the smallest. | [Holm 1979](https://www.jstor.org/stable/4615733); [Aickin & Gensler 1996](https://doi.org/10.2105/AJPH.86.5.726); [Bender & Lange 2001](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0895-4356(00)00314-0); [ICH E9 1998](https://database.ich.org/sites/default/files/E9_Guideline.pdf); [Games & Howell 1976](https://doi.org/10.3102/10769986001002113); [Kramer 1956](https://doi.org/10.2307/3001469); [Dunn 1964](https://doi.org/10.1080/00401706.1964.10490181) |
| `table.compare_each_pair_of()` `against=` | Dunnett's test: each group against the one named, with simultaneous intervals (SciPy stats.dunnett, seeded). | Leave out against= for all pairs. | **P2.** Dunnett's procedure compares several treatments with one control and keeps the family error rate (Dunnett 1955). **Checks:** Refused with test_method=, based_on= or show="letters". | [Dunnett 1955](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1955.10501294) |
| `table.compare_each_pair_of()` `matched_by=` | Paired t-tests (or Wilcoxon signed-rank with based_on="ranks") for each pair, Holm's adjustment (as R pairwise.t.test(paired = TRUE)). | based_on="ranks"; after Friedman's test test_method="nemenyi" or "conover". | **P2.** As table.compare_each_pair_of: Holm's method (Holm 1979). **Checks:** Subjects not measured in every group are dropped (note); duplicate subject-group rows are refused. | [Holm 1979](https://www.jstor.org/stable/4615733) |
| `table.compare_each_pair_of()` `matched_by=, test_method="nemenyi"` | Nemenyi's test after Friedman's (R PMCMRplus::frdAllPairsNemenyiTest()), on the ranks within each matched row; its studentized range allows for all the pairs, so there is no further adjustment. | The default (Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Holm); test_method="conover". | **P2.** Nemenyi's test is the usual follow-up to Friedman's in method comparisons (Demšar 2006), but mean-rank tests make each pair depend on the other groups in the study (Benavoli et al. 2016), so it is offered on request, with that note, and Wilcoxon-Holm stays the default. **Checks:** Fewer than 3 groups is refused; rows not measured in every group are left out with a note. | [Demšar 2006](https://jmlr.org/papers/v7/demsar06a.html); [Benavoli et al. 2016](https://jmlr.org/papers/v17/benavoli16a.html) |
| `table.compare_each_pair_of()` `matched_by=, test_method="conover"` | Conover's test after Friedman's (R PMCMRplus::frdAllPairsConoverTest()), with Holm's adjustment. | test_method="nemenyi"; the default Wilcoxon-Holm. | **P2.** Conover's test on the ranks within blocks is more powerful than Nemenyi's (Conover & Iman 1979); Holm keeps the chance of any false alarm to at most 5 % (Holm 1979), as for Conover after Kruskal-Wallis. PMCMRplus's default is single-step and scikit-posthocs adjusts nothing, so the printout says why their numbers differ. The same mean-rank caveat applies (Benavoli et al. 2016). **Checks:** As test_method="nemenyi". | [Conover & Iman 1979](https://www.osti.gov/biblio/6057803); [Holm 1979](https://www.jstor.org/stable/4615733); [Benavoli et al. 2016](https://jmlr.org/papers/v17/benavoli16a.html) |
| `table.compare_each_pair_of()` `based_on="percents"` | Each pair by the 2 x 2 chi-square test with Yates' correction that compare_counts_of uses, Holm's adjustment, and the difference with Newcombe's interval: as R pairwise.prop.test() (whose default is the same). | test_method="fisher" (Fisher's exact test for each pair). | **P3.** Yates' correction (Yates 1934) makes the chi-square p-value closer to the exact one for small counts, at the cost of being conservative (Haviland 1990); simplydata keeps it so that each pair agrees with compare_counts_of, compare_risks and R. Holm controls the chance of any false alarm (Holm 1979); Newcombe's interval for the difference keeps its coverage (Newcombe 1998). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Expected counts below 5 get a note pointing to test_method="fisher"; a pair whose rows all have one value gets no p-value (as R) and is left out of Holm's adjustment. | [Yates 1934](https://doi.org/10.2307/2983604); [Haviland 1990](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.4780090403); [Holm 1979](https://www.jstor.org/stable/4615733); [Newcombe 1998](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19980430)17:8<873::AID-SIM779>3.0.CO;2-I) |
| `table.compare_before_and_after()` | Numbers: the paired t-test with Cohen's d_z. Yes/no: McNemar's test with continuity correction (as R mcnemar.test()). Three or more kinds: Bowker's test of symmetry. Chosen from the kind of column. | based_on="ranks" (Wilcoxon signed-rank), test_method="sign", p_value_by="exact" (exact McNemar), p_value_by="shuffling", same_within=. | **P3.** The paired t-test uses the changes within each subject. McNemar's test with continuity correction is R's default; Fagerland, Lydersen & Laake 2013 found the uncorrected asymptotic and mid-p tests better, and the exact test conservative. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 2 rows, the same column twice, or no change at all are refused; a Shapiro-Wilk note on the changes; one-sided McNemar and Bowker are refused. | [McNemar 1947](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02295996); [Bowker 1948](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1948.10483284); [Fagerland et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-13-91); [Wilcoxon 1945](https://doi.org/10.2307/3001968) |
| `table.compare_before_and_after()` `test_method="bayesian"` | The JZS Bayes factor paired t-test (R BayesFactor::ttestBF(after, before, paired = TRUE)), medium width, and the 95 % credible interval of the average change. | prior_width="wide", "ultrawide" or a number; leave out test_method= for the paired t-test. | **P3.** A Bayes factor says how much more likely the data are under one hypothesis than under the other, so it can also speak for no difference, which a p-value cannot (Rouder et al. 2009; Wagenmakers et al. 2018). The default prior widths are BayesFactor's (the field's reference software, also used by JASP for t tests); the Bayesian reporting guidelines ask for the prior to be stated and for its influence to be checked (Kruschke 2021), so the width is printed and prior_width= changes it. The interval comes from 10,000 posterior draws with a fixed seed, so it is the same on every run. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Both columns must hold numbers; The other options of the usual test are refused with test_method="bayesian"; prior_width= without test_method="bayesian" is refused; no p-value and no verdict words are printed. | [Rouder et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.3758/PBR.16.2.225); [Wagenmakers et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-017-1343-3); [Kruschke 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-021-01177-7) |
| `table.compare_average_to()` | The one-sample t-test with Cohen's d. | based_on="ranks" (Wilcoxon signed-rank); test_method="z" with standard_deviation=; same_within=. | **P3.** The one-sample t-test is the standard test of an average against a value (Student 1908). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A value that is not a finite number and fewer than 2 values are refused. | [Student 1908](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/6.1.1) |
| `table.compare_average_to()` `test_method="bayesian"` | The JZS Bayes factor one-sample t-test (R BayesFactor::ttestBF() on the values minus the number), medium width, and the 95 % credible interval of the average minus the number. | prior_width="wide", "ultrawide" or a number; leave out test_method= for the one-sample t-test. | **P3.** A Bayes factor says how much more likely the data are under one hypothesis than under the other, so it can also speak for no difference, which a p-value cannot (Rouder et al. 2009; Wagenmakers et al. 2018). The default prior widths are BayesFactor's (the field's reference software, also used by JASP for t tests); the Bayesian reporting guidelines ask for the prior to be stated and for its influence to be checked (Kruschke 2021), so the width is printed and prior_width= changes it. The interval comes from 10,000 posterior draws with a fixed seed, so it is the same on every run. BayesFactor's posterior() leaves its mu = argument out, so the number is taken away first; the Bayes factor is the same either way. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The other options of the usual test are refused with test_method="bayesian"; prior_width= without test_method="bayesian" is refused; no p-value and no verdict words are printed. | [Rouder et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.3758/PBR.16.2.225); [Wagenmakers et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.3758/s13423-017-1343-3); [Kruschke 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-021-01177-7) |
| `table.compare_counts_of()` | The chi-square test of independence, with Yates' continuity correction for 2 x 2 tables only (as R chisq.test()); Cramér's V. | test_method="fisher", "g", "barnard", "boschloo", "trend"; p_value_by="shuffling" or "exact"; show="cells". | **P3.** Barnard's and Boschloo's tests take the for_each_group_in= groups as the two samples (R Exact::exact.test() fixes the row totals). simplydata matches R chisq.test() so that results agree with R. The evidence on small tables favours other choices: Campbell 2007 recommends the 'N - 1' chi-square without Yates' correction (and Fisher's test when an expected count is below 1), and Yates' correction is conservative (Haviland 1990). Cochran 1954's rule is no expected count below 1 and at most 20 % below 5; simplydata's note is stricter. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A row or column of zeros is refused; when an expected count is below 5, a note suggests test_method="fisher" or p_value_by="shuffling" (never a silent switch). | [Cochran 1954](https://doi.org/10.2307/3001616); [Campbell 2007](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2832); [Haviland 1990](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.4780090403) |
| `table.compare_counts_of()` `after use_survey_design()` | Pearson's chi-square with the Rao-Scott correction, as an F test (R survey::svychisq(), statistic = "F"). | table.use_survey_design() off for the chi-square test of the rows. | **P1.** The plain chi-square test is too liberal on survey data; the Rao-Scott second-order correction keeps it close to its nominal size (Rao & Scott 1984). **Checks:** for_each_group_in= is required. | [Rao & Scott 1984](https://doi.org/10.1214/aos/1176346391) |
| `table.compare_counts_of()` `test_method="bayesian"` | The Gunel-Dickey Bayes factor for a table of counts from R BayesFactor::contingencyTableBF(), with independent multinomial sampling and each group's total fixed (the groups are the rows), prior concentration 1, and each group's shares with 95 % credible intervals. | leave out test_method= for the chi-square test. | **P3.** A Bayes factor for a table depends on how the data were collected; with groups chosen first (such as wards or exposure groups) each group's total is fixed, which is independent multinomial sampling (Gunel & Dickey 1974; Jamil et al. 2017). The printout names the sampling plan. Concentration 1 is BayesFactor's default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The other options of the usual test are refused with test_method="bayesian"; prior_width= without test_method="bayesian" is refused; no p-value and no verdict words are printed. | [Gunel & Dickey 1974](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/61.3.545); [Jamil et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.3758/s13428-016-0739-8); [Kruschke 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-021-01177-7) |
| `table.compare_shape_to()` | Against the normal distribution: the Shapiro-Wilk test (as R shapiro.test()). Against another named shape: the Anderson-Darling test with a parametric-bootstrap p-value (9999 samples, seed 1). | test_method="lilliefors", "dagostino", "jarque-bera"; a distribution object (Kolmogorov-Smirnov or Cramér-von Mises). | **P2.** Shapiro-Wilk is among the most powerful normality tests against most alternatives (Shapiro & Wilk 1965; Yap & Sim 2011). With estimated parameters the Anderson-Darling table p-values do not hold (Stephens 1974), so the p-value comes from a parametric bootstrap (Stute, González Manteiga & Presedo Quindimil 1993). **Checks:** Each test's smallest size is checked; Shapiro-Wilk above 5000 values is refused; with 30 or more values a note says the test flags even harmless differences. | [Shapiro & Wilk 1965](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/52.3-4.591); [Yap & Sim 2011](https://doi.org/10.1080/00949655.2010.520163); [Stephens 1974](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1974.10480196); [Stute et al. 1993](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02613687) |
| `table.compare_shape_to()` `shape="even around the circle"` | The Rayleigh test (R circular::rayleigh.test), with the units always given; the default test when full_turn= is given. | test_method="rayleigh" says the same; on a line, the bell-shape tests of compare_shape_to. | **P1.** The Rayleigh test is the standard test of uniformity against one preferred direction, and the most powerful against a von Mises alternative (Mardia & Jupp 1999, 6.3; Fisher 1993, 4.3). It cannot see directions gathered at two opposite sides, so the printout says so when it finds no preferred direction. **Checks:** Fewer than 5 directions refused; full_turn= required; a fixed distribution with full_turn= refused. | [Mardia & Jupp 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316979); [Fisher 1993](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511564345) |
| `table.compare_shape_to()` `test_method="skewness"` | D'Agostino's test of the skewness alone (SciPy skewtest, as R moments::agostino.test()). | Leave test_method= out for Shapiro-Wilk; "kurtosis", "dagostino" (skewness and kurtosis together), "jarque-bera". | **P3.** D'Agostino 1970 gives the transformation that makes the sample skewness close to normal from 8 values, which SciPy and R use. **Checks:** At least 8 values. | [D'Agostino 1970](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/57.3.679) |
