| `table.compare_shape_to()` `test_method="kurtosis"` | The Anscombe-Glynn test of the kurtosis alone (SciPy kurtosistest, as R moments::anscombe.test()); SciPy's warning below 20 values is left out, as R gives the same z without it. | Leave test_method= out for Shapiro-Wilk; "skewness", "dagostino", "jarque-bera". | **P3.** Anscombe & Glynn 1983 give the transformation of the sample kurtosis that SciPy and R use. **Checks:** SciPy's smallest size (5 values). | [Anscombe & Glynn 1983](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/70.1.227) |
| `table.add_adjusted_p_value_column()` | Holm's method (as R p.adjust(method = "holm")). | test_method="bonferroni", "benjamini-hochberg", "benjamini-yekutieli", "hochberg", "hommel"; "storey" (q-values) and "local fdr" (R qvalue). | **P2.** Holm's method controls the chance of any false alarm, like Bonferroni, but is uniformly more powerful (Holm 1979; Aickin & Gensler 1996); it is R's default. Benjamini-Hochberg controls the share of false alarms instead, for screening many tests (Benjamini & Hochberg 1995). **Checks:** p-values outside 0 to 1 are refused; empty p-values stay empty and are not counted. | [Holm 1979](https://www.jstor.org/stable/4615733); [Aickin & Gensler 1996](https://doi.org/10.2105/AJPH.86.5.726); [Benjamini & Hochberg 1995](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1995.tb02031.x); [Bender & Lange 2001](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0895-4356(00)00314-0) |
| `table.add_adjusted_p_value_column()` `test_method="storey"` | Storey's q-values from R qvalue::qvalue() with its defaults: the share of true nulls (pi0) estimated from the p-values by the smoother over lambda = 0.05 to 0.95. | test_method="benjamini-hochberg" (pi0 = 1); "local fdr". | **P2.** Estimating pi0 makes the false discovery rate less conservative than Benjamini-Hochberg when many tests are real (Storey 2002; Storey & Tibshirani 2003); qvalue is the authors' own package. The gain needs many p-values: with few, pi0 is unstable. **Checks:** Fewer than 100 p-values get a note; when qvalue cannot estimate pi0 the word refuses and names Benjamini-Hochberg; p-values outside 0 to 1 are refused and empty ones skipped. | [Storey 2002](https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-9868.00346); [Storey & Tibshirani 2003](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1530509100); [Benjamini & Hochberg 1995](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1995.tb02031.x) |
| `table.add_adjusted_p_value_column()` `test_method="local fdr"` | Each test's local false discovery rate from R qvalue::qvalue()$lfdr (pi0 from the smoother), in a column called "local false discovery rate". | test_method="storey" (q-values: about all the results at least this small). | **P2.** The local false discovery rate is the chance that one test is a false discovery (Efron et al. 2001); qvalue gives it with the same pi0 as the q-values (Storey & Tibshirani 2003). **Checks:** As test_method="storey". | [Efron et al. 2001](https://doi.org/10.1198/016214501753382129); [Storey & Tibshirani 2003](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1530509100) |
| `table.compare_spreads_of()` | Levene's test centred on the median (the Brown-Forsythe form), as R car::leveneTest(). | test_method="bartlett", "fligner"; for two groups "ansari", "mood", "f". | **P2.** In Conover, Johnson & Johnson's comparison of 56 tests, the median-centred Levene test was among the few that kept their error rate without a bell shape (Brown & Forsythe 1974; Conover et al. 1981); Bartlett's test does not. **Checks:** No spread in every group is refused; Bartlett gets a note when a group is not bell-shaped. | [Brown & Forsythe 1974](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1974.10482955); [Conover et al. 1981](https://doi.org/10.1080/00401706.1981.10487680) |
| `table.compare_spreads_of()` `test_method="o'brien"` | The one-way ANOVA of O'Brien's scores, whose group averages are the group variances (SciPy obrientransform, then f_oneway). | Leave test_method= out for Levene's test centred on the median; "bartlett", "fligner". | **P3.** O'Brien 1981 turns each value into a score whose group average is the group's variance, so the ordinary ANOVA tests the spreads. **Checks:** At least 3 values in each group. | [O'Brien 1981](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.89.3.570) |
| `table.compare_counts_to()` | The chi-square goodness-of-fit test, against equal shares unless expected= gives them; Cohen's w. | expected= the shares you expect. | **P3.** The goodness-of-fit chi-square is the standard test of counts against expected shares. Cochran 1954 asks that no expected count be below 1 and at most 20 % below 5; simplydata's note, at any expected count below 5, is stricter. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Shares at or below 0 and totals other than 1 or 100 are refused; expected counts below 5 get a note (compare_percent_to for two values, or merge small groups). | [Cochran 1954](https://doi.org/10.2307/3001616) |
| `table.compare_counts_to()` `test_method="g"` | The G-test (SciPy power_divergence(lambda_="log-likelihood")), as R DescTools::GTest(); "freeman-tukey", "neyman", "modified g" and "cressie-read" (lambda 2/3) are the other lambda_ values. | Leave test_method= out for Pearson's chi-square (the default). | **P3.** All of them belong to Cressie & Read's power-divergence family and share the chi-square limit; Cressie & Read 1984 recommend lambda 2/3 as a compromise between Pearson's chi-square and G. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As compare_counts_to: expected counts below 5 get a note. | [Cressie & Read 1984](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1984.tb01318.x) |
| `table.compare_percent_to()` | The exact binomial test (as R binom.test()), with the exact (Clopper-Pearson) interval and Cohen's h. | interval_by="wilson" or "agresti-coull". | **P3.** The exact test is valid at any size. The exact interval is conservative; Agresti & Coull 1998 and Brown, Cai & DasGupta 2001 prefer Wilson's or Agresti-Coull's, which interval_by= gives. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** percent= must lie between 0 and 100; below 1 a note asks whether you meant a share. | [Clopper & Pearson 1934](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/26.4.404); [Agresti & Coull 1998](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1998.10480550); [Brown et al. 2001](https://doi.org/10.1214/ss/1009213286) |
| `table.compare_matched_columns()` | Friedman's test with Kendall's W; for yes/no columns Cochran's Q; two columns go to compare_before_and_after. | test_method="quade" or "page"; compare_averages_of(matched_by=) for repeated-measures ANOVA on averages. | **P3.** Friedman's rank test compares several measurements on the same subjects without a bell shape (Friedman 1937); Cochran's Q is its form for yes/no answers (Cochran 1950). For a comparison of averages in long format, compare_averages_of(matched_by=) gives repeated-measures ANOVA. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Text with more than 2 values and fewer than 2 rows are refused. | [Friedman 1937](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1937.10503522); [Cochran 1950](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/37.3-4.256) |
| `table.compare_shapes_of()` | Two groups: the two-sample Kolmogorov-Smirnov test (exact below 10000 pairs, as R ks.test()). Three or more: the k-sample Anderson-Darling test with a permutation p-value (9999, seed 1). | shuffles=, with_seed=. | **P3.** Kolmogorov-Smirnov is the standard test of two whole distributions and matches R ks.test() (Massey 1951). The k-sample Anderson-Darling test (Scholz & Stephens 1987) also works for two groups and is usually more powerful, especially in the tails; simplydata keeps Kolmogorov-Smirnov for two groups to match R. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 99 shuffles are refused. | [Massey 1951](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1951.10500769); [Scholz & Stephens 1987](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1987.10478517) |
| `table.compare_shapes_of()` `two groups` | Two groups: the two-sample Kolmogorov-Smirnov test, exact while the groups make fewer than 10000 pairs of values; with tied values the exact p-value comes from R ks.test(), which allows for the ties. | Three or more groups get the k-sample Anderson-Darling test; the groups can be cut down to two with keep_rows_where(). | **P3.** Kolmogorov-Smirnov is the standard test of two whole distributions (Massey 1951). SciPy's exact p-value assumes no ties, so with ties the p-value of R, which counts them, is used: the field's standard software decides. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The printout says when the p-value came from R because of tied values. | [Massey 1951](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1951.10500769) |
| `table.compare_shapes_of()` `test_method="cramer-von mises"` | SciPy cramervonmises_2samp's method="auto": the exact p-value when both groups have 20 values or fewer, Anderson's approximation above. | Leave test_method= out for Kolmogorov-Smirnov; "anderson-darling" or "epps-singleton". | **P3.** The exact distribution is used where it can be worked out quickly, and Anderson 1962's limiting distribution otherwise. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Two groups. | [Anderson 1962](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177704477) |
| `table.compare_shapes_of()` `test_method="epps-singleton"` | SciPy epps_singleton_2samp's two points t = (0.4, 0.8) and its small-sample correction (used when a group has fewer than 25 values). | Leave test_method= out for Kolmogorov-Smirnov; "cramer-von mises" or "anderson-darling". | **P1.** Epps & Singleton 1986 recommend these two points and give the correction; the test works on characteristic functions, so it also suits values with many ties. **Checks:** Two groups of at least 5 values each. | [Epps & Singleton 1986](https://doi.org/10.1080/00949658608810963) |
| `table.check_if_random_order()` | The runs test about the median, without continuity correction (as R randtests::runs.test()); values equal to the median are dropped and counted. | sorted_by= for the order. | **P3.** The runs test asks whether values above and below the middle alternate as often as chance would make them (Wald & Wolfowitz 1940; Swed & Eisenhart 1943 for the one-sample form). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 3 values, or no values on one side of the median, are refused. | [Wald & Wolfowitz 1940](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177731909); [Swed & Eisenhart 1943](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177731494) |
| `table.compare_correlations()` | Two groups: Fisher's z test with Zou's interval of the difference. Two correlations sharing a column in one sample: Steiger's z (R cocor). | There is no other test here. | **P2.** Steiger 1980 gives the tests for correlations from the same sample; Zou 2007 gives intervals for the difference; cocor implements both (Diedenhofen & Musch 2015). **Checks:** Exactly 2 groups, at least 4 rows per group, and pairs that share exactly one column; otherwise refused. | [Steiger 1980](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.87.2.245); [Zou 2007](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.12.4.399); [Diedenhofen & Musch 2015](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0121945) |
| `sdata.compare_averages()` | Welch's t-test (two groups) or Welch's ANOVA (three or more), from averages, standard deviations and counts. | test_method="student" or "anova". | **P2.** As table.compare_averages_of: Welch's tests stay valid when the spreads differ (Delacre et al. 2017, 2019). **Checks:** Counts must be whole numbers of at least 2 and standard deviations above 0. | [Welch 1947](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/34.1-2.28); [Delacre et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.5334/irsp.82); [Delacre et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.5334/irsp.198) |
| `sdata.compare_counts()` | The same test as table.compare_counts_of, from a table of counts. | As table.compare_counts_of. | **P3.** As table.compare_counts_of. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.compare_counts_of. | [Cochran 1954](https://doi.org/10.2307/3001616); [Campbell 2007](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2832) |

## Risks and rates

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.compare_risks()` | Against the first group in sorted order: the risk in each group with its exact interval, the risk difference with Newcombe's score interval, the risk ratio and odds ratio with Wald intervals on the log scale, and the chi-square p-value with Yates' correction (as R prop.test()); the number needed to treat. | against=; p_value_by="exact" (Fisher, with the conditional odds ratio); allowing_for= (Mantel-Haenszel); same_within=, not_lower=, not_higher=. | **P2.** Absolute risks and the risk difference are printed with the ratios, as reporting guidance asks (Lang & Altman 2015). Newcombe's score interval for a difference of proportions does far better than the Wald interval (Newcombe 1998); the log-scale intervals of the ratios are the standard ones (Katz et al. 1978; Woolf 1955); the NNT interval follows Altman 1998. The Yates-corrected p-value matches R prop.test(); Campbell 2007 prefers the uncorrected 'N - 1' chi-square. **Checks:** No 0.5 is added to empty cells (a note says so); when more than 10 % have the outcome, a note says the odds ratio is not a risk ratio (the 10 % rule of Zhang & Yu 1998; the gap grows with the baseline risk and the size of the effect, Davies et al. 1998); a group with no events gives an empty p-value, as prop.test(). | [Newcombe 1998](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19980430)17:8<873::AID-SIM779>3.0.CO;2-I); [Katz et al. 1978](https://doi.org/10.2307/2530610); [Woolf 1955](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1469-1809.1955.tb01348.x); [Altman 1998](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.317.7168.1309); [Lang & Altman 2015](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijnurstu.2014.09.006); [Campbell 2007](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2832); [Zhang & Yu 1998](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.280.19.1690); [Davies et al. 1998](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.316.7136.989) |
| `table.compare_risks()` `allowing_for=` | The Mantel-Haenszel odds ratio and risk ratio over the strata, the Mantel-Haenszel test with continuity correction (as R mantelhaen.test()), and the Breslow-Day test that the odds ratios agree (without Tarone's correction). | Leave out allowing_for= for the crude comparison. | **P3.** Mantel-Haenszel pooling is the classic way to compare two groups within strata (Mantel & Haenszel 1959; Greenland & Robins 1985 for the risk ratio). Tarone 1985 showed that the Breslow-Day statistic needs a correction to follow the chi-square distribution; simplydata does not apply it yet. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Two groups and two-sided only; strata with one group are left out (with a note); fewer than 2 usable strata are refused; when Breslow-Day's p is below 0.05 a note asks for each stratum's odds ratio. | [Mantel & Haenszel 1959](https://doi.org/10.1093/jnci/22.4.719); [Greenland & Robins 1985](https://doi.org/10.2307/2530643); [Tarone 1985](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/72.1.91) |
| `table.compare_risks()` `same_within=` | Two one-sided tests of the risk difference against the margin, with the 90 % Wald interval (as R TOSTER::twoprop_test()); not_lower= or not_higher= give one-sided Wald tests. | not_lower=, not_higher=. | **P3.** Equivalence is tested with two one-sided tests (Schuirmann 1987; Lakens 2017). The Wald interval matches TOSTER; it is too narrow for small samples or risks near 0 or 100 % (Newcombe 1998), where score intervals do better. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Two groups only; a margin between 0 and 1 gets a note asking whether percentage points were meant. | [Schuirmann 1987](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01068419); [Lakens 2017](https://doi.org/10.1177/1948550617697177); [Newcombe 1998](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19980430)17:8<873::AID-SIM779>3.0.CO;2-I) |
| `table.compare_risks()` `show="attributable fractions"` | The attributable fraction among the exposed and in the whole population from R epiR::epi.2by2(method = "cohort.count"), with the intervals epiR gives for one table (among the exposed, from the score interval of the risk ratio; in the population, from the exact intervals of the two risks); for an exposure that goes with fewer events, the preventable fraction among the exposed (epiR's fraction with the roles of the groups swapped). | compare_risks() without show= (the risk difference, the risk and odds ratios, the number needed to treat). | **P5.** These are the standard definitions (Rockhill et al. 1998). The fractions here are crude: adjusting the population fraction for confounders needs a model, and a fraction from a crude risk ratio is biased when confounding is present (Rockhill et al. 1998), so the printout says "crude". *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Two groups only; the printout says that the risks must come from a cohort or cross-sectional study and that the fractions are causal only if the exposure causes the events. | [Rockhill et al. 1998](https://doi.org/10.2105/AJPH.88.1.15) |
| `table.compare_rates()` | The rate ratio against the first group in sorted order, with the exact conditional test and the exact interval of R poisson.test(); rates per 1000 (a starting value). | against=, per=; same_within=, not_lower=, not_higher=. | **P3.** Conditioning on the total number of events gives an exact test of two Poisson rates (Przyborowski & Wilenski 1940), R's poisson.test(); exact tests are conservative, and mid-p versions are an alternative (Fay 2010). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Without R the interval is left out, with a note; no events in either group gives an empty result, with a note. | [Przyborowski & Wilenski 1940](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/31.3-4.313); [Garwood 1936](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/28.3-4.437); [Fay 2010](https://doi.org/10.32614/RJ-2010-008) |
| `table.compare_rates()` `standard_rates=` | Indirect standardisation with R epiR::epi.indirectadj(): the expected events from the standard rates (per per= units of time), observed / expected (SMR) with its exact Poisson interval, and the p-value of R poisson.test(observed, expected). | rate_of(..., standard_population=) for directly standardised rates. | **P2.** The exact Poisson interval of the observed count, divided by the expected, is the standard interval of an SMR (Ulm 1990). Indirect standardisation needs only the group's total events, so it works where some bands of a group are small. **Checks:** Bands without a standard rate are refused and named; groups without person-time are refused; the printout says that two groups' ratios are each against the standard, not against each other. | [Ulm 1990](https://doi.org/10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a115507) |
| `table.compare_rates()` `test_method="e-test"` | The other group against against= (the first group in sorted order unless against= says), with diff=0: equal rates (SciPy poisson_means_test). | sides= for one side; leave test_method= out for the exact conditional test of R poisson.test(). | **P2.** Krishnamoorthy & Thomson 2004 found the E-test more powerful than the conditional test while keeping its error rate; diff=0 is SciPy's default and the usual question. **Checks:** Two groups, each with some time; not with same_within=, not_lower=, not_higher=, allowing_for= or standard_rates=. | [Krishnamoorthy & Thomson 2004](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0378-3758(02)00408-1) |
| `table.rate_of()` | Events per 1000 units of time, with the exact Poisson interval (as R poisson.test()). | per= another unit; interval= another level. | **P3.** The exact (Garwood) interval is valid even with few events (Garwood 1936). Per 1000 is simplydata's starting value for the unit. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Events must be whole numbers of at least 0 and time at least 0. | [Garwood 1936](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/28.3-4.437) |
| `table.rate_of()` `standard_population=` | Direct standardisation with R epiR::epi.directadj(): the rate each group would have with the standard population's make-up, with Fay & Feuer's gamma interval (the same numbers as epitools::ageadjust.direct()). | standard_population="whole table" (the pooled person-time) or a table of population sizes; compare_rates(..., standard_rates=) for indirect standardisation. | **P2.** Fay & Feuer's interval keeps its coverage when a few bands hold most of the weight, where the normal interval fails (Fay & Feuer 1997). **Checks:** Bands missing from the standard or the table are refused and named; a group with no person-time in a band is refused (pointing to indirect standardisation). | [Fay & Feuer 1997](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19970415)16:7<791::AID-SIM500>3.0.CO;2-#) |
| `sdata.compare_risks()` | The same measures as table.compare_risks, from counts, against the first group given (the order of names=, as R prop.test() takes the counts in the order they are given). | As table.compare_risks. | **P2.** As table.compare_risks. **Checks:** As table.compare_risks. | [Newcombe 1998](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19980430)17:8<873::AID-SIM779>3.0.CO;2-I); [Katz et al. 1978](https://doi.org/10.2307/2530610); [Altman 1998](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.317.7168.1309) |
| `sdata.compare_rates()` | The same test as table.compare_rates, from counts and times, against the first group given (the order of names=, as R poisson.test() takes the counts in the order they are given). | As table.compare_rates. | **P3.** As table.compare_rates (Przyborowski & Wilenski 1940). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.compare_rates. | [Przyborowski & Wilenski 1940](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/31.3-4.313) |
| `table.compare_balanced_groups_of()` | Weighting by the inverse of the propensity score (R WeightIt, method = "glm"), for everyone (ATE); the effect from WeightIt::lm_weightit() with M-estimation standard errors that allow for the estimated weights; a balance table of standardised differences before and after (R cobalt, every column, the guide 0.1). | test_method="matching"; effect_for="<a group>" for the effect among that group (ATT); show="balance" or "propensity scores". | **P2.** The propensity score balances the measured columns between the groups (Rosenbaum & Rubin 1983). Weighting keeps every row and gives the average effect for everyone; standard errors that ignore the estimated weights are wrong, which M-estimation corrects (Lunceford & Davidian 2004). Balance is judged by standardised differences, not by p-values, which depend on the number of rows (Austin 2009; Austin & Stuart 2015). **Checks:** Two groups only; the overlap of the scores is printed with the rows outside it; columns still more than 0.1 apart after balancing get a note; weights above 20 get a note; the assumptions (no unmeasured column affecting both, overlap, a right propensity model) are printed every time. | [Rosenbaum & Rubin 1983](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/70.1.41); [Lunceford & Davidian 2004](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1903); [Austin 2009](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.3697); [Austin & Stuart 2015](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.6607) |
| `table.compare_balanced_groups_of()` `test_method="matching"` | 1:1 nearest-neighbour matching on the propensity score without replacement (R MatchIt's default), the effect among the rows of the effect_for= group (ATT) from lm() on the matched rows with standard errors clustered on the pairs (sandwich::vcovCL), as the MatchIt vignette does. | match_within=0.2 (a caliper of 0.2 standard deviations of the logit of the score); test_method="weighting" for the effect for everyone. | **P3.** Nearest-neighbour matching is the most used and best studied matching method (Stuart 2010; Ho et al. 2011). A caliper of 0.2 standard deviations of the logit of the score removes most of the bias in simulations (Austin 2011); it is offered, not the default, because it changes the group the effect is about (unmatched rows leave). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** effect_for= must name a group (matching gives the effect among one group; asked otherwise, it says to use weighting); rows left without a match are counted and named in a note. | [Stuart 2010](https://doi.org/10.1214/09-STS313); [Ho et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v042.i08); [Austin 2011](https://doi.org/10.1002/pst.433) |

## Diagnostic tests

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.compare_to_truth()` | A 2 x 2 table: sensitivity, specificity and the predictive values with exact (Clopper-Pearson) intervals, likelihood ratios (R epiR), and the same results in natural frequencies ('of 1000 people'). A score column goes to an ROC curve (AUC with DeLong's interval, the best cut-off by Youden's index). | cut_off=, positive_when="lower", prevalence=. | **P1.** STARD asks for these measures with their intervals (Bossuyt et al. 2015); natural frequencies make them easier to read (Gigerenzer & Hoffrage 1995). The exact interval matches epiR; method comparisons prefer Wilson's interval for single proportions (Brown, Cai & DasGupta 2001). A cut-off chosen on the same data makes the test look better than it is (Ewald 2006; Leeflang et al. 2008). **Checks:** A truth column with no positives or no negatives is refused; a cut-off chosen on these data gets a note that it will look worse on new data; an AUC below 0.5 suggests positive_when=. | [Bossuyt et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.h5527); [Gigerenzer & Hoffrage 1995](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-295X.102.4.684); [Clopper & Pearson 1934](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/26.4.404); [Brown et al. 2001](https://doi.org/10.1214/ss/1009213286); [DeLong et al. 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2531595); [Youden 1950](https://doi.org/10.1002/1097-0142(1950)3:1<32::AID-CNCR2820030106>3.0.CO;2-3); [Ewald 2006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclinepi.2005.11.025); [Leeflang et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1373/clinchem.2007.096032) |
| `table.compare_to_truth()` `a column of numbers` | Higher scores count as positive, said before the ROC curve is made (pROC direction = "<"); the AUC with DeLong's interval; the best cut-off by Youden's index. | positive_when="lower"; cut_off= for a chosen cut-off. | **P5.** DeLong's interval needs no assumption about the shape of the scores (DeLong et al. 1988). Fixing the direction first keeps a score that works the wrong way round from looking good, as pROC's own guess would make it; Youden's index is the usual summary of a cut-off (Youden 1950). **Checks:** An AUC below 0.5 gets a note that suggests positive_when=. | [DeLong et al. 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2531595); [Youden 1950](https://doi.org/10.1002/1097-0142(1950)3:1<32::AID-CNCR2820030106>3.0.CO;2-3) |
| `table.compare_accuracy()` | DeLong's test of two AUCs on the same people (pROC::roc.test(method = "delong", paired = TRUE)). | There is no other test here. | **P2.** DeLong's method compares correlated ROC curves without assuming a shape (DeLong, DeLong & Clarke-Pearson 1988). **Checks:** Exactly two number columns. | [DeLong et al. 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2531595) |
| `sdata.compare_to_truth()` | The same measures as table.compare_to_truth, from counts; from sensitivity, specificity and prevalence alone, the arithmetic only, without intervals. | As table.compare_to_truth. | **P1.** As table.compare_to_truth. **Checks:** Prevalence must lie between 0 and 100. | [Bossuyt et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.h5527); [Gigerenzer & Hoffrage 1995](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-295X.102.4.684) |
| `chart.draw_trade_off_curve()` | Higher scores count as positive; the AUC with DeLong's 95 % interval in the legend; the best cut-off by Youden's index marked with a dot. | positive_when="lower"; several scores, or colour= groups. | **P2.** DeLong's interval needs no assumption about the score's distribution (DeLong et al. 1988). Youden's index is the usual summary of a cut-off (Youden 1950), though other criteria choose other points (Perkins & Schisterman 2006), and a cut-off chosen on the same data is optimistic (Leeflang et al. 2008). The direction is fixed, not pROC's 'auto', which can hide a test that works the wrong way round. **Checks:** Needs R pROC; the score must hold numbers. | [DeLong et al. 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2531595); [Youden 1950](https://doi.org/10.1002/1097-0142(1950)3:1<32::AID-CNCR2820030106>3.0.CO;2-3); [Perkins & Schisterman 2006](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwj063); [Leeflang et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1373/clinchem.2007.096032) |
| `chart.draw_trade_off_curve()` `get_numbers_drawn()` | The row of the marked dot holds the best cut-off by Youden's index in its value column (R pROC::coords(roc, "best", best.method = "youden") threshold), the sensitivity there as y and the false alarms (1 - specificity) as x, as drawn. | table.compare_to_truth gives the same cut-off with its sensitivity and specificity as a result table. | **P3.** The number written next to the dot is pROC's threshold, given back unchanged. Youden's index is the usual summary of a cut-off (Youden 1950), though other criteria choose other points (Perkins & Schisterman 2006). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Needs R pROC; one marked dot per curve. | [Youden 1950](https://doi.org/10.1002/1097-0142(1950)3:1<32::AID-CNCR2820030106>3.0.CO;2-3); [Perkins & Schisterman 2006](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwj063) |

## Agreement and reliability

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.agreement_between()` | Number columns: the ICC for single raters, absolute agreement, two-way random (ICC(A,1), Shrout and Fleiss' ICC(2,1)), from R psych::ICC(). Categories: Cohen's kappa for two raters, Fleiss' kappa (a generalisation of Scott's pi) for more. | raters=, checking_for=, of= for the other ICC forms; test_method="kappa", "krippendorff", "lin"; near_misses="linear" or "quadratic" (weighted kappa). | **P1.** The ICC form must be chosen and reported; ICC(A,1) is the form Koo & Li's flowchart leads to when the raters are a sample of possible raters and single ratings will be used (Shrout & Fleiss 1979; McGraw & Wong 1996). Kappa is printed with the raw agreement, because it can be low when agreement is high (Feinstein & Cicchetti 1990). The ICC intervals follow McGraw & Wong (psych::ICC; irr::icc's interval for ICC(A,k) does not), and Krippendorff's alpha comes from R icr, which gives Krippendorff's published examples (irr::kripp.alpha weights the pairs wrongly for 3 or more raters without empty cells; Hayes & Krippendorff 2007). **Checks:** The ICC words (poor to excellent) use both ends of the interval (Koo & Li 2016); fewer than 2 complete rows are refused. | [Koo & Li 2016](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jcm.2016.02.012); [Shrout & Fleiss 1979](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.86.2.420); [McGraw & Wong 1996](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.1.1.30); [Cohen 1960](https://doi.org/10.1177/001316446002000104); [Cohen 1968](https://doi.org/10.1037/h0026256); [Fleiss 1971](https://doi.org/10.1037/h0031619); [Hayes & Krippendorff 2007](https://doi.org/10.1080/19312450709336664); [Feinstein & Cicchetti 1990](https://doi.org/10.1016/0895-4356(90)90158-L) |
| `table.agreement_between()` `columns of numbers` | The intraclass correlation from R irr::icc(): two-way, absolute agreement, one rater (ICC(A,1), Shrout and Fleiss' ICC(2,1)). | raters="these only" or "different per row"; checking_for="consistency"; of="average of raters"; test_method="lin". | **P1.** The ICC form must be chosen and named; ICC(A,1) is the form for raters who are a sample of possible raters when single ratings will be used (Shrout & Fleiss 1979; McGraw & Wong 1996; Koo & Li 2016). irr's own default, the one-way model, would ignore that the same raters scored every row. **Checks:** The form is always printed beside the value. | [Shrout & Fleiss 1979](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.86.2.420); [McGraw & Wong 1996](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.1.1.30); [Koo & Li 2016](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jcm.2016.02.012) |
| `table.limits_of_agreement()` | Bland-Altman limits: the average difference plus and minus 1.96 standard deviations (n - 1), with intervals for the average and both limits; Lin's concordance as well. | interval= another level. | **P1.** Bland & Altman's limits answer 'do the two methods agree?', which a correlation does not (Bland & Altman 1986); the intervals of the limits are their approximate ones (Bland & Altman 1999), which are a little too narrow in small samples, where exact intervals exist (Carkeet 2015). **Checks:** Needs R; both columns must hold numbers. | [Bland & Altman 1986](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(86)90837-8); [Bland & Altman 1999](https://doi.org/10.1177/096228029900800204); [Carkeet 2015](https://doi.org/10.1097/OPX.0000000000000513); [Lin 1989](https://doi.org/10.2307/2532051) |
| `table.reliability_of()` | Cronbach's alpha (raw), with Feldt's interval and alpha with each item left out (R psych). | interval= another level. | **P3.** Alpha with an interval is the usual report (Cronbach 1951; Feldt 1965); the printout says that alpha rises with more items and does not show that the items measure one thing (Sijtsma 2009). Methodologists prefer omega, which does not assume that every item weighs the same (McNeish 2018); simplydata does not yet offer it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Columns must hold numbers; a note names an item whose removal raises alpha. | [Cronbach 1951](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02310555); [Feldt 1965](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02289499); [Sijtsma 2009](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11336-008-9101-0); [McNeish 2018](https://doi.org/10.1037/met0000144) |
| `chart.draw_difference_dots()` | The Bland-Altman picture: differences against averages, with the limits (n - 1 standard deviation) and their 95 % intervals shaded. | There is no option for the limits. | **P1.** As table.limits_of_agreement (Bland & Altman 1999). statsmodels' own limits divide by n, so they come from R. **Checks:** Needs R; two different number columns. | [Bland & Altman 1986](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(86)90837-8); [Bland & Altman 1999](https://doi.org/10.1177/096228029900800204) |

## Fitting

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.fit_line()` | Least squares (SciPy linregress); the 95 % interval of the slope from the t distribution with n - 2 degrees of freedom, as R confint(lm(...)). | test_method="theil-sen", "siegel", "standardised major axis", "major axis", "deming", "passing-bablok"; with_bends=; changes_at=. | **P3.** Least squares predicts y from x. When both columns carry error and the question is how they change together, another line answers it (Warton et al. 2006), which test_method= offers. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 3 rows and a constant x are refused; notes on the shape of the residuals. | [Warton et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1017/S1464793106007007) |
| `table.fit_line()` `test_method="theil-sen"` | Sen's slope (the median of all pairwise slopes) with its 95 % interval; the intercept is median(y) - slope x median(x) (Conover; SciPy's default). | test_method="siegel" (repeated medians). | **P3.** The Theil-Sen slope resists outliers (Sen 1968); R mblm takes the median of y - slope x x instead, which the help says. *Basis: convention.* | [Sen 1968](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1968.10480934); [Siegel 1982](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/69.1.242) |
| `table.fit_line()` `test_method="standardised major axis"` | The standardised major axis with smatr's own 95 % intervals (R smatr::sma()). | test_method="major axis". | **P2.** When neither column is the 'cause', as in allometry, the standardised major axis describes how two measures change together (Warton et al. 2006, 2012). **Checks:** A note when the slope's sign is not pinned down (p of 0.05 or more). | [Warton et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1017/S1464793106007007); [Warton et al. 2012 (smatr)](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2041-210X.2011.00153.x) |
| `table.fit_line()` `test_method="deming", y_error_over_x_error=` | Deming regression with the ratio of error variances you give (1 when not given), with mcr's analytical intervals. | y_error_over_x_error=. | **P2.** Deming regression allows for error in both methods of a method comparison (Linnet 1993). The ratio should come from replicate measurements; 1 when it is not given is simplydata's starting value. The analytical intervals repeat exactly, where mcr's default bootstrap changes from run to run. *Basis: starting value.* | [Linnet 1993](https://doi.org/10.1093/clinchem/39.3.424) |
| `table.fit_line()` `test_method="passing-bablok"` | Passing and Bablok's rank-based line with its own intervals (mcr's "analytical"). | test_method="deming". | **P2.** Passing & Bablok 1983 designed the method for comparing two laboratory methods; it resists outliers and assumes no distribution. **Checks:** Fewer than 10 pairs and negative values are refused; below 30 pairs a note. | [Passing & Bablok 1983](https://doi.org/10.1515/cclm.1983.21.11.709) |
| `table.fit_line()` `with_bends=` | A continuous broken line (segmented's model, seed 1), with Davies' test of 'no bend at all'. | with_bends= 1 to 3. | **P2.** The bend's own p-value is not valid because the data chose where the bend is; Davies' test allows for that (Davies 1987; Muggeo 2003). **Checks:** Needs 5 rows per piece; when no bend is found the straight line is kept, with a note. | [Davies 1987](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/74.1.33); [Muggeo 2003](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1545) |
| `table.fit_line()` `changes_at=` | Segmented regression of an interrupted time series: a change of level and of slope at the point given, rows at the point counting as after; the lag-1 autocorrelation of the residuals is printed. | changes_at= the point. | **P2.** Lopez Bernal, Cummins & Gasparrini 2017 model both changes and ask that autocorrelation be checked and, when it is large, allowed for (for example with HAC standard errors); simplydata prints it but does not adjust. **Checks:** At least 3 rows on each side of the point. | [Lopez Bernal et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1093/ije/dyw098) |
| `table.fit_curve()` | A polynomial of degree 2 by least squares (raw powers, as R lm(y ~ poly(x, 2, raw = TRUE))), fitted on x mapped onto [-1, 1] and turned back into the raw powers by statsmodels t_test(), so the standard errors keep their digits when the powers are nearly in proportion (NIST Filip). | of_degree= 1 to 10; of_kind= a named curve; fit_formula() for any formula. | **P5.** Degree 2 is a starting value, not a published rule; from degree 4 a note says the curve wiggles and points to smoothing or a formula. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Degrees outside 1 to 10 and too few distinct x are refused. |  |
| `table.fit_curve()` `of_kind="logistic growth"` | Start values from R's self-starting model (stats::getInitial()), or a search of the whole range without R; then least squares. | of_kind= another named curve. | **P3.** Self-starting models find start values from the data, so the fit does not depend on a guess (Pinheiro & Bates 2000; Bates & Watts 1988); the least-squares answer is the same either way. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Refused with a hint when the fit does not settle. | [Pinheiro & Bates 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/b98882); [Bates & Watts 1988](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316757) |
| `table.fit_curve()` `always=` | Isotonic regression by pool adjacent violators (SciPy optimize.isotonic_regression) on y in the order of x; at a tied x the biggest y comes first for a rising curve (the smallest for a falling one), as R isoreg() orders them (order(x, -y)), so the values at a tied x are pooled. | always="falling"; weights= for weighted pooling (R isoreg() has no weights); of_degree= or of_kind= for a curve with a formula. | **P3.** Pool adjacent violators gives the least-squares curve that never goes down (Ayer et al. 1955); ordering the ties as R isoreg() does makes the fitted values equal R's $yf. **Checks:** No of_degree= or of_kind= with always=; weights= above 0 in every row used. | [Ayer et al. 1955](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177728423) |
| `table.fit_formula()` | Nonlinear least squares by Levenberg-Marquardt (as minpack.lm::nlsLM(); R nls() uses Gauss-Newton and gives the same estimates when both converge), start values 1 (or the middle of between=), Wald t intervals as summary(nls); AIC as R logLik.nls. | test_method="trust region" or "dogbox"; search="whole range" (differential evolution first); start=, between=. | **P3.** Least squares is what R nls() fits. The Wald intervals rest on a straight-line approximation; Bates & Watts 1988 show they can be poor for strongly curved models and prefer profile-likelihood intervals (R confint()), which simplydata does not yet offer. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Levenberg-Marquardt with limits, impossible start values and a search that does not settle are refused; values the data do not pin down get infinite standard errors, a note and converged "no" (R nls() stops there); with R squared near 0 as well, the note says the search may have stopped in a flat region and suggests other start values or search="whole range"; R squared below 0.01 alone gets the same hint; a value at its limit gets a note. | [Bates & Watts 1988](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316757) |
| `table.fit_dose_response()` | The 4-parameter log-logistic curve (drc's LL.4), with the ED50 and its delta-method interval. | of_kind="3 parameter log-logistic", "5 parameter log-logistic", "weibull"; doses_for_effect=. | **P3.** LL.4 is drc's usual model (Ritz et al. 2015); the OECD guidance lists log-logistic curves among several models for ecotoxicity data (OECD 2006), so of_kind= offers the others. The ED50 should come from the fitted curve with its interval, not from the raw averages (Ritz et al. 2015); drc gives no interval unless asked, so simplydata always asks. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Negative doses and too few distinct doses are refused; an ED outside the doses tested gets a note. | [Ritz et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0146021); [OECD 2006](https://doi.org/10.1787/9789264085275-en) |
| `table.find_best_power_of()` | The Box-Cox power with its 95 % profile-likelihood interval, and the simple power (1, 0.5, 0, -1 ...) inside the interval nearest to it. | test_method="yeo-johnson" (also for values at or below 0); using= (R car::powerTransform). | **P2.** Box & Cox 1964 chose the power by likelihood; the interval shows which simple powers fit as well. For counts and shares a matching model is better than a transformation (O'Hara & Kotze 2010), which a note says. **Checks:** Fewer than 10 values are refused; Box-Cox refuses values at or below 0. | [Box & Cox 1964](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1964.tb00553.x); [O'Hara & Kotze 2010](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2041-210X.2010.00021.x) |
| `table.find_best_power_of()` `show="log-likelihood"` | The powers -2 to 2 in steps of 0.1 (row 21 is power 0, the log), each with SciPy boxcox_llf; yeojohnson_llf with test_method="yeo-johnson". | show="power" (the default) for the best power itself with its interval. | **P5.** -2 to 2 holds the usual powers (Box & Cox 1964; Yeo & Johnson 2000), and steps of 0.1 are fine enough to read the curve; the range is simplydata's starting value, the same as for the normal plot. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** At least 10 values; Box-Cox needs values above 0; not with test_method="tukey lambda". | [Box & Cox 1964](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1964.tb00553.x); [Yeo & Johnson 2000](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/87.4.954) |
| `table.find_best_power_of()` `show="normal plot correlation"` | 41 powers from -2 to 2 (SciPy boxcox_normplot, its N=41 points); yeojohnson_normplot with test_method="yeo-johnson", and ppcc_plot of the Tukey lambda shape with test_method="tukey lambda". | show="best by normal plot" for the single straightest power. | **P5.** The probability plot correlation measures how straight the plot is (Filliben 1975); 41 points from -2 to 2 match the log-likelihood table, step 0.1. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** At least 10 values; Box-Cox needs values above 0. | [Filliben 1975](https://doi.org/10.1080/00401706.1975.10489279) |
| `table.find_best_power_of()` `show="best by normal plot"` | The Box-Cox power whose normal probability plot is straightest (SciPy boxcox_normmax(method="pearsonr")). | show="power" (the default) for the power by maximum likelihood, as Box & Cox chose it. | **P3.** Maximum likelihood is Box & Cox's own way and stays the default; the straightest plot (Filliben 1975) is offered for those who choose by the plot. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Box-Cox only (Yeo-Johnson is refused); at least 10 values above 0. | [Box & Cox 1964](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1964.tb00553.x); [Filliben 1975](https://doi.org/10.1080/00401706.1975.10489279) |
| `table.find_best_power_of()` `test_method="tukey lambda"` | The Tukey lambda shape whose probability plot correlation is biggest (SciPy ppcc_max, its default dist="tukeylambda"); the sentence names the usual shape it is close to. | test_method="box-cox" or "yeo-johnson" for a power of the values. | **P3.** Filliben 1975 introduced the probability plot correlation with the Tukey lambda family to tell long tails from short ones. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 10 values; not with show="log-likelihood" or "best by normal plot". | [Filliben 1975](https://doi.org/10.1080/00401706.1975.10489279) |

## Finding and solving

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.find_where_smallest()` | One unknown with between=: Brent's method (as R optimize()) after a scan of 200 points. Several unknowns: Nelder-Mead (R optim()'s default), or L-BFGS-B within limits; linear problems: the HiGHS solver. | test_method=; search="whole range" (differential evolution); rules=; whole_numbers=True. | **P3.** Nelder-Mead and Brent's method are R's defaults (Nelder & Mead 1965). The 200-point scan, L-BFGS-B for limits and HiGHS for linear problems are simplydata's choices. Nelder-Mead can stop at a point that is not a minimum (McKinnon 1998), which is why the scan and search="whole range" exist. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A missing start or range is refused; notes when the search did not settle or the best point is at an edge. | [Nelder & Mead 1965](https://doi.org/10.1093/comjnl/7.4.308); [McKinnon 1998](https://doi.org/10.1137/S1052623496303482) |
| `sdata.find_where_smallest()` `test_method="golden section"` | The lowest of 200 evenly spaced points and its two neighbours make the bracket, so the golden sections stay inside between=; then SciPy minimize_scalar(method="golden") to a tolerance of 1e-10, as Brent's method in find_where_smallest (R optimize(tol = 1e-10)). | Without test_method=: Brent's method (golden sections with parabolic steps, R optimize()); start= instead of between= makes the bracket around the start; search="whole range" looks everywhere. | **P3.** The golden-section search closes in on one dip inside a bracket (Kiefer 1953); the 200-point scan makes that bracket inside the range, as for Brent's method, and the tolerance is find_where_smallest's, so the two methods agree. The 200 points are simplydata's own starting value; a narrower between= looks closer. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A formula with no value at any of the 200 points is refused; a lowest point at an end of the range, or a flat bottom (several points as low), gets a note. | [Kiefer 1953](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0002-9939-1953-0055639-3) |
| `sdata.find_where_smallest()` `test_method="grid search"` | SciPy brute on about 20,000 grid points in all, shared evenly among the names (20,000 for one name, 141 along each of two), then a local polish that keeps to between= (L-BFGS-B), as after DIRECT; SciPy brute's own finish (fmin) may leave the range. | search="whole range" with test_method="differential evolution", "dual annealing", "shgo", "basin hopping" or "direct". | **P4.** A grid finds the best region only as finely as its spacing, so a local search settles inside it; L-BFGS-B keeps to the limits (Byrd et al. 1995), where Nelder-Mead, brute's own finish, does not. The 20,000 points are simplydata's own starting value; no option changes them. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Up to 9 names (at least 3 points along each); more are refused, with test_method="differential evolution" named. | [Byrd et al. 1995](https://doi.org/10.1137/0916069) |
| `sdata.find_where_smallest()` `test_method="newton conjugate gradient"` | SciPy minimize(method="Newton-CG") with the slopes from scipy.differentiate.jacobian (Newton-CG needs slopes as a function and SciPy has no default for them), the bends by SciPy's central differences of those slopes (hess="3-point"), a tight tolerance (xtol 1e-10) and up to 10,000 steps. | Without test_method=: Nelder-Mead (L-BFGS-B within limits); test_method="truncated newton", "bfgs" and the other slope methods. | **P5.** SciPy leaves the slopes to the user, so simplydata works them out with SciPy's own differentiation; SciPy's default for the bends is too rough to settle on a flat minimum, so central differences are used. The tolerance and the steps are simplydata's own starting values. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Newton-CG cannot keep to between=, so it is used for the start only (a note says so); a formula with a corner or a jump (abs, floor, sign ...) gets a note. |  |
| `sdata.find_where_smallest()` `test_method="truncated newton"` | SciPy minimize(method="TNC") with the slopes by SciPy's central differences (jac="3-point"; its default uses forward differences), tight tolerances (xtol 1e-12, ftol 1e-15, gtol 1e-10) and up to 20,000 evaluations; TNC keeps to between= when it is given. | Without test_method=: Nelder-Mead (L-BFGS-B within limits); test_method="newton conjugate gradient", "bfgs" and the other slope methods. | **P5.** A truncated Newton method solves each Newton step only roughly, by an inner iteration (Nash 1984), as R nloptr's NLOPT_LD_TNEWTON; central differences give more accurate slopes than forward ones. The tolerances are simplydata's own starting values. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A formula with a corner or a jump (abs, floor, sign ...) gets a note; so does a search that did not settle. | [Nash 1984](https://doi.org/10.1137/0721052) |
| `sdata.find_where_biggest()` | The same methods as find_where_smallest, on the negative of the formula. | As find_where_smallest. | **P3.** As find_where_smallest. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As find_where_smallest. | [Nelder & Mead 1965](https://doi.org/10.1093/comjnl/7.4.308) |
| `sdata.solve()` | One unknown in a range: every sign change in a scan of 1000 steps, each solved by Brent's method (as rootSolve::uniroot.all(), whose own default is 100 steps); a polynomial: all its roots (as polyroot()); equations together: exact linear algebra (as solve()), or Powell's hybrid method (MINPACK hybrd, SciPy root()'s default; R nleqslv's default is Broyden with a double-dogleg step). | test_method="bisection", "ridder", "toms748", "levenberg-marquardt", "broyden"; start=. | **P5.** Brent's method and Powell's hybrid method are the standard solvers of SciPy and MINPACK. The 1000-step scan is simplydata's own starting value; the printout says that roots that only touch zero can be missed by a sign scan. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A different number of unknowns and equations, and singular linear systems, are refused. |  |
| `sdata.solve()` `exactly=True with a rule` | Rules (<, >, <=, >=) are solved over the real numbers (SymPy solveset with domain S.Reals; reduce_inequalities for several rules in one letter). | None: equations (=) give every solution, complex ones too. | **P4.** < and > compare real numbers only; SymPy solveset's own default domain is the complex numbers, where a rule has no meaning. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One letter only; equations and rules are not mixed; the answer is written as ranges with their ends. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `sdata.solve()` `test_method="fixed point"` | SciPy optimize.fixed_point with Steffensen's acceleration (method="del2", SciPy's default) from start= (or the middle of between=), to a tolerance of 1e-12, as R uniroot(tol = 1e-12) in solve; up to 1000 steps. | Without test_method=: Brent's method in a range (between=); test_method="secant" from start=. | **P3.** Aitken's delta-squared process (Aitken 1927), used at every step as in Steffensen's method, speeds up a fixed-point iteration that would otherwise settle slowly; it is SciPy's default. The tolerance is the one of solve's other methods, so they agree. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The equation needs the letter alone on the left (x = g(x)); an iteration that does not settle, or an answer that does not meet the equation, is refused; an answer outside between= gets a note. | [Aitken 1927](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0370164600022070) |
| `sdata.solve()` `test_method="broyden second"` | Each equation as left minus right, solved by SciPy root(method="broyden2") from start= to a tight tolerance (fatol 1e-12) and up to 5000 steps; SciPy's own default stops at about 6e-6. | Without test_method=: exact linear algebra for straight-line equations, else Powell's hybrid method (MINPACK hybrd); test_method="broyden", "levenberg-marquardt" and the other solvers of SciPy root. | **P5.** SciPy's own tolerance for these solvers stops at about 6e-6, far looser than solve's other methods (1e-12, as R uniroot), so a tight one is used and every equation is checked at the answer. Broyden's second method updates the inverse of the Jacobian (Broyden 1965). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A search that breaks down, or values that do not make every equation true (left minus right within 1e-8 of the size at the start), is refused; a value outside between= gets a note. | [Broyden 1965](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0025-5718-1965-0198670-6) |
| `sdata.solve()` `test_method="diagonal broyden"` | Each equation as left minus right, solved by SciPy root(method="diagbroyden") from start= to a tight tolerance (fatol 1e-12) and up to 5000 steps; SciPy's own default stops at about 6e-6. | Without test_method=: exact linear algebra for straight-line equations, else Powell's hybrid method (MINPACK hybrd); test_method="broyden", "levenberg-marquardt" and the other solvers of SciPy root. | **P5.** SciPy's own tolerance for these solvers stops at about 6e-6, far looser than solve's other methods (1e-12, as R uniroot), so a tight one is used and every equation is checked at the answer. The diagonal Broyden method is SciPy's; no published comparison fixes these settings. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A search that breaks down, or values that do not make every equation true (left minus right within 1e-8 of the size at the start), is refused; a value outside between= gets a note. |  |
| `sdata.solve()` `test_method="anderson mixing"` | Each equation as left minus right, solved by SciPy root(method="anderson") from start= to a tight tolerance (fatol 1e-12) and up to 5000 steps; SciPy's own default stops at about 6e-6. | Without test_method=: exact linear algebra for straight-line equations, else Powell's hybrid method (MINPACK hybrd); test_method="broyden", "levenberg-marquardt" and the other solvers of SciPy root. | **P5.** SciPy's own tolerance for these solvers stops at about 6e-6, far looser than solve's other methods (1e-12, as R uniroot), so a tight one is used and every equation is checked at the answer. Anderson mixing combines the last few steps to choose the next one (Anderson 1965). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A search that breaks down, or values that do not make every equation true (left minus right within 1e-8 of the size at the start), is refused; a value outside between= gets a note. | [Anderson 1965](https://doi.org/10.1145/321296.321305) |
| `sdata.solve()` `test_method="newton-krylov"` | Each equation as left minus right, solved by SciPy root(method="krylov") from start= to a tight tolerance (fatol 1e-12) and up to 5000 steps; SciPy's own default stops at about 6e-6. | Without test_method=: exact linear algebra for straight-line equations, else Powell's hybrid method (MINPACK hybrd); test_method="broyden", "levenberg-marquardt" and the other solvers of SciPy root. | **P5.** SciPy's own tolerance for these solvers stops at about 6e-6, far looser than solve's other methods (1e-12, as R uniroot), so a tight one is used and every equation is checked at the answer. Newton-Krylov methods take Newton steps without forming the Jacobian (Knoll & Keyes 2004). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A search that breaks down, or values that do not make every equation true (left minus right within 1e-8 of the size at the start), is refused; a value outside between= gets a note. | [Knoll & Keyes 2004](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jcp.2003.08.010) |
| `sdata.solve()` `test_method="linear mixing"` | Each equation as right minus left (these methods take the Jacobian to be about minus 1, as for x = g(x)), solved by SciPy root(method="linearmixing") from start= to a tight tolerance (fatol 1e-12) and up to 5000 steps; SciPy's own default stops at about 6e-6. | Without test_method=: exact linear algebra for straight-line equations, else Powell's hybrid method (MINPACK hybrd); test_method="broyden", "levenberg-marquardt" and the other solvers of SciPy root. | **P5.** SciPy's own tolerance for these solvers stops at about 6e-6, far looser than solve's other methods (1e-12, as R uniroot), so a tight one is used and every equation is checked at the answer. With right minus left, an equation written as x = g(x) settles as a damped fixed-point iteration. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A search that breaks down, or values that do not make every equation true (left minus right within 1e-8 of the size at the start), is refused; a value outside between= gets a note. When it fails, the sentence says to write each name alone on the left, one equation for each name in order. |  |
| `sdata.solve()` `test_method="exciting mixing"` | Each equation as right minus left (these methods take the Jacobian to be about minus 1, as for x = g(x)), solved by SciPy root(method="excitingmixing") from start= to a tight tolerance (fatol 1e-12) and up to 5000 steps; SciPy's own default stops at about 6e-6. | Without test_method=: exact linear algebra for straight-line equations, else Powell's hybrid method (MINPACK hybrd); test_method="broyden", "levenberg-marquardt" and the other solvers of SciPy root. | **P5.** SciPy's own tolerance for these solvers stops at about 6e-6, far looser than solve's other methods (1e-12, as R uniroot), so a tight one is used and every equation is checked at the answer. With right minus left, an equation written as x = g(x) settles as a tuned fixed-point iteration. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A search that breaks down, or values that do not make every equation true (left minus right within 1e-8 of the size at the start), is refused; a value outside between= gets a note. When it fails, the sentence says to write each name alone on the left, one equation for each name in order. |  |
| `sdata.solve()` `whole_numbers_only=` | The whole-number solutions SymPy diophantine gives, with each change of sign and order (permute=True), and the kind of equation SymPy finds (classify_diop); a family of solutions keeps SymPy's letters (t_0, p, q), any whole numbers, and is said to be SymPy's family, which may leave out multiples and swaps of its members. | Without whole_numbers_only=: the solutions among all numbers (SymPy solve). | **P4.** SymPy's default (permute=False) gives one solution of each kind only, so x**2 + y**2 = 25 would miss most of its 12 solutions; for some equations (x**2 + y**2 = z**2) SymPy gives only its primitive family, so simplydata never calls a family every solution. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One polynomial equation (=), with exactly=True. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `sdata.find_sensitivity_of()` | The names in the order they first turn up in the formula, each spread evenly over its range; a Sobol' sample of 1024 points for each base sample and seed 1 (SciPy sobol_indices). | how_many= (a power of 2, at least 64) and with_seed=. | **P5.** Sobol' indices with Saltelli et al. 2010's estimators are the standard variance-based sensitivity measure; an even spread says only the range is known. 1024 points and seed 1 are simplydata's starting values (more points, steadier indices). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A range for every name in the formula and none extra, each low then high; a formula that does not vary is refused. | [Saltelli et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cpc.2009.09.018) |
| `sdata.find_best_pairing()` | The exact answer of the linear assignment problem: SciPy optimize.linear_sum_assignment (the shortest augmenting path of Crouse 2016), as R lpSolve::lp.assign(); rows and columns named 1, 2, 3 ... (or by the table's names), not counted from 0. | aim_for="biggest total" for scores instead of costs; with_distances= for the quadratic assignment problem. | **P3.** The assignment problem has an exact answer that is quick to find, so no approximate method is needed; Crouse 2016 describes the algorithm SciPy uses. Positions count from 1 everywhere in simplydata. **Checks:** Empty cells are pairs that are not allowed, and a grid where they leave no full pairing is refused; infinite numbers are refused; rows or columns left free are said; test_method= is refused without with_distances=. | [Crouse 2016](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAES.2016.140952) |
| `sdata.find_best_pairing()` `with_distances=` | The Fast Approximate QAP method (Vogelstein et al. 2015), SciPy quadratic_assignment's default; test_method="2-opt" swaps places instead. | test_method="2-opt" (swaps of two places until no swap helps, with_seed=1); aim_for="biggest total". | **P5.** The quadratic assignment problem is hard: no method proves the best answer for more than a few items, so simplydata says the answer is a good one. FAQ is SciPy's default (Vogelstein et al. 2015); R has no common equivalent. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Both grids square and of the same size, with a number in every cell. | [Vogelstein et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0121002) |

## Formulas and exact answers

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.formula()` | Every letter of the formula is handed to SymPy as a plain Symbol of its own (no assumptions such as positive or real), so E, I, N, S and beta stay letters. | letters_are= gives a letter SymPy's assumptions (positive, zero or more, negative, real, whole number); write pi for 3.14159... and exp(1) for e. | **P4.** SymPy's parser alone reads E as the number e, I as the imaginary unit and N, S, beta as its own functions, which would change a researcher's formula without a word (Meurer et al. 2017 describe the parser). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The text is checked with Python's ast first: numbers, letters, + - * / ** ^, brackets and the functions of fit_formula only; an equation (=) is refused with a pointer to sdata.solve(exactly=True). | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.make_simpler()` `by=` | Each value is one of SymPy's own simplifiers with SymPy's own defaults: "logs" is logcombine, which joins logs only where the letters allow it (force=False), and "trig rules" is fu. | Without by=: SymPy simplify, which tries many of them and keeps the shortest. | **P3.** SymPy's defaults never change a formula into one that is wrong for some values (log(x) + log(y) is log(x*y) only for positive x and y); letters_are= says what a letter is, so the step may go further. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The plain name or SymPy's name of the simplifier. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.turn_into_sum()` `by=` | Each value is one of SymPy's expand_* functions with SymPy's own defaults: "logs" (expand_log) splits a log only where the letters allow it (force=False). | Without by=: SymPy expand, every kind of step at once. | **P3.** As for make_simpler(by=): a step that is wrong for some values is left out unless letters_are= allows it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The plain name or SymPy's name; powers of sums with letters above 100 are refused. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_slope_formula()` `step=` | The forward difference (f(x + step) - f(x))/step: SymPy differentiate_finite at the points x and x + step; with step=1 it is SymPy difference_delta, the step between neighbouring terms of a sequence. | Without step=: the slope itself (SymPy diff). | **P5.** SymPy's own default for differentiate_finite is the central difference at x - 1/2 and x + 1/2, which for a sequence uses half-steps that are not terms; the forward difference is the one of sequences and of difference equations. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** step= is a number other than 0 or a new letter; one letter, how_many_times=1. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_value_at()` | One value: a decimal is put in as the fraction it stands for (SymPy nsimplify) and the answer is worked out exactly before it becomes a decimal (subs, then evalf). Many values: SymPy lambdify with NumPy and SciPy. | None; for exact answers use put_in(x=...) and keep the formula. | **P3.** Exact arithmetic first loses no digits on the way; lambdify is SymPy's own route to fast numbers. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Every letter needs a value; a value that is not an ordinary number is refused. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_limit_as()` | At a number, the limit from both sides (SymPy limit(dir="+-")); at infinity, from below. | from_side="above" or "below" for one side only (SymPy dir="+" or "-"); when the two sides differ, simplydata says so and gives the value from each. | **P4.** A limit in school maths and in most textbooks is two-sided; SymPy's own default (from above only) would give a value where the limit does not exist, such as 1/x at 0. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** goes_to= is a number, "infinity" or "-infinity". | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_taylor_series()` | Around 0, up to the power 4 (SymPy series with n = up_to_power + 1, the rest left out with removeO). | around= another point; up_to_power= more or fewer powers. | **P5.** A few powers show the shape near the point; the option names the highest power kept, not SymPy's order of the left-out term, which learners misread. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** up_to_power= a whole number, 0 or more. |  |
| `formula.group_by_powers_of()` | The formula is multiplied out first (SymPy expand), then the terms are gathered by the powers of the letter (collect). | None: formula.turn_into_product() or formula.make_simpler() write it in other ways; formula.undo() takes the step back. | **P5.** SymPy collect gathers only the terms it can see, so (a + x)*(b + x) would stay as it is; expanding first gives the grouping a reader expects from the name, as in textbooks. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The letter must be a letter of the formula; a sum with letters raised to a whole power above 100 is refused before it is multiplied out. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_roots()` | Every root of the polynomial, exactly (SymPy all_roots: radicals where SymPy can write them, else its CRootOf, worked out to any number of digits), one row for each different root with how many times it repeats; real roots first, as SymPy orders them. | real_only=True (real_roots), fractions_only=True (ground_roots), between= (the real roots in a range), exactly=False (nroots), show="count" (count_roots) or show="intervals" (intervals). | **P5.** A polynomial of degree n has n roots counted with their repeats (the fundamental theorem of algebra); SymPy lists a repeated root once for each time, and one row with the count reads as textbooks write it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One letter, number coefficients (whole numbers or fractions for the exact roots); anything else is refused with the word that does it (sdata.solve, exactly=False). | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_roots()` `fractions_only=` | The whole-number and fraction roots that factoring over the rationals finds (SymPy ground_roots), smallest first. | Without fractions_only=: every root (all_roots). | **P5.** SymPy ground_roots gives a dict without an order; smallest first is the order of the other roots tables. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As find_roots. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_roots()` `exactly=` | exactly=False: every root in decimals from SymPy nroots with 15 significant digits, about as many as a float keeps. | exactly=True (the default): exact roots. | **P3.** 15 digits is nroots' own default (n=15) and what a float can hold, so nothing printed is beyond a float's precision. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As find_roots; not with real_only= or fractions_only=. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_degree()` | A formula of one letter: its highest power (SymPy degree). A formula of several letters without for_letter=: the total degree, the highest sum of powers in one term (SymPy total_degree). | for_letter="x" gives the degree in one letter (degree); a list gives each (degree_list). | **P5.** SymPy degree alone takes the first letter in its own order (alphabetical), which depends on how the letters are named; the total degree is the degree of a polynomial of several letters in algebra texts. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The formula must be a polynomial in the letters; 0 has degree minus infinity, as in SymPy. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.take_transform()` | The transform, from the letter t to s for Laplace, x to k for Fourier, sine and cosine, x to s for Mellin, r to k for Hankel (each the other way for the inverse); the Laplace transform is printed with where it holds (Re(s) > a, from SymPy) and the Mellin transform with its strip; the others are given without SymPy's conditions (noconds=True). The Fourier transform has SymPy's convention, 2*pi in the power (exp(-2*pi*I*x*k)). | from_letter= and to_letter= other letters; order= the order of the Hankel transform's Bessel function; between= the strip of s of the inverse Mellin transform. | **P3.** Where a Laplace or Mellin transform holds is part of its answer, as in the tables of Abramowitz and Stegun (1964, chapter 29); the letters are the ones tables use, and the Fourier convention with 2*pi in the power has the same form forwards and back. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The name or SymPy's name of the transform; to_letter= must be a new letter; an unworked answer is refused. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.check_if_always()` `between=` | Without between=, every real number; with it, the range between the two ends. The check is made inside the part of that range where the formula has a value (SymPy continuous_domain), so sqrt(x) is rising and log(x) on [0, infinity] is rising; a formula with a gap inside the range (1/x) gives None. | between=[low, high] for a range. | **P3.** A formula rises or falls where it has a value; SymPy's own check on a closed range with an end where the slope has no value (log(x) at 0, sqrt(x) at 0) answers False, which is wrong for these formulas. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** between= needs the low end first; SymPy's None (it cannot tell) is said plainly. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_fourier_series()` | Over -pi to pi, the first 3 parts of the series that are not 0, the constant being one part and each frequency (its cosine and sine together) one part (SymPy fourier_series, then truncate with its default of 3). | between= another range of finite length; how_many_terms= from 1 to 100. | **P3.** -pi to pi is the range of the Fourier series in textbooks, and SymPy truncate keeps 3 parts by default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** between= must have finite ends. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.find_wronskian()` `whole_numbers_only=` | The Casoratian as a formula in the letter (SymPy casoratian with zero=False), not made simpler. | Without whole_numbers_only=: the Wronskian (SymPy wronskian). | **P4.** SymPy's own default (zero=True) puts 0 in for the letter, which gives a number where a reader of a difference-equation text expects the formula; the formula at 0 is then find_value_at(n=0). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The letter that counts is the formulas' one letter, or for_letter=. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `formula.show_as_tree()` | Each part on its own line with its kind (Add, Pow, Symbol), without the assumptions SymPy's print_tree lists for each part by default. | as_graphviz=True gives the tree in Graphviz's dot language (SymPy dotprint). | **P5.** The assumptions (commutative, complex, finite ...) fill about ten lines for each part and hide the shape of the formula, which is what the word shows. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** None. |  |

## Areas and paths over time

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.find_area_under()` | SciPy integrate.quad (QUADPACK's adaptive Gauss-Kronrod rules, as R integrate()), with SciPy's own tolerances (1.49e-8). | test_method="gauss" (fixed_quad, 5 points); exactly=True (SymPy integrate). | **P3.** QUADPACK (Piessens et al. 1983) is the standard library for one-dimensional integrals in SciPy, R and MATLAB; R integrate() calls the same routines with a looser tolerance (about 1.2e-4). **Checks:** The formula has one letter (known= fixes others); SciPy's warnings are passed on as notes. | [Piessens et al. 1983](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-61786-7) |
| `sdata.find_area_under()` `test_method=` | test_method="gauss": Gauss-Legendre with 5 points (SciPy fixed_quad's own default), exact for polynomials up to the power 9. | test_method="quad" (the default) for an error estimate and infinite ends. | **P3.** The fixed rule needs no stopping rule; SciPy's default number of points is kept. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Both ends must be numbers. |  |
| `sdata.find_area_under()` `test_method="quasi monte carlo"` | SciPy integrate.qmc_quad with scrambled Halton points with_seed=1 (8 samples of 1024 points, SciPy's own numbers), so the same call gives the same estimate; the standard error is printed. | test_method="quad" (the default), "tanh-sinh", "cubature", "gauss" or "romberg on even steps"; exactly=True. | **P5.** Quasi-random points such as Halton's fill the range more evenly than random ones (Halton 1960); the fixed seed makes the scrambling repeatable. Seed 1 is simplydata's own starting value; no option changes it. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Both ends must be numbers (not infinity). | [Halton 1960](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01386213) |
| `sdata.find_area_under()` `test_method="romberg on even steps"` | SciPy integrate.romb on 1025 evenly spaced points (2**10 + 1, as romb needs). | test_method="quad" (the default) for an error estimate, "tanh-sinh", "cubature", "gauss" or "quasi monte carlo". | **P5.** romb needs 2**k + 1 evenly spaced points; 1025 is simplydata's own starting value, and the printout says that romb gives no error estimate. SciPy removed integrate.romberg in version 1.15, so test_method="romberg" points here. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** One letter; both ends numbers; a formula with no value at some of the points (such as a division by 0 at an end) is refused, with test_method="tanh-sinh" named. |  |
| `sdata.find_volume_under()` | SciPy integrate.dblquad (two letters), tplquad (three) or nquad (more), QUADPACK in each direction; the first letter of between= is the outer one. | None: the order of between= sets the order of the letters. | **P3.** Nested QUADPACK integrals are SciPy's standard for constant limits (Piessens et al. 1983). **Checks:** A range for every letter and no other. | [Piessens et al. 1983](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-61786-7) |
| `sdata.find_volume_under()` `exactly=` | SymPy integrate over each letter in turn, the inner letter (the last of between=) first, as without exactly=True. | Without exactly=True: the volume in numbers (SciPy dblquad, tplquad, nquad). | **P5.** The same order of letters as the numeric word, so the two agree; integrate is SymPy's exact integrator. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** An answer that is infinite, does not converge or cannot be found exactly is refused, with a sentence. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `sdata.find_volume_under()` `test_method="quasi monte carlo"` | SciPy integrate.qmc_quad with scrambled Halton points with_seed=1 (8 samples of 1024 points), as for find_area_under; the standard error is printed. | test_method="quad" (the default, nested QUADPACK) or "cubature"; exactly=True. | **P5.** As find_area_under(test_method="quasi monte carlo") (Halton 1960); seed 1 is simplydata's own starting value. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A range of two numbers for every letter (not infinity). | [Halton 1960](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01386213) |
| `sdata.find_path_over_time()` | SciPy integrate.solve_ivp with RK45 (Dormand-Prince), relative and absolute tolerance 1e-6 as R deSolve; the table holds the times of time_step= (else the solver's own steps). | test_method="LSODA" (R deSolve's default), "stiff" (Radau), "BDF", "DOP853", "RK23"; exactly=True (SymPy dsolve). | **P3.** RK45 is SciPy's and MATLAB's (ode45) default for problems that are not stiff (Dormand & Prince 1980); SciPy's own tolerances (1e-3) are looser than R deSolve's (1e-6, Soetaert et al. 2010), which are used so that both agree. **Checks:** Every value that changes has a start; letters that do not change need known=; the solver's failure is said. | [Dormand & Prince 1980](https://doi.org/10.1016/0771-050X(80)90013-3); [Soetaert et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v033.i09) |
| `sdata.find_path_over_time()` `exactly=` | SymPy dsolve with the start as its initial condition, the answer written as simply as SymPy can (simplify). | Without exactly=True: the path in numbers (solve_ivp). | **P3.** dsolve is SymPy's own solver of differential equations (Meurer et al. 2017). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One equation only; an answer SymPy can give only as an equation (not a formula) is refused, with a sentence. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `sdata.find_path_over_time()` `equations="d2x/dt2 = ..."` | A second-order equation becomes two first-order ones, with the speed dx/dt as a second changing value, solved by SciPy solve_ivp (RK45, tolerances 1e-6 as R deSolve). | exactly=True: SymPy dsolve; test_method= as for first-order equations. | **P3.** Writing an equation of order n as n first-order equations is the standard route of every initial-value solver (Soetaert et al. 2010 for R deSolve; SciPy solve_ivp takes first-order systems only). **Checks:** One second-order equation on its own; at_start= needs x and dx/dt. | [Soetaert et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v033.i09); [Dormand & Prince 1980](https://doi.org/10.1016/0771-050X(80)90013-3) |
