| `numbers.turn_into_running_area()` | The trapezoid rule (SciPy cumulative_trapezoid) with the first value 0, so the result is as long as the readings; time step 1 unless given or remembered. | test_method="simpson" (cumulative_simpson); time_step= or sampling_rate=. | **P3.** The trapezoid rule assumes nothing about the curve between readings; it is SciPy's, MATLAB's (cumtrapz) and R pracma's (cumtrapz) standard. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty values are refused. |  |
| `sdata.find_sum_of_series()` | The letter that counts is a whole number (a SymPy Symbol with integer=True), summed with SymPy summation; with no closed form, Sum.is_convergent decides whether it settles, and the decimal is bounded: the first terms added by evalf, the rest between two integrals (the integral test, with the trapezoid and midpoint rules when the terms bend upwards), and only the digits both bounds round to are printed. | for_letter= says which letter counts; known= fixes the others; between= may end at a letter for the formula of the partial sum. | **P4.** A series counts in whole numbers, and SymPy can simplify terms such as (-1)**(2*k) only when it knows that; the convergence test says plainly when a sum does not settle instead of giving a meaningless decimal. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A finite sum with no closed form and more than 10,000 terms is refused rather than added term by term. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `sdata.find_path_between_ends()` | SciPy integrate.solve_bvp on 101 evenly spaced places in the table, with a straight line between the known ends as the first guess and a tolerance of 1e-8 (SciPy's own default is 1e-3), as R bvpSolve's 1e-6 or tighter. | step= for other places in the table; known= fixes other letters. | **P5.** solve_bvp is the collocation with control of the residual of Kierzenka & Shampine 2001 (MATLAB bvp4c). A straight line is the simplest guess that meets both ends; the 101 places and the tolerance are simplydata's own starting values. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As many known values as values that change; ends that are numbers; step= must fit the range a whole number of times; a collocation that does not settle is refused. | [Kierzenka & Shampine 2001](https://doi.org/10.1145/502800.502801) |
| `sdata.find_product_of_series()` | The letter that counts is a whole number (SymPy integer=True), as in find_sum_of_series; a finite product has at most 1000 terms; SymPy's answer is given as SymPy writes it. | between= with a letter at the end gives the formula of the product up to it. | **P3.** A product of a series runs over whole numbers by definition; 1000 terms keeps factorial(1000), the biggest counting number formula text allows. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The ends are whole numbers, "infinity" or a letter; an unworked product is refused. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |

## Values between points

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.find_values_at()` | Straight lines between the points (as R approx()); repeated places averaged; places outside the data left empty. | joined_by="smooth curve", "curve without overshoot", "steps", "nearest" and more; smoothness=; outside="extend". | **P3.** Straight lines invent the least; the other ways are one option away. These are R approx()'s defaults (ties = mean, rule = 1). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Too few points, and collinear points on a map, are refused; extension gets a note. |  |
| `table.find_values_at()` `joined_by="rational curve"` | SciPy FloaterHormannInterpolator of degree 3 (d=3, SciPy's default), or one less than the number of points when there are fewer. | joined_by="straight lines" (the default), "smooth curve", "aaa", "polynomial" and the others. | **P3.** Floater & Hormann 2007 rational curves have no poles on the real line, so they never shoot to infinity between the points; d=3 is SciPy's default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Several values at one place are averaged first; places outside the points are empty. | [Floater & Hormann 2007](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00211-007-0093-y) |
| `table.find_values_at()` `joined_by="aaa"` | The real part of SciPy's AAA curve (SciPy gives complex numbers); a place between two known points where the curve has a pole is left empty, with a note. | joined_by="rational curve" (no poles), "smooth curve" and the others. | **P4.** The AAA algorithm (Nakatsukasa et al. 2018) works in complex numbers; for real points the imaginary part is only rounding. A value next to a pole means nothing, so it is left empty. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Several values at one place are averaged first; places outside the points are empty. | [Nakatsukasa et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1137/16M1106122) |
| `table.find_values_at()` `joined_by="smooth triangles"` | SciPy CloughTocher2DInterpolator, with the slopes at the points found step by step to a tolerance of 1e-12 (SciPy's default is 1e-6, at which the order of the points changes the last digits), up to 10,000 steps. | joined_by="straight lines" (flat triangles), "smooth surface" (thin-plate spline) or "nearest"; outside="nearest end". | **P5.** Clough-Tocher surfaces are cubic over each Delaunay triangle with matching slopes (Renka & Cline 1984); SciPy estimates the slopes by an iteration (Nielson 1983), which simplydata runs to 1e-12 so that the answer does not depend on the order of the rows. The tolerance is simplydata's own starting value. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Two directions only; outside="extend" is refused; points on one line are refused; places outside the outline are counted (empty, or the nearest point's value with outside="nearest end"). | [Renka & Cline 1984](https://doi.org/10.1216/RMJ-1984-14-1-223); [Nielson 1983](https://doi.org/10.1090/S0025-5718-1983-0679444-7) |
| `table.make_map_of()` | 50 steps in each direction, joined as find_values_at (triangles for scattered points, bilinear on a full grid), places outside the points left empty. | steps=, joined_by=, outside=. | **P5.** The same rules as find_values_at; 50 steps is a starting value for a smooth-looking map. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** At most 1 000 000 places. |  |
| `sdata.curve_through_points()` | A cubic spline through every point with natural ends (no bend at the first and last point): SciPy make_interp_spline(bc_type="natural"), as R splinefun(method = "natural") and find_values_at(joined_by="smooth curve"); places outside the points are empty. | degree= 1 to 5; ends="not-a-knot", "periodic" or "clamped"; smoothness=, allowed_error= or knots= to pass near the points; slopes=; in_order=True for a path; z= for a surface. | **P3.** Of all curves through the points, the natural cubic spline has the smallest total squared bend (de Boor 1978); it is R's and find_values_at's smooth curve, so all three agree. A curve carried on beyond its points is guesswork, so those places are empty. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** x and y of the same length, numbers only; two points with the same x are refused (average them, or smoothness=); one way of making the curve at a time; weights= only for a curve that passes near the points. | [de Boor 1978](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4612-6333-3) |
| `sdata.curve_through_points()` `smoothness=` | x rescaled to run from 0 to 1, so smoothness= is R smooth.spline's lambda, as in find_values_at (SciPy make_smoothing_spline, a knot at every distinct x; values at a repeated x averaged and weighted by their count, as R). | smoothness="auto": R smooth.spline chooses lambda by generalised cross-validation; smoothness=0 (the default) passes through every point. | **P3.** The smoothing spline makes the sum of squared distances plus lambda times the total squared bend as small as possible (Green & Silverman 1994); rescaling x as R does makes one lambda give the same curve in both. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** No degree= (the curve is cubic) and no weights=; "auto" needs R. | [Green & Silverman 1994](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4899-4473-3) |
| `sdata.curve_through_points()` `knots=` | Least squares with the inner knots given and the first and last x repeated degree + 1 times at the ends (SciPy make_lsq_spline), as splines::bs() in R. | Without knots=: through every point; allowed_error= lets FITPACK choose the knots. | **P3.** A B-spline needs degree + 1 knots at each end to reach the first and last point (de Boor 1978); this is the knot sequence of R lm(y ~ splines::bs(x, knots = ...)), so the fitted curves agree. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Each knot strictly between the first and last x; knots that leave a piece without points (Schoenberg-Whitney) are refused with a sentence that explains it. | [de Boor 1978](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4612-6333-3) |
| `sdata.curve_through_points()` `degree=` | SciPy's own ends for degrees other than 3 (make_interp_spline with no bc_type: not-a-knot for odd degrees); natural, clamped and periodic ends are chosen with ends= for cubic splines. | ends="periodic" for any degree; degree=3 (the default) with ends="natural", "not-a-knot" or "clamped". | **P3.** Natural ends are defined for cubic splines; for other degrees SciPy's own ends keep the curve the same as SciPy's make_interp_spline and splrep(s=0). The sentence says which ends were used. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** degree= a whole number from 1 to 5, with at least degree + 1 points; ends="clamped" or "not-a-knot" with another degree is refused. |  |
| `sdata.curve_through_points()` `z=` | Points that form a full grid go to SciPy RectBivariateSpline (through every point); scattered points to SmoothBivariateSpline (FITPACK's own smoothing); knots= to LSQBivariateSpline. | allowed_error= for the smoothing; knots={"x": [...], "y": [...]}; degree=; on_sphere=True. | **P3.** On a full grid a spline through every point is exact and quick; scattered points need FITPACK's smoothing surface, as bisplrep. The sentence says which spline was used. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One z for each x and y; on a grid at least degree + 1 values along each direction, scattered at least (degree + 1)**2 points; weights= send grid points to the scattered spline; places outside are empty. |  |
| `sdata.curve_through_points()` `on_sphere=` | Latitude in degrees turned into SciPy's colatitude in radians (90 degrees minus the latitude), and longitude in degrees into radians from -pi to pi, as RectSphereBivariateSpline needs (a full grid); scattered points use SmoothSphereBivariateSpline. | knots= for LSQSphereBivariateSpline; allowed_error= for the smoothing. | **P5.** Maps give latitude and longitude in degrees; SciPy's sphere splines take colatitude and longitude in radians, so simplydata converts them and the words keep taking degrees. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Latitudes from -90 to 90; z= is needed; slopes and volumes on a sphere are not given (as_scipy() has the spline in radians). |  |
| `curve.find_value_at()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), read at the places: SciPy BSpline called there, as splev and R predict(splinefun(...)); places outside the points are empty, with a note. | A path (in_order=True) takes the fraction along it; a surface takes find_value_at(x, y). | **P3.** The numbers are those of the SciPy spline itself, so they agree with SciPy and with R splinefun. A curve carried on beyond its points is guesswork. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Places are numbers; a path takes fractions from 0 to 1; a surface needs x and y. |  |
| `curve.find_slope_at()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), its slope at the places: SciPy BSpline.derivative, as splev(der=) and R splinefun(...)(x, deriv = 1); order=1 is the slope. | order=2 and up to the degree; order="all" gives every slope at one place (as spalde); along="y" for a surface. | **P3.** The numbers are those of the SciPy spline itself, so they agree with SciPy and with R splinefun. Places outside the points are empty, as for find_value_at. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** order= from 1 to the degree of the curve; slopes on a sphere are refused. |  |
| `curve.find_area_between()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), integrated from low to high: SciPy BSpline.integrate, exact for the spline (as splint); area below 0 counts as minus. | y_between= for the volume under a surface; turn_into_area_curve() for the area at every place. | **P3.** The numbers are those of the SciPy spline itself, so they agree with SciPy and with R splinefun. An area beyond the points would rest on guesswork. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** low and high are numbers inside the points; an area beyond them is refused; a volume on a sphere is refused. |  |
| `curve.find_zeros()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), solved piece by piece: SciPy PPoly.solve (as sproot), each place kept once. | where_value_is= another value than 0. | **P3.** The numbers are those of the SciPy spline itself, so they agree with SciPy and with R splinefun. Solving the pieces finds every crossing inside the points, not only the first. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A curve of y over x only; when the curve never gets there, the sentence says so. |  |
| `curve.turn_into_slope_curve()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), turned into its slope: SciPy BSpline.derivative (as splder), a spline one degree lower for each order. | order=2 and up to the degree for the slope of the slope. | **P3.** The numbers are those of the SciPy spline itself, so they agree with SciPy and with R splinefun. The new curve is a curve like any other, so every word works on it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A curve of y over x only; order= from 1 to the degree. |  |
| `curve.turn_into_area_curve()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), turned into its area: SciPy BSpline.antiderivative (as splantider), one degree higher; the area curve starts at 0 at the first point. | None; curve.find_area_between(low, high) for one area. | **P5.** SciPy's antiderivative is fixed only up to a constant; starting at 0 at the first point makes its value the area from the first point, the same as find_area_between. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A curve of y over x only. |  |
| `curve.show_pieces()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), shown as polynomial pieces (SciPy PPoly.from_spline), one row per piece; pieces of no width (the repeated knots at the ends) are left out. | basis="bernstein" gives the pieces in the Bernstein basis (SciPy BPoly), the control values of a Bezier curve. | **P5.** A piece of no width holds no part of the curve, so leaving it out gives one row for each stretch between knots. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A curve of y over x only; basis= "powers" or "bernstein". |  |
| `curve.show_knots()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), shown in one table of knots and coefficients (SciPy BSpline t and c, as splrep's tck): the coefficients run out before the knots (there are degree + 1 fewer), so the last cells are empty. | None; a surface gives its knots along x and along y, a curve with slopes= its points. | **P5.** One table keeps the knots and coefficients together as SciPy and FITPACK hold them; empty cells say plainly that there is no coefficient there. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `curve.as_scipy()` | The curve made by curve_through_points (by default a cubic spline through every point with natural ends, as R splinefun(method = "natural")), handed over as the same SciPy object (a BSpline; a PPoly for slopes=; a bivariate spline for a surface), with places outside the points empty (extrapolate=False). | None; SciPy's own words work on the object. | **P5.** Handing over the object itself lets every number be checked in SciPy; extrapolate=False keeps simplydata's rule of no guesswork beyond the points. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Distributions

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.distribution()` | Parameters with plain names (average=, standard_deviation=, tries=, chance=), turned into SciPy's. | R's names (mean=, sd=, size=, prob=) work too; any SciPy distribution by its SciPy name. | **P3.** The parameters are R's, so the answers match R's d/p/q/r functions, which each sentence names. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `sdata.distribution()` `"studentized range"` | Every SciPy distribution by a plain name, with a plain name for each parameter: here k is groups= and df degrees_of_freedom=; SciPy's names work too. | SciPy's name ("studentized_range") and parameters (k=, df=). | **P3.** Plain names read as words; SciPy's numbers are unchanged, so the percentiles agree with R ptukey()/qtukey(). *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `sdata.distribution()` `"multivariate normal"` | Joint distributions are names of sdata.distribution too: averages= is SciPy's mean and covariance= its cov; make_random_numbers gives one row per draw. | SciPy's names (mean=, cov=) work too. | **P3.** One maker for every distribution; SciPy multivariate_normal does the sums, so the numbers agree with SciPy's. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** covariance= must be a square, symmetric grid with no negative eigenvalue. |  |
| `sdata.distribution()` `"multinomial"` | For counts, find_density_at gives the chance of exactly those counts (SciPy multinomial pmf). | find_chance_that_value(is_below=) for the chance of each count below the values. | **P3.** For counts the chance of a value is the density, as R dmultinom() gives it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** chances= of 0 or more adding up to 1; tries= a whole number. |  |
| `sdata.distribution()` `"wishart"` | A value of a matrix distribution is a grid; random draws come as a block, one page per draw (SciPy wishart). | None: a grid is the only form of a matrix value. | **P5.** A grid holds a matrix as it is; one page per draw keeps each draw whole. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** scale= must be a square, symmetric grid with no negative eigenvalue. |  |
| `sdata.distribution()` `"mixture"` | The shares times each distribution's density and chances, added up (as SciPy stats.Mixture); percentiles by root finding, random numbers by first picking a distribution with its share. | None: of= and shares= say the mixture. | **P3.** This is the definition of a mixture; picking the distribution first is the standard way to draw from one. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 2 distributions of one value, all smooth or all counts; one share each, 0 or more, adding up to 1. |  |
| `sdata.distribution()` `"from values"` | The values sorted, each with its chance (SciPy rv_discrete(values=)); without chances= every value has the same chance. | chances= for chances of your own. | **P3.** SciPy rv_discrete needs the values in order; equal chances is the plain reading of a list of values. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Different values, one chance each, 0 or more, adding up to 1. |  |
| `sdata.distribution()` `"histogram"` | The values are spread evenly within each bin (SciPy rv_histogram); of=numbers with bins= counts the numbers first (NumPy histogram, 10 bins unless bins= says). | counts= and bin_edges= for bins of your own. | **P3.** Even within each bin is SciPy rv_histogram's model; 10 bins is NumPy histogram's default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One more edge than counts, each bigger than the one before; counts 0 or more, not all 0; of= needs at least 2 numbers. |  |
| `table.fit_distribution_to()` | The normal distribution, fitted by maximum likelihood (as R MASS::fitdistr() or fitdistrplus). | distribution= any name; fixed= to hold values; "kernel density". | **P3.** Maximum likelihood is the standard estimator (Venables & Ripley 2002; Delignette-Muller & Dutang 2015). The normal's standard deviation by maximum likelihood divides by n, which a note says. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Data outside the distribution's range, no spread, and a negative binomial with variance not above the mean are refused; a note when the search did not settle. | [Venables & Ripley 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-21706-2); [Delignette-Muller & Dutang 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v064.i04) |
| `table.fit_distribution_to()` `distribution="gev"` | The GEV fitted by maximum likelihood with SciPy (genextreme, then a numerical polish), the shape stored as xi. | distribution="gumbel" for xi = 0; "generalised pareto" for the amounts above a threshold. | **P3.** Maximum likelihood is the standard fit of the GEV (Coles 2001, 3.3); the xi sign follows Coles and extRemes, while SciPy's genextreme takes c = -xi, so simplydata turns it round and says so. *Basis: convention.* | [Coles 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-3675-0) |
| `table.fit_distribution_to()` `distribution="generalised pareto"` | The generalised Pareto curve fitted by maximum likelihood with SciPy (genpareto with the threshold at 0), the shape as xi. | Subtract the threshold first; find_return_levels_of(values_above=) does the whole peaks-over-threshold analysis. | **P3.** Maximum likelihood is the standard fit of the generalised Pareto curve to the amounts above a threshold (Davison & Smith 1990; Coles 2001, 4.3); SciPy's genpareto and extRemes share the xi sign. *Basis: convention.* | [Davison & Smith 1990](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1990.tb01796.x); [Coles 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-3675-0) |
| `table.fit_distribution_to()` `censored_by=` | The rows where censored_by= says yes are right-censored (the value is only a lower limit): maximum likelihood adds the log density of each value seen and the log chance above of each censored one, as SciPy fit(CensoredData) and R fitdistrplus::fitdistcens(); no location for the Weibull, as R survival::survreg(). | fixed= to hold parameters; leave censored_by= out to treat every value as seen. | **P3.** This is the standard likelihood for right-censored data (Kalbfleisch & Prentice 2002); simplydata polishes SciPy's search so the answers agree with R to about 5 digits. **Checks:** A yes/no column; at least 2 values that are not censored; smooth distributions only; no standard errors (no package simplydata uses gives them for a censored fit). | [Kalbfleisch & Prentice 2002](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118032985); [Delignette-Muller & Dutang 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v064.i04) |
| `table.compare_distributions_for()` | Normal, lognormal, gamma and Weibull, ranked by AIC, with the goodness-of-fit statistics of fitdistrplus::gofstat (no p-values). | distributions= your own list. | **P2.** AIC compares models fitted to the same data (Akaike 1974; Burnham & Anderson 2002); a note says a difference below 2 is too small to choose. **Checks:** Count and continuous families are not mixed; a family that fails is left out, with a note. | [Akaike 1974](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAC.1974.1100705); [Burnham & Anderson 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97636); [Delignette-Muller & Dutang 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v064.i04) |
| `table.compare_distributions_for()` `show="wasserstein distance"` | One row per pair of groups, in sorted order (A and B first), each the area between the groups' cumulative shares (SciPy wasserstein_distance, as R transport::wasserstein1d()); a list of columns uses wasserstein_distance_nd. | show="energy distance"; leave show= out to fit and rank distributions. | **P5.** Every pair is shown, in the order the groups sort, so the table is the same each time. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 2 groups; needs for_each_group_in=; not with distributions=. |  |
| `table.compare_distributions_for()` `show="energy distance"` | Székely's energy distance (SciPy energy_distance) for each pair of groups in sorted order: A and B, A and C, then B and C. | show="wasserstein distance" (also for a list of columns). | **P5.** SciPy's energy distance is the square root of Székely & Rizzo's energy statistic; the pairs in sorted order make the table the same each time. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One column; at least 2 groups; needs for_each_group_in=. | [Székely & Rizzo 2013](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jspi.2013.03.018) |
| `numbers.fit_distribution()` | The same fit as table.fit_distribution_to. | As table.fit_distribution_to. | **P3.** As table.fit_distribution_to. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.fit_distribution_to. | [Venables & Ripley 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-21706-2); [Delignette-Muller & Dutang 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v064.i04) |
| `distribution.find_chance_that_value()` | The chance above a value is the survival function sf(), not 1 - cdf(). | is_below=, is_between=. | **P3.** 1 - cdf() loses all its digits far out in the tail; sf() keeps them. This is numerical care, not a statistical choice. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `distribution.find_density_at()` | The height of the SciPy density (pdf) at the value, with the parameters under R's names (shape, rate) turned into SciPy's (a, scale = 1 / rate). | A list of values; distribution.find_chance_that_value() for a chance. | **P3.** R's names are the ones most textbooks and papers use; turning them into SciPy's is bookkeeping, not a statistical choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Count distributions are refused: they have no density. |  |
| `distribution.find_percentile()` | The value with that percent below it; for counts, the smallest count with at least that share at or below it. | find_cut_off() and find_middle_range() for the other questions. | **P3.** As R's q functions (qnorm, qbinom ...): the answer is written with the same call in R. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `distribution.find_cut_off()` | The value with a chance above it is SciPy's inverse survival function isf(), not ppf(1 - chance); the value with a chance below it is ppf(). | with_chance_below=; a list of chances. | **P3.** 1 - chance loses digits when the chance is tiny; isf() keeps them. This is numerical care, not a statistical choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Chances outside 0 to 1 are refused, with find_percentile() offered for percents. |  |
| `distribution.find_middle_range()` | The middle percent with equal tails: half of the rest is cut off at each end (ppf() of it for the lower end, the same share from the top for the upper end). | Any percent above 0 and below 100. | **P3.** Equal tails are the usual way to give a middle range (R qnorm(c(0.025, 0.975)) gives the same); the shortest range would differ for skewed distributions. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A percent of 0, 100 or outside them is refused. |  |
| `distribution.find_average()` | The mean of the SciPy distribution, with R's parameter names turned into SciPy's. | distribution.show_summary() for every summary at once. | **P3.** The average of a distribution is a fact of the distribution; only the names need care. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A distribution with no finite average (heavy tails) gets a sentence that says so. |  |
| `distribution.find_median()` | The median of the SciPy distribution (its 50 % point), with R's parameter names turned into SciPy's. | distribution.find_percentile(50) gives the same. | **P3.** A fact of the distribution; only the names need care. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `distribution.find_standard_deviation()` | The standard deviation of the SciPy distribution itself (no sample, so no n - 1), with R's parameter names turned into SciPy's. | distribution.find_variance() for its square. | **P3.** A fact of the distribution; only the names need care. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A distribution with no finite spread gets a sentence that says so. |  |
| `distribution.find_variance()` | The variance of the SciPy distribution, with R's parameter names turned into SciPy's. | distribution.find_standard_deviation() for its square root. | **P3.** A fact of the distribution; only the names need care. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A distribution with no finite variance gets a sentence that says so. |  |
| `distribution.show_summary()` | One row: average, median, standard deviation, variance, skewness, excess kurtosis (0 for the normal distribution), entropy in nats and the lowest and highest possible values, all from SciPy. | The single distribution words for one number each. | **P3.** Excess kurtosis is the form SciPy and most software give, so 0 means as heavy-tailed as the normal distribution. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** An entropy SciPy cannot work out is left empty. |  |
| `distribution.make_random_numbers()` | NumPy's generator (PCG64) seeded with with_seed=1, so the same seed gives the same numbers. | with_seed= any whole number, or None for different numbers each time; rows= and columns= for a grid. | **P5.** A fixed seed makes every example and analysis repeatable; the printout says R's generator differs, so R's numbers will not match. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `distribution.draw_chart()` | The density curve from the 0.1 % to the 99.9 % point at 200 evenly spaced places; bars of each chance for count distributions. | show="cumulative"; between= for another range. | **P5.** Showing results: the middle 99.8 % holds the whole visible curve without long flat tails. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `distribution.turn_into_table()` | One row: the distribution's name and its parameters under their plain names; parameters not given are left out; a fitted distribution adds its standard errors, log-likelihood, AIC and BIC. | distribution.show_parameters() for a dictionary. | **P5.** Bookkeeping, not a method: the plain names are the ones the user typed. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `distribution.take_function()` | The change of variables: the chances of the new values come from the old distribution (exp of a normal is a lognormal), as SciPy stats.exp, stats.log and stats.abs. | name="log", "exp", "absolute value" or "order statistic" (with rank= and out_of=). | **P3.** The change of variables is exact, so no random numbers are needed. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A smooth distribution of one value; the log needs values above 0. |  |
| `distribution.take_function()` `"order statistic"` | rank=1 is the smallest of out_of= values drawn (SciPy stats.order_statistic's r= and n=). | rank=out_of for the biggest. | **P5.** Positions count from 1 everywhere in simplydata, as SciPy's r= also does. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Whole numbers with 1 <= rank <= out_of. |  |
| `sdata.find_parameter_that_gives()` | The one parameter left out is found with scipy.special's inverse function for it (stdtridf, bdtri, nrdtrisd ...); for counts chance_below= means fewer than at_value, as find_chance_that_value(is_below=). | chance_at_most=, chance_above= or chance_at_least=. | **P3.** The inverse functions of scipy.special (from the Cephes and CDFLIB libraries) solve for the parameter directly; "below" means the same in every simplydata word. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One chance between 0 and 1; exactly one parameter left out; at_value= a whole number for counts; no parameter giving that chance is refused, with a sentence. |  |

## Models

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.explain()` | A linear model by least squares (as R lm()), with t-based 95 % intervals, R squared and the F test. Group columns are compared with their first level in sorted order. The outcome family is never switched by itself. The estimates come from statsmodels' QR fit; their standard errors come from the QR factor as R's summary.lm() takes them, with chol2inv() (R's own method, done here with NumPy's QR and LAPACK's dpotri, the routine chol2inv calls; approved by Manish 2026-11-22), because statsmodels' inv(R'R) loses digits on nearly collinear columns (NIST Longley). | outcome= (yes or no, counts, positive numbers, ordered groups, shares ...), grouped_by=, effects=, robust_errors=, table_of_tests=, interval_by=, p_value_by= and the other options below. | **P4.** simplydata follows R's lm() so that every number matches what reviewers can check. It never chooses a model family from the data, because that choice belongs to the question asked; instead the printout checks the model and points to a better family. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Notes: residuals not bell-shaped; fewer than 10 rows per effect (a starting value: Austin & Steyerberg 2015 found about 2 per column enough for linear models); a variance inflation factor above 5 (a rule of thumb whose limits O'Brien 2007 discusses); a 0/1 outcome suggests outcome="yes or no". Refused: no more rows than values to estimate, columns that say the same thing, a group column with one level. | [O'Brien 2007](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11135-006-9018-6); [Austin & Steyerberg 2015](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclinepi.2014.12.014) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="yes or no"` | Logistic regression (as R glm(family = binomial)): odds ratios with Wald 95 % intervals (as R confint.default()) and z-tests, the model as a whole by a likelihood-ratio test; 'yes' chosen from the usual pairs (yes/no, true/false, 1/0), else the second value in sorted order, and printed. | interval_by="profile"; p_value_by="likelihood ratio"; show="risk ratios"; link="probit" or "cloglog". | **P3.** Wald intervals and z-tests are what R's summary() and confint.default() give. Profile-likelihood intervals and likelihood-ratio tests are more accurate with few rows or large effects (Venzon & Moolgavkar 1988; Hosmer, Lemeshow & Sturdivant 2013), and R's confint() now gives profile intervals; both are one option away. With few events per column the model is unreliable (Peduzzi et al. 1996; Vittinghoff & McCulloch 2007), and with separation the estimates do not exist (Albert & Anderson 1984): both are checked. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Notes: fewer than 10 events per effect; separation (R's 'fitted probabilities numerically 0 or 1'), with the advice to use fewer columns or merge groups; more than 10 % with the outcome, where an odds ratio is not a risk ratio (Zhang & Yu 1998). The fit is summarised by McFadden's, Nagelkerke's and Tjur's R squared (Nagelkerke 1991; Tjur 2009). | [Venzon & Moolgavkar 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2347496); [Hosmer et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118548387); [Peduzzi et al. 1996](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0895-4356(96)00236-3); [Vittinghoff & McCulloch 2007](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwk052); [Albert & Anderson 1984](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/71.1.1); [Zhang & Yu 1998](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.280.19.1690); [Nagelkerke 1991](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/78.3.691); [Tjur 2009](https://doi.org/10.1198/tast.2009.08210) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="counts"` | Poisson regression with a log link (as R glm(family = poisson)): rate ratios with Wald intervals (as R confint.default()); time= adds the log of the time as an offset. | outcome="counts that vary a lot" (negative binomial), "counts (overdispersed)" (quasi-Poisson), "counts with many zeros"; link=; interval_by="profile". | **P4.** Counts are best modelled as counts, not log-transformed (O'Hara & Kotze 2010); overdispersion must be checked before trusting the intervals (Zuur, Ieno & Elphick 2010). Profile intervals are more accurate than Wald intervals with few rows (Venzon & Moolgavkar 1988) and are one option away. **Checks:** Non-whole or negative counts are refused; when the residual deviance is more than 1.5 times its degrees of freedom (a starting value; the Pearson statistic is the more usual measure), a note says the intervals are too narrow and names the other families (never a silent switch). | [O'Hara & Kotze 2010](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2041-210X.2010.00021.x); [Zuur et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2041-210X.2009.00001.x); [Ver Hoef & Boveng 2007](https://doi.org/10.1890/07-0043.1); [Venzon & Moolgavkar 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2347496) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="counts that vary a lot"` | Negative binomial regression (NB2, as R MASS::glm.nb()), theta by maximum likelihood. | outcome="counts (overdispersed)" for quasi-Poisson. | **P3.** Negative binomial and quasi-Poisson weight the counts differently; Ver Hoef & Boveng 2007 show how to choose. glm.nb is the reference implementation (Venables & Ripley 2002). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** When theta grows without limit (no extra spread), the fit is refused and outcome="counts" suggested. | [Ver Hoef & Boveng 2007](https://doi.org/10.1890/07-0043.1); [Venables & Ripley 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-21706-2) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="counts (overdispersed)"` | Quasi-Poisson with the Pearson dispersion, t-tests (as R summary(glm(family = quasipoisson))) and Wald intervals. | outcome="counts that vary a lot". | **P3.** The quasi-likelihood dispersion is estimated from Pearson's statistic (Wedderburn 1974; McCullagh & Nelder 1989), as R does. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** There is no AIC (no likelihood), which is said. | [Wedderburn 1974](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/61.3.439); [McCullagh & Nelder 1989](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4899-3242-6); [Ver Hoef & Boveng 2007](https://doi.org/10.1890/07-0043.1) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="positive numbers"` | Gamma regression with a log link (as R glm(family = Gamma(link = "log"))), Pearson dispersion, t-tests. | link="inverse" (R's own default for Gamma) or "identity". | **P5.** The log link gives ratios, which are easier to read, and keeps predictions positive (McCullagh & Nelder 1989); choosing it over R's inverse link is simplydata's own choice. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Values at or below 0 are refused. | [McCullagh & Nelder 1989](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4899-3242-6) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="yes or no (overdispersed)"` | Quasi-binomial, as R glm(family = quasibinomial): the shares, with the tries as weights and the Pearson dispersion. | outcome="yes or no" with out_of=. | **P3.** Wedderburn's quasi-likelihood with the Pearson dispersion is R's quasibinomial; a two-column outcome in statsmodels would give another dispersion. *Basis: convention.* | [Wedderburn 1974](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/61.3.439); [McCullagh & Nelder 1989](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4899-3242-6) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="ordered groups"` | The proportional-odds model with a logit link (as R MASS::polr()). | outcome="groups" for groups with no order. | **P3.** McCullagh's proportional-odds model is the standard model for ordered categories (McCullagh 1980). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 3 groups are refused; text needs in_order=. | [McCullagh 1980](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1980.tb01109.x) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="groups"` | Multinomial logistic regression, each group against the first in sorted order (as R nnet::multinom()). | in_order= to choose the first group. | **P3.** The baseline-category logit model is the usual model for unordered outcomes; R's first level is the baseline. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="shares"` | Beta regression with a logit link and one precision (R betareg). | There is no other family for shares here. | **P2.** Beta regression models shares between 0 and 1 directly (Ferrari & Cribari-Neto 2004). **Checks:** Values outside (0, 1) are refused, with a hint to divide percents by 100. | [Ferrari & Cribari-Neto 2004](https://doi.org/10.1080/0266476042000214501) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="counts with many zeros that vary a lot"` | Zero-inflated negative binomial with a logistic zero part and the same columns in both parts (pscl::zeroinfl(dist = "negbin")'s defaults). | zero_part_using= other columns for the zero part; outcome="counts with many zeros" (Poisson). | **P3.** Lambert 1992 introduced zero-inflated models; pscl is their R reference (Zeileis, Kleiber & Jackman 2008). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Counts with no zeros are refused (use the plain count outcomes). | [Lambert 1992](https://doi.org/10.2307/1269547); [Zeileis et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v027.i08) |
| `table.explain()` `outcome="counts in two steps"` | A hurdle model: a logistic part for zero or not, and a Poisson part cut off at 1 for the counts above 0 (pscl::hurdle()). | outcome="counts in two steps that vary a lot" (negative binomial counts); zero_part_using=. | **P3.** Mullahy 1986's two-part model; pscl's binomial zero part is the textbook form (Zeileis et al. 2008), where statsmodels' default zero part is Poisson. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** All zeros or no zeros are refused. | [Mullahy 1986](https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-4076(86)90002-3); [Zeileis et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v027.i08) |
| `table.explain()` `zero_part_using=` | The zero part gets its own columns (pscl's y ~ x \| z); without the option both parts use the columns of using=. | Leave it out for the same columns in both parts. | **P3.** Both forms are pscl's (Zeileis, Kleiber & Jackman 2008). *Basis: convention.* | [Zeileis et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v027.i08) |
| `table.explain()` `weights=` | Precision weights, as R lm(weights =) (statsmodels WLS, or GLM var_weights); rows with weight 0 are left out, as R does. | There is no frequency-weight option. | **P3.** Precision weights are what R's weights= means; frequency weights would change the degrees of freedom. The printout says which kind was used. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Negative weights and text columns are refused. |  |
| `table.explain()` `matched_by=` | Conditional logistic regression with the exact conditional likelihood (as R survival::clogit(), method = "exact"), fitted by Newton's method to the end. | There is no other method here. | **P3.** Matched sets must be compared within themselves; the exact conditional likelihood is clogit's default (Gail, Lubin & Rubinstein 1981). statsmodels' default optimiser stops a little short, so Newton's method finishes. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Sets without both a yes and a no are dropped (with a note); fewer than 2 such sets are refused. | [Gail et al. 1981](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/68.3.703) |
| `table.explain()` `show="risk ratios"` | Modified Poisson regression (Zou 2004): a Poisson model for the 0/1 column with robust (HC0) standard errors. | test_method="log-binomial". | **P2.** Odds ratios overstate risk ratios when the outcome is common (Zhang & Yu 1998). Modified Poisson gives the risk ratio and always converges (Zou 2004); the log-binomial model often fails to (McNutt et al. 2003). **Checks:** Only for yes/no outcomes. | [Zou 2004](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwh090); [McNutt et al. 2003](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwg074); [Zhang & Yu 1998](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.280.19.1690) |
| `table.explain()` `test_method="log-binomial"` | A binomial model with a log link, as R glm(family = binomial(link = "log")). | show="risk ratios" alone (modified Poisson). | **P4.** The log-binomial model gives risk ratios directly but often does not converge (McNutt et al. 2003). **Checks:** When it does not converge or a predicted chance reaches 1, simplydata says so and points to modified Poisson. | [McNutt et al. 2003](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwg074); [Zou 2004](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwh090) |
| `table.explain()` `giving_outliers_less_weight=` | Huber M-estimation with k = 1.345, the median absolute deviation as scale, Huber's H1 covariance (R MASS::rlm()'s defaults). | There is no MM-estimation here. | **P3.** Huber's estimator gives large residuals less weight (Huber 1964); k = 1.345 is the default of MASS::rlm and statsmodels (Venables & Ripley 2002). It does not resist rows with extreme x, which the printout says. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Numbers outcomes only; the down-weighted rows are listed. | [Huber 1964](https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177703732); [Venables & Ripley 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-21706-2) |
| `table.explain()` `with_knots=` | A natural cubic spline with its inner knots at the quantiles of all the values, as R splines::ns() places them, without its constant; the whole-curve test asks 'is it flat?'. | with_knots= 1 to 10; curve_for= for a smooth chosen by the data. | **P3.** Natural splines are the standard way to let a number column bend inside a model (Perperoglou et al. 2019). simplydata places the knots as R ns() does; Harrell 2015 places restricted cubic spline knots at fixed quantiles instead (for example 5, 35, 65 and 95 %). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Too many repeated values for separate knots are refused. | [Perperoglou et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12874-019-0666-3); [Harrell 2015](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-19425-7) |
| `table.explain()` `show="standardised effects"` | The model fitted again on z-scores (standard deviation with n - 1) of the number columns, and of the outcome when it is a number (as R effectsize::standardize_parameters(method = "refit")). | Leave show= out for effects in the columns' own units. | **P3.** Standardised effects depend on this sample's spread and are not measures of importance (Greenland, Schlesselman & Criqui 1986), which the printout says; Gelman 2008 discusses scaling by two standard deviations instead. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column with no spread is refused. | [Greenland et al. 1986](https://doi.org/10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a114229); [Gelman 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.3107) |
| `table.explain()` `interval_by="bootstrap"` | Rows drawn again whole (case resampling), the same model fitted to each, 9999 times (seed 1), BCa intervals (as R boot::boot.ci(type = "bca")). | interval_by="profile"; resamples=, with_seed=. | **P2.** Case resampling keeps each row's columns together (Davison & Hinkley 1997); BCa intervals correct for bias and skew (Efron 1987). **Checks:** A resample that loses a rare group is refused (merge it, or use profile intervals); below 20 rows a note. | [Davison & Hinkley 1997](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511802843); [Efron 1987](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1987.10478410) |
| `table.explain()` `interval_by="profile"` | Profile-likelihood intervals, R's confint() of a glm. | interval_by="wald" (the default) or "bootstrap". | **P2.** Profile intervals are safer than Wald intervals with few rows or effects far from 1 (Venzon & Moolgavkar 1988); since R 4.4 they are what confint() gives for a glm. **Checks:** Yes/no and count outcomes only. | [Venzon & Moolgavkar 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2347496) |
| `table.explain()` `p_value_by="likelihood ratio"` | Each column left out in turn and the deviances compared (chi-square, 1 df), as R drop1(m, test = "LRT"). | Leave it out for Wald z-tests. | **P2.** Likelihood-ratio tests behave better than Wald tests in small samples and near the edge of the parameter space (Hosmer, Lemeshow & Sturdivant 2013 in the logistic case). **Checks:** Yes/no and count outcomes only; not with grouped_by=. | [Hosmer et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118548387) |
| `table.explain()` `robust_errors=` | HC3 standard errors (statsmodels for linear models, R sandwich::vcovHC(type = "HC3") for the others). | Leave it out for the model's own standard errors; effects="for the whole population" for grouped rows. | **P2.** HC3 keeps its error rate in small samples better than White's original HC0 (MacKinnon & White 1985; Long & Ervin 2000, who recommend it below 250 rows). **Checks:** Refused with grouped_by= (GEE is suggested). | [White 1980](https://doi.org/10.2307/1912934); [MacKinnon & White 1985](https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-4076(85)90158-7); [Long & Ervin 2000](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.2000.10474549) |
| `table.explain()` `table_of_tests=` | No table unless asked; with table_of_tests="type 2" (the usual choice), each column tested after the others but before its interactions (as R car::Anova(type = 2)). | table_of_tests="type 3" (sum-to-zero coding done for you) or "in order" (type 1, as R anova()). | **P2.** Type II tests respect the principle of marginality and are more powerful than type III for unbalanced data (Langsrud 2003); type III needs sum-to-zero coding to mean anything, which simplydata sets. **Checks:** Not with grouped_by= and the newer outcome families. | [Langsrud 2003](https://doi.org/10.1023/A:1023260610025) |
| `table.explain()` `grouped_by=` | A linear mixed model with a random intercept for each group, fitted by REML (as R lme4::lmer()); Wald z-tests and Wald intervals. Yes/no and count outcomes go to R lme4::glmer() (Laplace approximation). | p_value_by="satterthwaite" (lmerTest t-tests); own_slope_for= (random slopes); crossed_with=; nested groups with a list. | **P5.** REML is the default of lme4 and nlme and corrects the downward bias of maximum likelihood in the variances (Harville 1977; Bates et al. 2015). lme4 itself prints no p-values: the Wald z-tests are simplydata's choice. They are anti-conservative with few groups, and Satterthwaite or Kenward-Roger degrees of freedom keep the error rate near 5 % (Luke 2017), which p_value_by="satterthwaite" gives (lmerTest's own default). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Fewer than 5 groups get a note (poorly estimated; Harrison et al. 2018); groups at least as many as rows, fewer than 2 groups and other outcomes are refused; a singular fit and a fit that did not settle get notes. | [Harville 1977](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1977.10480998); [Bates et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v067.i01); [Luke 2017](https://doi.org/10.3758/s13428-016-0809-y); [Satterthwaite 1946](https://doi.org/10.2307/3002019); [Kenward & Roger 1997](https://doi.org/10.2307/2533558); [Harrison et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj.4794); [Bolker et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tree.2008.10.008) |
| `table.explain()` `crossed_with=` | Two groupings crossed with each other, (1 \| school) + (1 \| class), fitted by REML as lme4's default. | grouped_by= alone for one grouping. | **P2.** Leaving out a crossed grouping (subjects and items) inflates false positives (Baayen, Davidson & Bates 2008); lme4 fits crossed groupings directly (Bates et al. 2015). **Checks:** Fewer than 5 groups in a grouping get a note. | [Baayen et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jml.2007.12.005); [Bates et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v067.i01) |
| `table.explain()` `effects=` | With effects="for the whole population": GEE with exchangeable correlation and robust (sandwich) standard errors, as R geepack::geeglm(). | within_groups="in time order" (AR-1) or "independent"; leave effects= out for a mixed model. | **P2.** GEE answers questions about the population average, mixed models about a typical group (Liang & Zeger 1986; Hubbard et al. 2010). The sandwich standard errors are too small with few groups; Mancl & DeRouen 2001 give a bias-corrected estimator, which simplydata does not yet offer. **Checks:** Fewer than 20 groups (a starting value) get a note that the robust standard errors may be too small. | [Liang & Zeger 1986](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/73.1.13); [Hubbard et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1097/EDE.0b013e3181caeb90); [Mancl & DeRouen 2001](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.0006-341X.2001.00126.x) |
| `table.explain()` `empty_cells=` | With empty_cells="multiple imputation": 20 tables filled by chained equations (predictive mean matching with 5 donors, seed 1), each fitted and pooled by Rubin's rules with Barnard-Rubin degrees of freedom (as R mice). | with_seed=; leave it out to use complete rows only. | **P5.** Multiple imputation keeps the uncertainty of the filled cells (Rubin 1987; Barnard & Rubin 1999); predictive mean matching with 5 donors is mice's default (van Buuren & Groothuis-Oudshoorn 2011). 20 imputations is simplydata's starting value (mice's own default is 5); White, Royston & Wood 2011 suggest at least as many as the percentage of incomplete rows, which simplydata does not check. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Rows with gaps in text columns are left out, with a note; without gaps the ordinary fit is used; refused with grouped_by=, time=, out_of=. | [Rubin 1987](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316696); [Barnard & Rubin 1999](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/86.4.948); [van Buuren & Groothuis-Oudshoorn 2011](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v045.i03); [White et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.4067) |
| `table.explain()` `curve_for=` | A generalised additive model (R mgcv::gam(method = "REML")) with a thin-plate regression spline for each column named. | with_knots= for a natural spline with fixed knots. | **P2.** REML chooses the smoothness more reliably than GCV, which tends to under-smooth (Wood 2011). **Checks:** Columns with fewer than 10 different values are refused (mgcv's own limit). | [Wood 2011](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-9868.2010.00749.x) |
| `table.explain()` `for_percentile=` | Quantile regression by R quantreg's exact simplex (Barrodale-Roberts), with standard errors by "nid" for every number of rows. | for_percentile= 1 to 99. | **P5.** Quantile regression (Koenker & Bassett 1978) describes the edges of the data, not only the average. The "nid" sandwich (Hendricks & Koenker 1992) gives standard errors and p-values at any size, which is why simplydata uses it; quantreg's own default below 1001 rows, rank-inversion intervals, covers better in small samples (Kocherginsky, He & Mu 2005) but gives no p-values. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Numbers outcomes only; a note below 20 rows. | [Koenker & Bassett 1978](https://doi.org/10.2307/1913643); [Hendricks & Koenker 1992](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1992.10475175); [Koenker 2005](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511754098); [Kocherginsky et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1198/106186005X27563) |
| `table.explain()` `changes_at=` | An interrupted time series: a change of level and of slope at the point given (rows at the point count as after), with the lag-1 autocorrelation of the residuals. | changes_at= the point. | **P2.** Lopez Bernal, Cummins & Gasparrini 2017 model both changes and ask that autocorrelation be checked and, when it is large, allowed for; simplydata prints it but does not adjust. | [Lopez Bernal et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1093/ije/dyw098) |
| `table.explain()` `spread_changes_with=` | Generalised least squares fitted by REML (nlme's default): a group column gives each group its own spread (varIdent); a number column a spread that grows as a power of it (varPower). | Leave it out for one spread. | **P3.** These are nlme's variance functions (Pinheiro & Bates 2000); the model with and without them is compared. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Zeros with varPower, groups with fewer than 2 rows and other outcomes are refused. | [Pinheiro & Bates 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/b98882) |
| `table.explain()` `neighbours_alike_in_order_of=` | Evenly spaced values give AR(1) correlation (corAR1); uneven gaps give continuous-time AR(1) (corCAR1). | Leave it out for independent rows. | **P3.** corCAR1 allows for the size of each gap (Pinheiro & Bates 2000); which one was used is printed. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Repeated order values are refused; a correlation above 0.99 gets a note. | [Pinheiro & Bates 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/b98882) |
| `table.explain()` `through=` | Causal mediation analysis with statsmodels Mediation (Imai, Keele & Tingley's quasi-Bayesian algorithm, 1000 simulations, with_seed=1, as R mediation::mediate()): the indirect effect, the direct effect, the total effect and the share that goes through, averaged over the two exposure levels. | outcome="yes or no" (logistic models); against= the starting value of the exposure; allowing_for= the columns taken into account in both models. | **P2.** The counterfactual definition of the indirect effect holds for any model and states its assumption (sequential ignorability), unlike the Baron-Kenny steps (Imai, Keele & Tingley 2010); the quasi-Bayesian simulation is the default of the authors' own package (Tingley et al. 2014). **Checks:** using= must be one column with two values; through= cannot be the exposure or a column of allowing_for=; the assumptions are printed every time; a share is said to mean little when the total effect is not clear. | [Imai et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1037/a0020761); [Tingley et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v059.i05) |
| `table.explain()` `after use_survey_design()` | A survey-weighted model from R survey::svyglm(): gaussian for numbers, quasibinomial for yes or no, quasipoisson for counts, with survey's t tests and intervals on the model's residual degrees of freedom. | outcome= "numbers", "yes or no" or "counts". | **P1.** Design-based regression weights each row by its sampling weight and takes the standard errors from the design (Lumley 2010); the quasi families avoid the warnings that non-whole weights give in binomial and Poisson fits. **Checks:** Only column=, using= and outcome= are taken; yes or no outcomes need yes/no, 1/0 or True/False. | [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066) |
| `table.explain()` `test_method="bayesian"` | A Bayesian regression from R rstanarm::stan_glm() (numbers, yes or no, counts) with rstanarm's default weakly informative priors scaled to the data, 4 chains of 2000 iterations and seed = with_seed; each effect as the posterior median with its central 95 % credible interval; rank-normalised R-hat and bulk and tail effective draws from the posterior package. | grouped_by= for a mixed model (stan_glmer); leave out test_method= for the usual model. | **P1.** Weakly informative priors keep estimates stable without overruling the data (Gelman et al. 2008); the reporting guidelines ask for the priors, the convergence measures and the interval type to be stated (Kruschke 2021). The older R-hat misses some problems; the rank-normalised R-hat with the guide 1.01 and at least 400 effective draws are the current recommendation (Vehtari et al. 2021). 4 chains of 2000 iterations are rstanarm's defaults. **Checks:** Warnings come before any estimate when R-hat is above 1.01, there are fewer than 400 effective draws or any divergent transition, each with what to do; options other than outcome=, grouped_by= and with_seed= are refused. | [Gelman et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1214/08-AOAS191); [Vehtari et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1214/20-BA1221); [Kruschke 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-021-01177-7) |
| `table.explain()` `test_method="bayesian", grouped_by=` | A Bayesian mixed model from R rstanarm::stan_glmer() with its own starting level for each group, (1 \| group), and rstanarm's decov prior for the group spread; otherwise as explain(test_method="bayesian"). | leave out test_method= for lme4. | **P1.** As explain(test_method="bayesian"); every group's own level is checked for convergence too (Vehtari et al. 2021). **Checks:** One grouped_by= column. | [Vehtari et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1214/20-BA1221); [Kruschke 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-021-01177-7) |
| `table.add_predicted_column()` | No interval unless asked; for_new_rows=True gives a 95 % prediction interval; intervals of GLMs are worked out on the link scale. | interval=, for_new_rows=. | **P3.** Intervals on the link scale stay inside the possible range when turned back (McCullagh & Nelder 1989). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** for_new_rows= is refused for outcomes other than numbers. | [McCullagh & Nelder 1989](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4899-3242-6) |
| `table.find_model_averages_of()` | Model averages from R emmeans on the response scale, other number columns at their average and groups counted equally; all pairs compared with Tukey's adjustment; for mixed models the degrees of freedom emmeans chooses (Kenward-Roger), printed. | comparing=, within_each_group_in=, over_range_of=. | **P3.** Estimated marginal means (Searle, Speed & Milliken 1980) compare groups at the same values of the other columns; emmeans is the R reference (Lenth). Comparisons do not need a significant overall test first. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column inside an interaction gets a note; a number column for for_each_group_in= is refused. | [Searle et al. 1980](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1980.10483031); [Lenth (emmeans)](https://CRAN.R-project.org/package=emmeans); [Kenward & Roger 1997](https://doi.org/10.2307/2533558); [Kramer 1956](https://doi.org/10.2307/3001469) |
| `table.find_model_averages_of()` `comparing="each with the first"` | Each group against the first group (in sorted order, or the first of in_order=), with emmeans' own adjustment for this method (dunnettx), printed. | comparing="each pair", "each with the next", "each with the average", "trend". | **P3.** Comparing with one control is Dunnett's problem (Dunnett 1955); emmeans' dunnettx is its approximation. *Basis: convention.* | [Dunnett 1955](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1955.10501294); [Lenth (emmeans)](https://CRAN.R-project.org/package=emmeans) |
| `table.find_model_averages_of()` `within_each_group_in=` | The interaction test first (emmeans joint_tests()), then the comparisons within each group. | Leave it out for the overall comparisons. | **P2.** Groups must be compared with the interaction test, not by which simple effects have small p-values: the difference between significant and not significant is not itself significant (Gelman & Stern 2006). | [Gelman & Stern 2006](https://doi.org/10.1198/000313006X152649) |
| `table.find_model_averages_of()` `over_range_of=` | 50 evenly spaced values from the smallest to the largest; the other columns as emmeans holds them. | over_range_of= another column. | **P5.** 50 points make a smooth line; a starting value, not a published rule. *Basis: starting value.* | [Lenth (emmeans)](https://CRAN.R-project.org/package=emmeans) |
| `table.check_model_of()` | The four pictures of R plot(lm): residuals against fitted values, Q-Q, scale-location, leverage with Cook's distance contours at 0.5 and 1; Breusch-Pagan (Koenker's studentised form) and White's tests as notes; for yes/no outcomes calibration in tenths, Hosmer-Lemeshow (10 groups) and the Brier score. | There are no options for the pictures. | **P2.** Model checks are shown before they are tested: tests flag harmless departures in large samples, and the Hosmer-Lemeshow test depends on its groups (Hosmer et al. 1997); calibration is best shown (Van Calster et al. 2019). Koenker's form of the Breusch-Pagan test does not assume bell-shaped errors (Koenker 1981). **Checks:** Rows with Cook's distance above 0.5 get a note; a large p-value is said not to prove a good fit. | [Cook 1977](https://doi.org/10.2307/1268249); [Breusch & Pagan 1979](https://doi.org/10.2307/1911963); [Koenker 1981](https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-4076(81)90062-2); [White 1980](https://doi.org/10.2307/1912934); [Hosmer et al. 1997](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19970515)16:9<965::AID-SIM509>3.0.CO;2-O); [Van Calster et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12916-019-1466-7) |
| `table.check_model_of()` `test_method="bayesian"` | The posterior predictive check: the data against 50 sets made up by the fitted model (rstanarm posterior_predict()), as densities for numbers (R stats::density(), as bayesplot::ppc_dens_overlay) and as bars with the median and 90 % interval of the made-up counts for yes/no and counts (as bayesplot::ppc_bars). | show="prior check" for the prior predictive check (prior_PD = TRUE). | **P2.** Pictures of the data against data made up by the model show what a model misses better than one number, and the prior predictive check shows whether a prior allows impossible values (Gabry et al. 2019). 50 sets is what bayesplot's pp_check() draws by default. **Checks:** The same convergence warnings as explain(test_method="bayesian"). | [Gabry et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1111/rssa.12378) |
| `table.check_model_of()` `show="numbers"` | The numbers check_model_of prints, as a table: variance inflation factors, Koenker's Breusch-Pagan test and White's test (least squares), the Brier score and the Hosmer-Lemeshow test (yes or no), the number of rows with a Cook's distance above 0.5; with test_method="bayesian" R-hat, effective draws and the posterior predictive check. | leave out show= for the pictures; show="prior check" with test_method="bayesian". | **P5.** The same calls as the printout (statsmodels het_breuschpagan(robust=True), het_white, variance_inflation_factor; R car::vif, performance::performance_hosmer, posterior::summarise_draws), so the table and the printed numbers agree. Koenker's form does not assume bell-shaped errors (Koenker 1981); the pictures stay the way to judge a model (Hosmer et al. 1997). **Checks:** A mixed model (grouped_by=) is refused: its check is pictures only. | [Koenker 1981](https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-4076(81)90062-2); [White 1980](https://doi.org/10.2307/1912934); [Hosmer et al. 1997](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19970515)16:9<965::AID-SIM509>3.0.CO;2-O); [Vehtari et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1214/20-BA1221) |
| `table.show_rows_that_pull_model()` | Rows with Cook's distance above 4/n, biggest first (at most 10), with leverage and residuals; for mixed models groups above 4/k. | There is no option for the cut-off. | **P5.** Cook's distance measures how much a row moves the fit (Cook 1977); 4/n is a rule of thumb (Bollen & Jackman 1985), not a test, and the printout says to remove a row only for a reason. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Above 1 the row is said to pull very strongly. | [Cook 1977](https://doi.org/10.2307/1268249); [Bollen & Jackman 1985](https://doi.org/10.1177/0049124185013004004) |
| `table.compare_models()` | Nested models tested as R anova(m0, m1): the F test for linear models, quasi-Poisson and Gamma; the likelihood-ratio test for the others; mixed models refitted by maximum likelihood first; AIC and BIC as R logLik. | using= a list of models for an AIC table; check_on_left_out_rows=True. | **P3.** Comparing nested models by F or likelihood-ratio tests is standard; models fitted by REML must be refitted by maximum likelihood before their fixed effects are compared (Pinheiro & Bates 2000). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Models that are not nested get AIC only (note); an AIC difference below 2 gets a note; identical models are refused. | [Pinheiro & Bates 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/b98882); [Akaike 1974](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAC.1974.1100705) |
| `table.compare_models()` `using=[[...], [...]]` | AICc when there are fewer than 40 rows per value estimated, else AIC, with Akaike weights; every value estimated counts, the residual spread too, as R logLik(). | There is no BIC table. | **P2.** Burnham & Anderson 2002 recommend AICc, and say it matters when n / K is below about 40 (Hurvich & Tsai 1989); counting the residual spread as R does keeps the numbers equal to R's. **Checks:** Quasi-Poisson models (no likelihood) and duplicate models are refused. | [Burnham & Anderson 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97636); [Hurvich & Tsai 1989](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/76.2.297) |
| `table.compare_models()` `check_on_left_out_rows=True` | 10-fold cross-validation (R boot::cv.glm(K = 10)), seed 1 so the parts repeat, the same parts for every model. | with_seed=. | **P2.** Ten parts balance bias and variance better than leaving out one row at a time, which is cv.glm's own default (Breiman & Spector 1992; Hastie, Tibshirani & Friedman 2009). **Checks:** Fewer than 20 rows, grouped_by= and other outcomes are refused. | [Breiman & Spector 1992](https://doi.org/10.2307/1403680); [Hastie et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-84858-7); [Davison & Hinkley 1997](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511802843) |
| `table.find_columns_that_predict()` | The lasso (R glmnet, alpha = 1), its penalty by 10-fold cross-validation (seed 1), the largest penalty within one standard error of the best (lambda.1se); columns standardised; no p-values. | test_method="elastic net" (alpha = 0.5); outcome="yes or no" or "counts". | **P3.** The one-standard-error rule, which its authors call a rule of thumb, picks a simpler model that predicts about as well (Hastie, Tibshirani & Friedman 2009; Friedman, Hastie & Tibshirani 2010). Ordinary p-values are not valid after selection, so none are printed (Whittingham et al. 2006; Berk et al. 2013). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Ridge (it keeps every column), fewer than 2 columns and fewer than 20 rows are refused. | [Tibshirani 1996](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1996.tb02080.x); [Friedman et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v033.i01); [Hastie et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-84858-7); [Whittingham et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1365-2656.2006.01141.x); [Berk et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1214/12-AOS1077) |
| `table.find_group_levels_of()` | Each group's level from R lme4 ranef(condVar = TRUE), with level plus and minus 1.96 conditional standard deviations. | There is no other method here. | **P4.** Without condVar ranef() gives no uncertainty. The printout warns not to use these levels as data in later tests, which ignores their uncertainty (Houslay & Wilson 2017). **Checks:** Needs grouped_by=; nested groups and other outcomes are refused. | [Houslay & Wilson 2017](https://doi.org/10.1093/beheco/arx023); [Bates et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v067.i01) |

## Patterns across columns

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.add_principal_components()` | Principal components of the columns on the same scale (the correlation matrix, as R prcomp(scale. = TRUE)), 2 components, each turned so its biggest weight is positive. | same_scale=False (the covariance matrix); how_many=. | **P2.** When columns are in different units, PCA of the unscaled columns is driven by the column with the biggest numbers; scaling is the standard advice (Jolliffe & Cadima 2016). Two components (for a picture) is simplydata's starting value. **Checks:** A column with no spread is refused when scaling; at least 2 columns and 3 rows. | [Jolliffe & Cadima 2016](https://doi.org/10.1098/rsta.2015.0202) |
| `table.find_principal_components_of()` | The same principal components as add_principal_components (correlation matrix). | same_scale=False. | **P2.** As add_principal_components (Jolliffe & Cadima 2016). **Checks:** As add_principal_components. | [Jolliffe & Cadima 2016](https://doi.org/10.1098/rsta.2015.0202) |
| `table.add_clusters()` | k-means (R stats::kmeans, Hartigan-Wong, 25 random starts, seed 1) with 3 clusters, on columns put on the same scale. | how_many=; test_method="hierarchical" (Ward's linkage, or average, complete, single); same_scale=False. | **P5.** Hartigan-Wong is R's default algorithm (Hartigan & Wong 1979); many starts avoid a poor local answer. The number of clusters is the user's question: 3 is a starting value, and silhouettes (Rousseeuw 1987) help choose it. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Fewer different rows than clusters are refused; tied heights in hierarchical clustering get a note. | [Hartigan & Wong 1979](https://doi.org/10.2307/2346830); [Ward 1963](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1963.10500845); [Rousseeuw 1987](https://doi.org/10.1016/0377-0427(87)90125-7) |
| `table.add_clusters()` `how_many="automatic"` | Average linkage on the distances, and the number of clusters (2 to 10) with the largest average silhouette width (R cluster::silhouette); on a tie, the fewer clusters. | how_many= a number. | **P5.** Choosing the number by the largest average silhouette is a common rule (Rousseeuw 1987; Kaufman & Rousseeuw 1990), not the winner of a method comparison (Milligan & Cooper 1985 found other rules better); 2 to 10 and 'fewer on a tie' are simplydata's starting values. Average linkage is chosen for Bray-Curtis distances, because Ward's method assumes straight-line distances (Ward 1963). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Fewer than 4 rows are refused. | [Rousseeuw 1987](https://doi.org/10.1016/0377-0427(87)90125-7); [Kaufman & Rousseeuw 1990](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316801); [Milligan & Cooper 1985](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02294245); [Ward 1963](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1963.10500845) |
| `table.add_clusters()` `measure=` | The counts as they are (no rescaling) for Bray-Curtis distances, with average linkage (Ward is refused with it), the same as draw_cluster_tree. | linkage="complete" or "single"; measure="euclidean". | **P3.** Ward's method minimises squared Euclidean distances (Ward 1963), so it does not fit Bray-Curtis itself; average linkage (UPGMA) is the common choice for ecological distances and usually keeps the distances best (highest cophenetic correlation; Borcard, Gillet & Legendre 2018). Ward on the square roots of Bray-Curtis distances is a valid alternative (Murtagh & Legendre 2014), not offered. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Ward with Bray-Curtis is refused. | [Ward 1963](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1963.10500845); [Borcard et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-71404-2); [Murtagh & Legendre 2014](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00357-014-9161-z) |
| `table.find_distances_between_rows()` | Bray-Curtis dissimilarity (vegan::vegdist's default), on the values as given. | measure="euclidean", "manhattan", "jaccard". | **P2.** Bray-Curtis ignores shared absences and was among the most robust measures of community difference in Faith, Minchin & Belbin 1987 (Bray & Curtis 1957); Euclidean distance on raw abundances is not. On raw abundances Bray-Curtis is dominated by the most abundant taxa, so a transformation first is often advised (Legendre & Gallagher 2001). **Checks:** Negative values and rows of zeros are refused for Bray-Curtis and Jaccard (Euclidean is suggested). | [Bray & Curtis 1957](https://doi.org/10.2307/1942268); [Faith et al. 1987](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00038687); [Legendre & Gallagher 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/s004420100716) |
| `table.find_distances_between_rows()` `measure="aitchison"` | With measure="aitchison": zeros replaced first (GBM, no column deleted), then the straight-line distance between the rows' clr log ratios. | measure="bray-curtis" (the default), "euclidean", "manhattan", "jaccard". | **P2.** As add_principal_coordinates(measure="aitchison") (Aitchison 1982; Gloor et al. 2017). **Checks:** As add_log_ratio_columns. | [Aitchison 1982](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1982.tb01195.x); [Gloor et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.3389/fmicb.2017.02224); [Martín-Fernández et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1177/1471082X14535524) |
| `table.find_distances_between_rows()` `measure="minkowski"` | power= is the p of the Minkowski distance (SciPy distance.cdist): 1 is Manhattan, 2 straight-line. | measure="euclidean" or "manhattan". | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** power= of 1 or more. |  |
| `table.find_distances_to_rows_of()` | The straight-line (Euclidean) distance (SciPy spatial.distance.cdist, metric="euclidean"). | test_method="manhattan", "chebyshev", "bray-curtis", "canberra" or "cosine". | **P3.** Straight-line distance is cdist's own default and the meaning of distance on a map. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Rows with an empty cell in the columns used are left out, with a note. |  |
| `table.add_nmds()` | Non-metric multidimensional scaling (vegan::metaMDS) on Bray-Curtis, 2 dimensions, 20 to 100 random starts (seed 1), and no automatic square-root or Wisconsin transformation (metaMDS's own default is to transform); the stress read with Clarke's guide. | dimensions=, tries=, measure=. | **P5.** NMDS keeps only the rank order of the distances (Kruskal 1964); Clarke 1993 gives the guide for reading the stress (below 0.05 excellent, 0.1 good, 0.2 usable). No hidden transformation and up to 100 starts are simplydata's own choices. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** All rows the same are refused; a note when the best answer was not found twice. | [Kruskal 1964](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02289565); [Clarke 1993](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1442-9993.1993.tb00438.x); [Faith et al. 1987](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00038687) |
| `table.compare_profiles_of()` | PERMANOVA (vegan::adonis2) on Bray-Curtis with 999 permutations (seed 1), followed by a test of the spread within groups (betadisper). | measure=, permutations=, with_seed=. | **P4.** PERMANOVA compares groups on any distance (Anderson 2001), but a difference in spread can look like a difference in place (Warton, Wright & Wang 2012), so the spread is tested too (Anderson 2006). **Checks:** Fewer than 2 groups or a group with 1 row are refused; when the spreads differ (p below 0.05), a note suggests looking at the NMDS. | [Anderson 2001](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1442-9993.2001.01070.pp.x); [Anderson 2006](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1541-0420.2005.00440.x); [Warton, Wright & Wang 2012](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2041-210X.2011.00127.x) |
| `table.compare_profiles_of()` `test_method="anosim"` | Clarke's ANOSIM R from ranked distances, 999 permutations after set.seed(1), with the spread check (betadisper). | Leave test_method= out for PERMANOVA. | **P3.** ANOSIM is Clarke's rank-based test (Clarke 1993). Like PERMANOVA it mixes a difference in spread with a difference in place, and ANOSIM more so (Anderson & Walsh 2013), so the spread check is printed (Anderson 2006). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As compare_profiles_of. | [Clarke 1993](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1442-9993.1993.tb00438.x); [Anderson & Walsh 2013](https://doi.org/10.1890/12-2010.1); [Anderson 2006](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1541-0420.2005.00440.x) |
| `table.compare_profiles_of()` `show="contributions"` | SIMPER: each column's average contribution to the Bray-Curtis difference between each pair of groups, without permutation p-values. | Leave show= out for the test. | **P2.** SIMPER ranks columns with large and variable counts high even when they do not differ, and its p-values mislead (Warton, Wright & Wang 2012); the table is a description, which the printout says. | [Warton, Wright & Wang 2012](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2041-210X.2011.00127.x) |
| `table.compare_profiles_of()` `measure="aitchison"` | Zeros replaced (GBM, no column deleted), then the Aitchison distance. | measure="bray-curtis" (the default). | **P2.** As add_principal_coordinates(measure="aitchison") (Aitchison 1982; Gloor et al. 2017). **Checks:** As add_log_ratio_columns. | [Aitchison 1982](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1982.tb01195.x); [Gloor et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.3389/fmicb.2017.02224) |
| `table.add_diversity_columns()` | Richness, Shannon with the natural log (as vegan, base e) and Simpson as 1 - the sum of squared shares (the Gini-Simpson index: the chance that two counts drawn at random are of different kinds). | show="inverse simpson", "chao1", "evenness", "hill numbers"; at_total_count= for rarefaction. | **P3.** These are vegan's definitions: Shannon's entropy (Shannon 1948) with the natural log, and 1 - the sum of squared shares, the Gini-Simpson form of Simpson's concentration (Simpson 1949 defined the concentration itself, drawing without replacement).
