The printout names the log base because scikit-bio used base 2 before version 0.6.1. Indices are not diversities: a 10 % drop in Shannon is not a 10 % drop in diversity, so the printout also gives the number of equally common kinds (Jost 2006). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Negative values are refused; a row of zeros gets empty cells and a note; when row totals differ more than two-fold, a note says richness depends on the number of counts and points to at_total_count=. | [Shannon 1948](https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1948.tb01338.x); [Simpson 1949](https://doi.org/10.1038/163688a0); [Jost 2006](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2006.0030-1299.14714.x) |
| `table.add_diversity_columns()` `at_total_count=` | Rarefaction as the expected richness in a sample of that many counts (Hurlbert 1971, vegan rarefy()), not a random subsample, so the answer is the same every time. | Leave it out for observed richness. | **P3.** The expected value (Hurlbert 1971) gives the same answer every time, where a random subsample would change from run to run; rarefying of any kind still discards the extra depth of deeper samples (McMurdie & Holmes 2014). Method comparisons prefer coverage-based standardisation for comparing richness (Chao & Jost 2012), which simplydata does not yet offer. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Non-whole numbers are refused (rarefaction needs raw counts); a row with fewer counts than asked gets an empty cell (vegan gives its observed richness), and the printout names it. | [Hurlbert 1971](https://doi.org/10.2307/1934145); [McMurdie & Holmes 2014](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pcbi.1003531); [Chao & Jost 2012](https://doi.org/10.1890/11-1952.1) |
| `table.add_diversity_columns()` `show="chao1"` | The bias-corrected Chao1 with its standard error, as vegan::estimateR(). | show="richness" for observed richness. | **P3.** Chao1 is a lower bound for the number of kinds, estimated from the kinds seen once or twice (Chao 1987); the bias-corrected form (Chao 2005) stays finite when no kind is seen twice. It is meaningless when singletons were removed (as some amplicon pipelines do), which simplydata does not check yet. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Non-whole numbers are refused: Chao1 needs raw counts. | [Chao 1987](https://doi.org/10.2307/2531532); [Chao 2005](https://doi.org/10.1002/0471667196.ess5051) |
| `table.add_diversity_columns()` `show="hill numbers"` | Hill numbers of order 0, 1 and 2 (richness, exp(Shannon), inverse Simpson). | show= the single indices. | **P2.** Hill numbers are numbers of equally common kinds, so a doubling means doubled diversity (Hill 1973; Jost 2006); comparing samples still needs the same depth or coverage, especially for orders 0 and 1 (Chao et al. 2014). | [Hill 1973a](https://doi.org/10.2307/1934352); [Jost 2006](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2006.0030-1299.14714.x); [Chao et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1890/13-0133.1) |
| `table.add_log_ratio_columns()` | The centred log ratio (clr, R compositions::clr()); zeros, when there are any, replaced first by zCompositions' Bayesian-multiplicative method (GBM), with no column deleted. | test_method="alr" (against= the reference) or "ilr"; zeros_replaced_by="adding 1". | **P2.** Counts that are shares of a total are compositional, and log ratios are the analysis that respects that (Aitchison 1982; Gloor et al. 2017). The Bayesian-multiplicative replacement estimates each zero from the data and keeps the ratios of the other parts (Martín-Fernández et al. 2015; Palarea-Albaladejo & Martín-Fernández 2015). cmultRepl's own default deletes columns that are more than 80 % zeros with only a message; keeping them is simplydata's choice, and their log ratios then rest mostly on replaced values (Lubbe, Filzmoser & Templ 2021 compare replacements when zeros are many). **Checks:** Negative values, a single column and rows of zeros are refused; with the multiplicative replacement, non-whole numbers are refused; a column with fewer than 2 values above 0 is refused, with the line that merges it with others. | [Aitchison 1982](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1982.tb01195.x); [Gloor et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.3389/fmicb.2017.02224); [Martín-Fernández et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1177/1471082X14535524); [Palarea-Albaladejo & Martín-Fernández 2015](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chemolab.2015.02.019); [Lubbe et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chemolab.2021.104248) |
| `table.add_log_ratio_columns()` `test_method="alr"` | Each part over the part named in against= (R compositions::alr(ivar =)). | test_method="clr" (the default) or "ilr". | **P3.** The additive log ratio needs a reference part, which you name (Aitchison 1982). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** against= is required. | [Aitchison 1982](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1982.tb01195.x) |
| `table.add_log_ratio_columns()` `test_method="ilr"` | Isometric log ratios with the default basis of R compositions::ilr(). | test_method="clr" (the default). | **P3.** ilr coordinates depend on the basis chosen (Egozcue et al. 2003): another basis (scikit-bio's) gives other numbers but the same distances between rows, which the printout says. *Basis: convention.* | [Egozcue et al. 2003](https://doi.org/10.1023/A:1023818214614) |
| `table.add_log_ratio_columns()` `zeros_replaced_by="adding 1"` | 1 added to every count before the logs (as vegan::decostand(pseudocount = 1)), only when there are zeros. | Leave it out for the Bayesian-multiplicative replacement. | **P3.** A pseudocount is simple but the answer depends on the number added, which the printout says; model-based replacement does not have that problem (Martín-Fernández et al. 2015). The 1 is simplydata's value. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Non-whole numbers are refused; ilr with adding 1 is refused. | [Martín-Fernández et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1177/1471082X14535524) |
| `table.add_principal_coordinates()` | Classical scaling (PCoA) of the Bray-Curtis distances on the counts as given (R stats::cmdscale(eig = TRUE)), 2 axes, each turned so its value of largest size is positive; the share of the first axis and the running total, out of the positive eigenvalues. | measure=, how_many=. | **P3.** Principal coordinates show any distance in a few axes (Gower 1966). Bray-Curtis is the convention for counts and held up well in simulated gradients (Faith et al. 1987); it is not a straight-line distance, so some eigenvalues are negative, and the printout says how many and how large. Turning the axes gives the same answer on every computer. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 3 rows are refused. | [Gower 1966](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/53.3-4.325); [Faith et al. 1987](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00038687) |
| `table.add_principal_coordinates()` `measure="aitchison"` | Zeros replaced first (GBM, no column deleted), then the Aitchison distance: the straight-line distance between the rows' clr log ratios. | measure="bray-curtis" (the default). | **P2.** The Aitchison distance is the distance of compositional data (Aitchison 1982; Gloor et al. 2017); vegan's vegdist refuses zeros for it, so simplydata replaces them as add_log_ratio_columns does. **Checks:** As add_log_ratio_columns. | [Aitchison 1982](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1982.tb01195.x); [Gloor et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.3389/fmicb.2017.02224); [Martín-Fernández et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1177/1471082X14535524) |
| `table.explain_profiles_of()` | Distance-based RDA on Bray-Curtis distances (vegan dbrda()), the same distance as compare_profiles_of; the adjusted R squared first; each column tested after all the others (by = "margin"); 999 permutations after set.seed(1). | measure=, test_method="canonical correspondence", allowing_for=, permutations=, with_seed=. | **P3.** db-RDA tests how columns explain differences in make-up on any distance (Legendre & Anderson 1999; vegan's dbrda follows McArdle & Anderson 2001, which keeps non-Euclidean distances without correction). The unadjusted R squared grows with every column, so the adjusted one is printed first (Peres-Neto et al. 2006). Each column is tested after all the others, so the order of using= does not matter; vegan's own default tests only the whole model, and by = "terms" would test in order. Marginal tests lose power when columns overlap and skip main effects that also appear in an interaction. Bray-Curtis and marginal tests are simplydata's choices. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** More columns than rows - 2 are refused; collinear columns get a note. | [Legendre & Anderson 1999](https://doi.org/10.1890/0012-9615(1999)069[0001:DBRATM]2.0.CO;2); [McArdle & Anderson 2001](https://doi.org/10.1890/0012-9658(2001)082[0290:FMMTCD]2.0.CO;2); [Peres-Neto et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1890/0012-9658(2006)87[2614:VPOSDM]2.0.CO;2) |
| `table.explain_profiles_of()` `measure="hellinger"` | RDA on the Hellinger-transformed counts (square roots of the shares), never on raw counts; each column tested after all the others. | measure="bray-curtis" (the default). | **P2.** Raw abundances give Euclidean-based RDA the problems of Euclidean distance; the Hellinger transformation reduces them, removing the double-zero problem and the dominance of abundant kinds (Legendre & Gallagher 2001). | [Legendre & Gallagher 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/s004420100716) |
| `table.explain_profiles_of()` `test_method="canonical correspondence"` | CCA on the counts themselves (chi-square distances), so no measure=; its adjusted R squared from permutations (vegan), after set.seed. | Leave test_method= out for db-RDA. | **P3.** Canonical correspondence analysis (ter Braak 1986) suits long gradients with unimodal responses. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Rows that add up to 0 are refused. | [ter Braak 1986](https://doi.org/10.2307/1938672) |
| `table.explain_profiles_of()` `allowing_for=` | A partial analysis: the column is taken into account first (vegan Condition()) and not tested. | Leave it out for no conditioning. | **P3.** This is partial RDA, as used in variation partitioning (Peres-Neto et al. 2006); allowing_for= has the same meaning on the other words. *Basis: convention.* | [Peres-Neto et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1890/0012-9658(2006)87[2614:VPOSDM]2.0.CO;2) |
| `table.find_correlation_between_profiles()` | The Pearson Mantel correlation between the two sets of distances, 999 permutations after set.seed(1); second= with 3 or more columns of whole numbers from 0 is read as counts and gets measure=, other measurements get straight-line distances after each column is put on the same scale. | test_method="procrustes"; measure=, permutations=, with_seed=. | **P3.** The Mantel test is the classic comparison of two distance matrices. It answers a question about distances, not about the columns themselves, which the printout says, and it has low power (Legendre, Fortin & Borcard 2015); Peres-Neto & Jackson 2001 found Procrustes more powerful, which test_method= gives. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Different rows in the two sets and fewer than 5 rows are refused. | [Legendre et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1111/2041-210X.12425); [Peres-Neto & Jackson 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/s004420100720) |
| `table.find_correlation_between_profiles()` `test_method="procrustes"` | PROTEST on the first two principal coordinates of each set: the Procrustes correlation and its permutation p-value. | Leave test_method= out for the Mantel test. | **P2.** Peres-Neto & Jackson 2001 found Procrustes analysis more powerful than the Mantel test for matching two data sets. Two axes per set is simplydata's starting value. **Checks:** As the Mantel test. | [Peres-Neto & Jackson 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/s004420100720) |
| `table.add_axes_that_separate_groups()` | Linear discriminant analysis (R MASS::lda()) with MASS's default prior chances, the group shares in the data; each axis turned so its biggest value is positive; leave-one-out classification. | how_many=. | **P3.** Fisher's discriminant axes (Fisher 1936) as MASS fits them (Venables & Ripley 2002); the prior is printed, because equal priors would classify differently. Leave-one-out accuracy is nearly unbiased, unlike in-sample accuracy, as long as the columns were not chosen on the same data. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 2 groups, a group with fewer than 2 rows, and no more rows - groups than columns (the pooled spread cannot be worked out) are refused; a group with no more rows than columns gets a note; a column constant within groups is refused with MASS's reason; how_many= is at most the smaller of groups - 1 and the number of columns. | [Fisher 1936](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1469-1809.1936.tb02137.x); [Venables & Ripley 2002](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-21706-2) |
| `table.add_correspondence_axes()` | Correspondence analysis as vegan's cca() with no explaining columns, row scores with scaling = "sites" (vegan's default is "species"); each axis turned so its biggest value is positive. | how_many=. | **P3.** Correspondence analysis (reciprocal averaging; Hill 1973b) fits counts with unimodal responses (ter Braak 1985); it weights rare kinds heavily (Legendre & Gallagher 2001). Site scaling keeps the chi-square distances between rows, which is why it is chosen for axes added to the rows. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Negative values, empty rows and columns of zeros are refused. | [Hill 1973b](https://doi.org/10.2307/2258931); [ter Braak 1985](https://doi.org/10.2307/2530959); [Legendre & Gallagher 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/s004420100716) |
| `table.add_correspondence_axes()` `show="numbers"` | The axes as a table instead of new columns: each row's number and coordinates, each axis's eigenvalue and share of the inertia (vegan summary(eigenvals())) and the total inertia (vegan tot.chi); for text columns (MCA) the shares and the total of the adjusted inertia (ca::mjca inertia.e and inertia.t). | leave out show= to add the columns CA1, CA2 to the table. | **P3.** Every number is the package's own, so it can be compared with a published table such as Oksanen's vegan tutorial (eigenvalues 0.5249 and 0.3568 of 2.083 on varespec). The MCA eigenvalues are not shown: R ca gives them only rounded (to 6 decimals), and simplydata does not work them out again (ter Braak 1985 for the eigenvalues of correspondence analysis). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The same checks as add_correspondence_axes. | [ter Braak 1985](https://doi.org/10.2307/2530959) |
| `table.find_hidden_factors_behind()` | Exploratory factor analysis by R psych::fa(): the number of factors by parallel analysis (psych fa.parallel(fa = "fa")), minimum residuals (minres) fitting and an oblimin rotation (through GPArotation), with R's random numbers seeded (with_seed=1); rows with any empty cell left out. | how_many= a number; test_method="maximum likelihood" or "principal axis"; rotation="varimax", "promax", "none". | **P2.** Parallel analysis keeps the factors whose eigenvalues stand out more than in random data of the same size (Horn 1965; here psych's common-factor variant, Humphreys & Ilgen 1969); the 'eigenvalue above 1' rule keeps too many (Zwick & Velicer 1986). Simulations disagree on whether the component or the factor variant is more accurate (Crawford et al. 2010; Lim & Jahng 2019), and no variant is reliable enough to treat the number as fixed, so try one factor fewer and one more; a different seed can change the number when it is borderline. Oblique rotations let factors correlate, as they often do, and are usually recommended over varimax (Fabrigar et al. 1999), though which rotation works best depends on the loading pattern (Sass & Schmitt 2010). Minres (Harman & Jones 1966), a least-squares fit, recovers weak factors better than maximum likelihood in simulations (Briggs & MacCallum 2003) and needs no bell shape; Fabrigar et al. 1999 prefer maximum likelihood for data close to normal, so this is a judgement. Minres and oblimin are psych::fa()'s defaults; choosing the number by parallel analysis is simplydata's choice (psych's default is one factor), and leaving out rows with empty cells too (psych would use pairwise correlations). The items are treated as numbers: for items with few categories, polychoric correlations are not yet offered. **Checks:** Needs at least 3 number columns that vary, and at least columns + 2 complete rows; rows with empty cells are left out, with a note; how_many= is at most the largest number of factors the columns can identify; refused when parallel analysis finds no factor. Notes for: KMO below 0.6 (a later convention; Kaiser rated only below 0.5 unacceptable); few rows for this many columns (how many are enough depends on how strongly the columns share factors: MacCallum et al. 1999); a factor with fewer than 3 loadings of 0.3 or more; a column loading on no factor or on two; Heywood cases (communality of 1 or more). | [Horn 1965](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02289447); [Humphreys & Ilgen 1969](https://doi.org/10.1177/001316446902900303); [Zwick & Velicer 1986](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.99.3.432); [Crawford et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1177/0013164410379332); [Lim & Jahng 2019](https://doi.org/10.1037/met0000230); [Fabrigar et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.4.3.272); [Sass & Schmitt 2010](https://doi.org/10.1080/00273170903504810); [Harman & Jones 1966](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02289468); [Briggs & MacCallum 2003](https://doi.org/10.1207/S15327906MBR3801_2); [Bernaards & Jennrich 2005](https://doi.org/10.1177/0013164404272507); [MacCallum et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.4.1.84) |
| `table.find_hidden_factors_behind()` `how_many=` | A number of factors chosen by you: no parallel analysis (the seed is still set, for the rotation). | how_many="automatic" (the default, parallel analysis). | **P3.** When theory fixes the number of factors, you name it; Fabrigar et al. 1999 advise also fitting one factor fewer and one more. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** how_many= must be a whole number from 1 to the largest number of factors the columns can identify. | [Fabrigar et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.4.3.272) |
| `table.find_hidden_factors_behind()` `test_method=` | With test_method="maximum likelihood": as base R factanal() fits; "principal axis" is psych's fm = "pa". | Leave test_method= out for minres. | **P3.** Maximum likelihood assumes roughly bell-shaped data and is preferred then (Fabrigar et al. 1999); it is more prone to improper solutions with small samples or weak factors, where least-squares methods (minres, principal axis) recover factors as well or better (Briggs & MacCallum 2003; de Winter & Dodou 2012). *Basis: convention.* | [Fabrigar et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.4.3.272); [Briggs & MacCallum 2003](https://doi.org/10.1207/S15327906MBR3801_2); [de Winter & Dodou 2012](https://doi.org/10.1080/02664763.2011.610445) |
| `table.find_hidden_factors_behind()` `rotation=` | With rotation="varimax" (as R factanal() uses): uncorrelated factors (Kaiser 1958); "promax" lets them correlate; "none" leaves them unrotated. | Leave rotation= out for oblimin. | **P3.** Varimax (Kaiser 1958) forces the factors to be uncorrelated, which psychological factors often are not (Fabrigar et al. 1999); it is offered to match older analyses. Unrotated, more than one factor is seldom interpretable. *Basis: convention.* | [Kaiser 1958](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02289233); [Fabrigar et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.4.3.272) |
| `table.check_hidden_factors()` | Confirmatory factor analysis by normal-theory maximum likelihood (R lavaan); with empty cells, missing = "ml" (full information maximum likelihood), which uses every value given. | formula= for a model in lavaan's own syntax. | **P2.** lavaan's default ("listwise") drops every row with an empty cell, reported only in its summary's row count. Full information maximum likelihood (lavaan's missing = "ml"; Rosseel 2012) uses every value given, and is unbiased and more efficient than listwise deletion when values are missing at random (Enders & Bandalos 2001; Schafer & Graham 2002); it assumes missing at random and roughly bell-shaped data. Robust standard errors (estimator MLR) and estimators for ordinal items are not yet offered. Fit guides are printed as guides, not pass marks (Hu & Bentler 1999; Marsh, Hau & Wen 2004). **Checks:** Needs measured_by= or formula=, not both; each factor needs at least 2 columns (3 advised); a factor name may not be a column name; columns must hold numbers and vary; rows with no value at all are left out, with a note; refused when lavaan does not converge or the model has negative degrees of freedom. Notes for: 0 degrees of freedom; a column measuring two factors; the 3 largest modification indices above 10, with a warning against fitting the model to the data. | [Rosseel 2012](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v048.i02); [Enders & Bandalos 2001](https://doi.org/10.1207/S15328007SEM0803_5); [Schafer & Graham 2002](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.7.2.147); [Hu & Bentler 1999](https://doi.org/10.1080/10705519909540118); [Marsh et al. 2004](https://doi.org/10.1207/s15328007sem1103_2) |
| `table.check_hidden_factors()` `formula=` | The model in lavaan's own syntax, run with lavaan::sem(), so hidden factors can explain each other (a structural equation model); paths shown standardised (lavaan's std.all, delta-method standard errors), as the loadings are. | measured_by= for a plain measurement model. | **P3.** lavaan's syntax is the most widely used way to write structural equation models in R (Rosseel 2012); sem() and cfa() have the same defaults, and sem() is used because the model may have paths. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A formula in fit_formula's style is refused; it needs at least one name =~ columns; every column must exist and hold numbers. | [Rosseel 2012](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v048.i02) |

## Directions, places and extremes

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.find_average_direction_of()` | The circular mean direction from 0 up to the full turn, the mean resultant length and the circular standard deviation sqrt(-2 ln R) (SciPy circmean, directional_stats, circstd); full_turn= has no default. | full_turn=24 (hours), 12 (months), 365 (days of the year) or "radians"; for_each_group_in= for groups. | **P1.** Directions must be averaged as unit vectors, not as numbers (Fisher 1993; Mardia & Jupp 1999): the plain average of 350° and 10° is 180°. The circular standard deviation sqrt(-2 ln R) is the standard one in both books and in R circular. full_turn= is required because R circular reads plain numbers as radians without a word, the trap the design checked. **Checks:** full_turn= must be given; values outside 0 to the full turn are read round the circle, with a note; a resultant length below 0.1 says there is no clear average direction. | [Fisher 1993](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511564345); [Mardia & Jupp 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316979) |
| `table.find_return_levels_of()` | A GEV curve fitted by maximum likelihood to block maxima (R extRemes::fevd), return levels with profile-likelihood 95 % intervals; the shape in the xi sign; no declustering. | values_above= for every value above a threshold (generalised Pareto); once_in= for other return periods. | **P1.** Coles (2001, 3.3.3) shows that normal-approximation intervals of long return levels are too narrow and too symmetric, and recommends the profile likelihood; maximum likelihood is extRemes's default (Gilleland & Katz 2016). The interval search range starts at the normal interval, because extRemes needs one. **Checks:** Daily (or any sub-yearly) rows refused for block maxima; fewer than 15 blocks noted; once_in= at or below 1 refused; a fit that does not converge noted. | [Coles 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-3675-0); [Gilleland & Katz 2016](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v072.i08) |
| `table.find_return_levels_of()` `values_above=` | Every value above the threshold, fitted with a generalised Pareto curve by maximum likelihood (R extRemes), the readings per year from the dates, the extremal index with a note below 0.7, and no silent declustering. | Leave values_above= out for yearly maxima; choose the threshold with chart.draw_threshold_check(). | **P1.** The peaks-over-threshold model (Davison & Smith 1990; Coles 2001, ch. 4) uses more of the data than yearly maxima. Values above a threshold often come in runs; the extremal index (Ferro & Segers 2003) says how much, and declustering is left to the user, because the run length is a choice. **Checks:** sorted_by= dates required, evenly spaced (daily, hourly, by the minute or monthly); empty readings refused; fewer than 10 values above refused and fewer than 30 noted. | [Davison & Smith 1990](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1990.tb01796.x); [Coles 2001](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-3675-0); [Ferro & Segers 2003](https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-9868.00401) |
| `table.find_variogram_of()` | R gstat's sample variogram (cutoff a third of the diagonal, 15 bins) and a spherical curve fitted by gstat's weighted least squares (fit.method = 7, weights pairs / distance^2) from gstat's own start values. | of_kind="exponential", "gaussian" or "matern"; between=[0, cutoff]. | **P3.** Weighted least squares with weights from the number of pairs and the distance follows Cressie (1985); the cutoff and bins are gstat's defaults (Pebesma 2004). The spherical curve is the usual first choice in gstat's own examples, not a published rule, so other curves are one option away; the range is said in words, because for the exponential, Gaussian and Matern curves gstat's range is not where the curve levels off. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Refused when gstat does not converge or the fit is singular; fewer than 30 places noted; shared places noted. | [Cressie 1985](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01032109); [Pebesma 2004](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cageo.2004.03.012) |
| `table.find_nearest_rows_to()` | The straight-line (Euclidean) distance in the columns given, nearest first (SciPy spatial.KDTree). | within= for every row up to a distance; how_many= for more rows. | **P5.** Straight-line distance is what "nearest" means on a map in metres; columns in other units should be put on one scale first. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Rows with an empty cell in the columns used are left out, with a note. |  |
| `table.find_fence_around()` | The convex hull from Qhull (SciPy spatial.ConvexHull), its corners in Qhull's order (anticlockwise). | None: R chull() gives the same corners, clockwise. | **P3.** Quickhull (Barber et al. 1996) is the standard convex-hull algorithm in SciPy, MATLAB and R geometry. **Checks:** At least 3 points that are not on one line. | [Barber et al. 1996](https://doi.org/10.1145/235815.235821) |
| `table.find_triangles_between_points()` | The Delaunay triangles of SciPy spatial.Delaunay (Qhull); the corners are the table's rows, counted from 1. | containing= gives the triangle that holds each of some points. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 3 points not on one straight line; rows with an empty cell are left out. |  |
| `table.find_triangles_between_points()` `containing=` | The triangle numbers count from 1; a point outside every triangle is empty. | Without containing=: every triangle. | **P5.** Positions count from 1 everywhere in simplydata. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As many numbers for x as for y. |  |
| `table.find_regions_around_points()` | A region that reaches out without end is open and has no area; a closed region is convex, so SciPy ConvexHull gives its area. | longitude= and latitude= for regions on the earth. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 3 points, no two at the same place. |  |
| `table.find_regions_around_points()` `longitude=` | Areas in square kilometres on a sphere of the earth's mean radius, 6371.0088 km (SciPy SphericalVoronoi's areas times the radius squared). | x= and y= in metres for a small area. | **P5.** The mean radius of the earth is the IUGG value. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 4 points spread over the earth. |  |

## Survival

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.find_survival_over_time()` | Kaplan-Meier curves with 95 % intervals on the log scale (Greenwood's standard error), as R survfit(); the median time with its interval. | at_time_points=, test_method="life table", allowing_for=, followed_from=, average_time_up_to=. | **P3.** Kaplan & Meier's estimator is the standard (Kaplan & Meier 1958); the log-scale interval is R survival's default (Therneau & Grambsch 2000). Log-log and arcsine-root intervals cover better in small samples (Klein & Moeschberger 2003), and SAS and Stata use log-log by default; simplydata does not yet offer them. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** An event column with three or more kinds is refused and find_risk_over_time named, because 1 - Kaplan-Meier overstates the risk when another event can come first (Gooley et al. 1999). | [Kaplan & Meier 1958](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1958.10501452); [Therneau & Grambsch 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-3294-8); [Klein & Moeschberger 2003](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97377); [Gooley et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19990330)18:6<695::AID-SIM60>3.0.CO;2-O) |
| `table.find_survival_over_time()` `at_time_points=` | The curve is flat between event times: the survival at a time is that of the last event time at or before it (R summary(survfit(), times =)). | at_time_points= the times. | **P3.** This is the Kaplan-Meier step function itself (Kaplan & Meier 1958). Times after a group's last follow-up are left empty, not carried on, which is said. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Negative and repeated times are refused; unsorted times are sorted, with a note. | [Kaplan & Meier 1958](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1958.10501452) |
| `table.find_survival_over_time()` `test_method="life table"` | The actuarial life table (R KMsurv::lifetab()): intervals start at the times given, the last is open, rows lost in an interval count as followed for half of it. | Leave test_method= out for Kaplan-Meier. | **P3.** The actuarial method of Cutler & Ederer 1958, for follow-up known only in intervals. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Needs at_time_points= starting at 0. | [Cutler & Ederer 1958](https://doi.org/10.1016/0021-9681(58)90126-7); [Klein & Moeschberger 2003](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97377) |
| `table.find_survival_over_time()` `allowing_for=` | Direct adjustment: each row's curve is predicted as if in each group from a Cox model and averaged over all rows (R adjustedCurves, method = "direct"), with intervals; never the curve of an 'average' row. | Leave it out for unadjusted curves. | **P2.** The curve of an 'average patient' is not the average of the patients' curves (Nieto & Coresh 1996; Cole & Hernán 2004); Denz, Klaaßen-Mielke & Timmesfeld 2023 compare the adjustment methods. **Checks:** Needs for_each_group_in=; refused with followed_from=. | [Nieto & Coresh 1996](https://doi.org/10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a008670); [Cole & Hernán 2004](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cmpb.2003.10.004); [Denz et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.9681) |
| `table.find_survival_over_time()` `followed_from=` | Each row is at risk only after its start (left truncation, R Surv(start, stop, event)). | Leave it out when everyone was followed from time 0. | **P4.** Counting a row as at risk before it entered the study biases the estimates (Cain et al. 2011); delayed entry is handled as Klein & Moeschberger 2003 describe. **Checks:** Empty start times and starts not before the end are refused; fewer than 10 rows at risk at the first event get a note that the start of the curve is shaky. | [Cain et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwq481); [Klein & Moeschberger 2003](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97377) |
| `table.find_survival_over_time()` `average_time_up_to=` | The restricted mean survival time up to the horizon you name (R summary(survfit(), rmean =)). | average_time_up_to= the horizon. | **P2.** The average time without the event up to a horizon is easier to read than a hazard ratio and needs no proportional hazards (Royston & Parmar 2013; Uno et al. 2014). The horizon must be chosen by you, so none is set silently. **Checks:** A horizon beyond the shortest group's follow-up is refused, with the largest allowed value. | [Royston & Parmar 2013](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-13-152); [Uno et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1200/JCO.2014.55.2208) |
| `table.compare_survival()` | The log-rank test (equal weight to every event time), as R survdiff(). | allowing_for= (stratified), followed_from=, average_time_up_to= (restricted means), competing_with= (Gray's test). | **P3.** The log-rank test is the standard comparison of whole survival curves and the most powerful when hazards are proportional (Mantel 1966; Peto & Peto 1972). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 2 groups or no events are refused; groups with no expected events are dropped, as R does, with a note. | [Mantel 1966](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/5910392/); [Peto & Peto 1972](https://doi.org/10.2307/2344317) |
| `table.compare_survival()` `allowing_for=` | The stratified log-rank test: groups compared within each stratum and the results added up (R survdiff(... + strata())). | Leave it out for the plain log-rank test. | **P3.** Stratifying keeps a strong factor from hiding or creating a difference (Kalbfleisch & Prentice 2002). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Number columns are refused; more than 20 strata get a note. | [Kalbfleisch & Prentice 2002](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118032985) |
| `table.compare_survival()` `followed_from=` | The log-rank test with delayed entry: each row counts as at risk only after its start (statsmodels survdiff(entry=)). | Leave it out when everyone was followed from time 0. | **P5.** R survdiff takes no start times. The log-rank test with delayed entry equals the score test of a Cox model with exact ties (coxph(Surv(start, stop, event) ~ group, ties = "exact")); the printout names that R call and the reliability study checks against it (Channing House: equal to 1.6e-11). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Refused with allowing_for=. | [Klein & Moeschberger 2003](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97377); [Therneau & Grambsch 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-3294-8) |
| `table.compare_survival()` `average_time_up_to=` | The difference and ratio of restricted mean survival times up to the horizon you name (R survRM2::rmst2()); arm 1 is the second group in sorted order. | average_time_up_to= the horizon. | **P2.** Royston & Parmar 2013 and Uno et al. 2014. survRM2's own default horizon is the shortest of the groups' last times; simplydata always passes yours. **Checks:** More than 2 groups give per-group values only, with a note. | [Royston & Parmar 2013](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-13-152); [Uno et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1200/JCO.2014.55.2208) |
| `table.compare_survival()` `competing_with=` | Gray's test of the chance of each event over time (cmprsk::cuminc), not a log-rank test with the competing event censored. | Leave it out when nothing competes. | **P2.** Gray 1988 compares cumulative incidence when another event can come first. **Checks:** value= is required; the same value in both is refused. | [Gray 1988](https://doi.org/10.1214/aos/1176350951) |
| `table.explain_survival()` | The Cox proportional hazards model with Efron's method for tied times (as R coxph()), Wald intervals; the proportional-hazards check by R cox.zph() and Harrell's C. | allowing_for= (strata), followed_from=, competing_with=, test_method="fine-gray", distribution= (accelerated failure time). | **P2.** Efron's approximation is more accurate than Breslow's when times are tied (Hertz-Picciotto & Rockhill 1997) and is R's default; the Schoenfeld-residual test checks proportional hazards (Grambsch & Therneau 1994); C is optimistic on the rows the model was fitted to (Harrell, Lee & Mark 1996), which is said. **Checks:** No events, too few rows, constant columns and columns that say the same thing are refused; a column whose effect may change over time gets a note pointing to average_time_up_to=; a fit that did not settle and extreme estimates get notes. | [Cox 1972](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1972.tb00899.x); [Efron 1977](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1977.10480613); [Hertz-Picciotto & Rockhill 1997](https://doi.org/10.2307/2533573); [Grambsch & Therneau 1994](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/81.3.515); [Harrell et al. 1996](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19960229)15:4<361::AID-SIM168>3.0.CO;2-4) |
| `table.explain_survival()` `allowing_for=` | A stratified Cox model: each stratum has its own baseline risk (R coxph(... + strata())), so it gets no hazard ratio. | Put the column in using= for its hazard ratio. | **P3.** Stratification allows for a factor whose hazards are not proportional (Therneau & Grambsch 2000). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Number columns are refused. | [Therneau & Grambsch 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-3294-8) |
| `table.explain_survival()` `followed_from=` | Each row is at risk only after its start (R coxph(Surv(start, stop, event) ~ ...)); when a row starts at an event time, the model is fitted by R coxph itself, and the printout says so. | Leave it out when everyone was followed from time 0. | **P4.** Start-stop rows handle delayed entry and columns that change over time; an exposure that starts during follow-up must not count from time 0, which would add immortal time (Suissa 2008; Therneau & Grambsch 2000). **Checks:** Empty start times and starts not before the end are refused. | [Suissa 2008](https://doi.org/10.1093/aje/kwm324); [Therneau & Grambsch 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4757-3294-8); [Klein & Moeschberger 2003](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97377) |
| `table.explain_survival()` `competing_with=` | Cause-specific Cox model: the event asked about, with the competing event counted as the end of follow-up. | test_method="fine-gray" for the risk itself. | **P2.** Austin, Lee & Fine 2016 recommend cause-specific hazards for questions about causes and Fine-Gray for predicting risk; the printout names the other model. | [Austin et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1161/CIRCULATIONAHA.115.017719); [Latouche et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclinepi.2012.09.017) |
| `table.explain_survival()` `test_method="fine-gray"` | Fine and Gray's subdistribution hazard model (cmprsk::crr) with robust (sandwich) standard errors. | Leave test_method= out for the cause-specific model. | **P2.** Fine & Gray 1999 model the risk of an event when another can come first; their hazard ratios are not ordinary hazard ratios, which the printout says (Austin, Lee & Fine 2016; Latouche et al. 2013). **Checks:** Needs value= and competing_with=; refused with allowing_for= or followed_from=. | [Fine & Gray 1999](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1999.10474144); [Austin et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1161/CIRCULATIONAHA.115.017719); [Latouche et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclinepi.2012.09.017) |
| `table.explain_survival()` `distribution=` | The accelerated failure time model of R survreg(): time ratios exp(coefficient), never hazard ratios; for Weibull the shape in words. | distribution="weibull", "exponential", "lognormal", "log-logistic". | **P3.** An accelerated failure time model estimates how much longer or shorter the time is (Kalbfleisch & Prentice 2002); AIC is printed so that distributions can be compared. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Times of 0 are refused (a Cox model is suggested). | [Kalbfleisch & Prentice 2002](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118032985) |
| `table.explain_survival()` `empty_cells="multiple imputation"` | The empty cells of the number columns of using= filled in 20 times (statsmodels MICEData) from the event, the Nelson-Aalen cumulative hazard (R mice::nelsonaalen()) and the other columns; R survival::coxph() on each filled-in table, pooled by R mice::pool() (Barnard-Rubin degrees of freedom). | empty_cells="leave out" (the default: rows with an empty cell are skipped). | **P2.** To fill in a covariate of a Cox model, the event and the cumulative hazard should be in the imputation model, not the time (White & Royston 2009); small-sample degrees of freedom follow Barnard & Rubin (1999). **Checks:** A plain Cox model only (allowing_for=, followed_from=, competing_with=, distribution= and test_method="fine-gray" are refused); empty cells in text columns, the time or the event leave those rows out. | [White & Royston 2009](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.3618); [Barnard & Rubin 1999](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/86.4.948); [Rubin 1987](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470316696) |
| `chart.draw_survival_curves()` | Kaplan-Meier curves with censoring marks; no confidence band and no numbers at risk unless asked. | with_confidence_band=True (95 %, log scale), with_at_risk_table=True, allowing_for=, followed_from=. | **P5.** Kaplan & Meier 1958. Here simplydata departs from reporting advice, which asks for the numbers at risk and the uncertainty to be shown (Pocock, Clayton & Altman 2002; Morris et al. 2019); both are one option away. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** An event column with three or more kinds is refused (draw_risk_curves is named). | [Kaplan & Meier 1958](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1958.10501452); [Pocock et al. 2002](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(02)08594-X); [Morris et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmjopen-2019-030215) |
| `chart.draw_survival_curves()` `followed_from=` | Each row is at risk only after its start (delayed entry). | Leave it out when everyone was followed from time 0. | **P4.** As table.find_survival_over_time(followed_from=). **Checks:** Refused with allowing_for=. | [Klein & Moeschberger 2003](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97377) |
| `chart.draw_survival_curves()` `allowing_for=` | Direct adjustment: each row's curve predicted in each group, then averaged over all rows. | Leave it out for unadjusted curves. | **P2.** As table.find_survival_over_time(allowing_for=) (Nieto & Coresh 1996). **Checks:** Needs colour=; refused with the at-risk table or followed_from=. | [Nieto & Coresh 1996](https://doi.org/10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a008670); [Denz et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.9681) |
| `table.add_time_and_event_columns()` | Follow-up ends at the first of the end dates (an empty date does not end it); the event counts when its date came first or on the same day; a year is 365.25 days and a month 30.4375 days. | unit="weeks", "months", "years"; until=. | **P3.** This is data preparation, not a statistical method; 365.25-day years are the convention of R Epi (cal.yr) and of person-year tables in epidemiology, and the printout says it. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** End dates before start dates are refused; events before the start (prevalent cases) are left out, with a note. |  |
| `table.find_risk_over_time()` | Nothing competing: 1 minus Kaplan-Meier. With competing_with=: the Aalen-Johansen estimate (R survfit with a factor event) with log-scale 95 % intervals; the competing event is a state of its own. | competing_with=, value=, for_each_group_in=. | **P2.** 1 - Kaplan-Meier overstates the risk when another event can come first (Gooley et al. 1999); the Aalen-Johansen estimate does not. The printout shows what 1 - Kaplan-Meier would have given. **Checks:** Three or more kinds of outcome without competing_with= are refused. | [Gooley et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19990330)18:6<695::AID-SIM60>3.0.CO;2-O); [Klein & Moeschberger 2003](https://doi.org/10.1007/b97377) |
| `chart.draw_risk_curves()` | With nothing competing, the chance of having had the event is 1 minus the Kaplan-Meier curve (R plot(survfit(...), fun = "event")). | competing_with=; with_confidence_band=True. | **P2.** As table.find_risk_over_time (Gooley et al. 1999). **Checks:** Three or more kinds of outcome without competing_with= are refused. | [Gooley et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19990330)18:6<695::AID-SIM60>3.0.CO;2-O) |
| `chart.draw_risk_curves()` `competing_with=` | The Aalen-Johansen estimate: the competing event is a state of its own, never counted as censored. | Leave it out when nothing competes. | **P2.** As table.find_risk_over_time (Gooley et al. 1999). **Checks:** Needs R survival. | [Gooley et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19990330)18:6<695::AID-SIM60>3.0.CO;2-O) |

## Combining studies

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.combine_studies()` | Risk ratios for counts (studies with no events in either group left out), Hedges' g for averages; random effects with REML; Hartung-Knapp intervals with the ad hoc rule (never narrower than the normal interval); the prediction interval from 3 studies; from 10 studies, a small-study test (Peters' test for counts, else Egger's) and trim-and-fill as a what-if. | show=, test_method= (DerSimonian-Laird, Paule-Mandel, common effect, Mantel-Haenszel, Peto), interval_by=, for_each_group_in=, using=. | **P1.** The method is chosen before the data, never by the heterogeneity test (Borenstein et al. 2010). REML and Paule-Mandel estimate the between-study spread better than DerSimonian-Laird; Veroniki et al. 2016 favour Paule-Mandel for counts and Langan et al. 2019 favour REML, the default of metafor and RevMan (Cochrane Handbook). Hartung-Knapp intervals keep their coverage (IntHout, Ioannidis & Borm 2014) once made never narrower than the normal ones (Röver, Knapp & Friede 2015). Prediction intervals say how much true effects differ (Riley, Higgins & Deeks 2011; IntHout et al. 2016). Funnel tests need 10 studies (Sterne et al. 2011); Peters' test was developed for odds ratios (Peters et al. 2006), and trim-and-fill performs poorly under heterogeneity (Peters et al. 2007), so it is printed as a what-if only. With rare events, inverse-variance methods with a 0.5 correction are biased and Mantel-Haenszel or Peto do better (Bradburn et al. 2007), which test_method= gives. **Checks:** Fewer than 2 studies, impossible values (events above the count, an estimate outside its interval ...) and mixed input routes are refused; notes below 5 studies (the spread is hard to estimate), without a prediction interval below 3, and 'too few studies (under 10)' for small-study effects, which are 'not proof of publication bias'; empty cells get 1/2 added (metafor's default, which can pull small studies towards no effect: Sweeting et al. 2004), with a note. There is no note yet for rare events. | [Deeks et al. (Cochrane Handbook, ch. 10)](https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-10); [Borenstein et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/jrsm.12); [Veroniki et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1002/jrsm.1164); [Langan et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1002/jrsm.1316); [IntHout et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-14-25); [Röver et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12874-015-0091-1); [Riley et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.d549); [IntHout et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmjopen-2015-010247); [Higgins et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-985X.2008.00552.x); [Sterne et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.d4002); [Peters et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.295.6.676); [Peters et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2889); [Egger et al. 1997](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.315.7109.629); [Duval & Tweedie 2000](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.0006-341X.2000.00455.x); [Deeks 2002](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1188); [Hedges 1981](https://doi.org/10.3102/10769986006002107); [Viechtbauer 2010](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v036.i03); [Hartung & Knapp 2001](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1009); [Hedges et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1890/0012-9658(1999)080[1150:TMAORR]2.0.CO;2); [Sweeting et al. 2004](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1761); [Bradburn et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2528) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `estimates=` | A column with 'ratio' (or OR, HR, RR ...) in its name is combined on the log scale; each estimate and its interval become a log ratio and its variance (metafor conv.wald()), and the result is shown back as a ratio. | estimates_are_ratios= decides otherwise; standard_errors= instead of intervals. | **P1.** Ratios are combined on the log scale, where their sampling distribution is close to normal (Cochrane Handbook, chapter 10). **Checks:** Interval columns that cannot be found are refused, with the names expected. | [Deeks et al. (Cochrane Handbook, ch. 10)](https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-10); [Viechtbauer 2010](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v036.i03) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `test_method=` | With test_method="dersimonian-laird": the moment estimator of the between-study spread (metafor "DL"); "paule-mandel" is "PM"; "common effect" is "EE". | Leave test_method= out for REML. | **P2.** DerSimonian-Laird is the classic estimator of older meta-analyses (DerSimonian & Laird 1986) but underestimates the spread (Veroniki et al. 2016). Paule-Mandel (Paule & Mandel 1982) is recommended for binary and continuous data by Veroniki et al. 2016; Langan et al. 2019 prefer REML and found Paule-Mandel biased upward when study sizes differ widely. **Checks:** When REML does not converge, the refusal names Paule-Mandel and DerSimonian-Laird. | [DerSimonian & Laird 1986](https://doi.org/10.1016/0197-2456(86)90046-2); [Paule & Mandel 1982](https://doi.org/10.6028/jres.087.022); [Veroniki et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1002/jrsm.1164); [Langan et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1002/jrsm.1316) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `test_method="mantel-haenszel"` | Mantel-Haenszel weights, a common-effect method: no between-study spread and no prediction interval. | Leave test_method= out for random effects. | **P3.** Mantel & Haenszel 1959; a common-effect method assumes every study measures the same effect, which the printout says. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Counts or rates only; no meta-regression; no trim-and-fill. | [Mantel & Haenszel 1959](https://doi.org/10.1093/jnci/22.4.719); [Deeks et al. (Cochrane Handbook, ch. 10)](https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-10) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `test_method="peto"` | Peto's one-step odds ratio, a common-effect method; it gives odds ratios only. | Leave test_method= out for random effects. | **P2.** Peto's method is the least biased for rare events with balanced groups (Bradburn et al. 2007; the Cochrane Handbook), but biased when effects are large (Yusuf et al. 1985 for the method). **Checks:** Counts only; other show= values are refused. | [Yusuf et al. 1985](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0033-0620(85)80003-7); [Bradburn et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2528); [Deeks et al. (Cochrane Handbook, ch. 10)](https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-10) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `interval_by=` | With interval_by="normal": the normal (z) interval and p-value of older meta-analyses. | Leave it out for Hartung-Knapp. | **P2.** The normal interval is too narrow with few studies (IntHout, Ioannidis & Borm 2014; Partlett & Riley 2017), so it is offered for comparison with older papers only. **Checks:** Hartung-Knapp with a common-effect method is refused. | [IntHout et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-14-25); [Partlett & Riley 2017](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.7140) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `for_each_group_in=` | Each subgroup its own random-effects model with its own between-study spread; the test of subgroup difference compares the subgroup estimates (a common-effect meta-regression), as RevMan and R meta do. | using= for a meta-regression. | **P3.** Separate spreads per subgroup follow RevMan and R meta (tau.common = FALSE); Borenstein & Higgins 2013 discuss both and advise one pooled spread when subgroups have few studies. Subgroups must be compared with the test of difference, not by which subgroup's interval leaves out no effect (Sun et al. 2010). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Subgroups with no informative study are dropped (note); fewer than 5 studies in a subgroup get a note. | [Borenstein & Higgins 2013](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11121-013-0377-7); [Sun et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.c117); [Deeks et al. (Cochrane Handbook, ch. 10)](https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-10) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `using=` | A meta-regression with the default estimator and Hartung-Knapp intervals; R squared says how much of the between-study spread the columns account for. | interval_by="normal". | **P2.** Knapp & Hartung 2003 give the test for meta-regression; Thompson & Higgins 2002 warn that few studies make chance links likely and that the link is between studies, not within them. **Checks:** Number columns only; fewer than 10 studies per column get a note. | [Knapp & Hartung 2003](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1482); [Thompson & Higgins 2002](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1187) |
| `table.combine_studies()` `p_values=` | Fisher's method when test_method= is left out (SciPy combine_pvalues's default method="fisher"); no R needed. | test_method="stouffer" (with counts= to weigh by the square root of each study's size), "pearson", "tippett" or "mudholkar-george". | **P3.** Fisher's method is the classic way to combine p-values and SciPy's default. It says whether any study shows an effect, not how big it is, which the sentence says. SciPy's own default (scipy.stats), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 2 p-values, each above 0 and at most 1; not with the other ways in (events=, estimates= ...). |  |
| `chart.draw_forest_plot()` | Ratios on a log scale with the line of no effect at 1, other effects on a plain scale with the line at 0; on combine_studies results, squares sized by weight, diamonds for the combined effects and a line for the prediction interval. | log_scale=, line_at=, size=. | **P2.** A log scale makes a ratio of 2 and of 0.5 the same distance from 1 (Lewis & Clarke 2001; Cochrane Handbook). **Checks:** With several interval levels, the first is drawn and a note names the rest. | [Lewis & Clarke 2001](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.322.7300.1479); [Deeks et al. (Cochrane Handbook, ch. 10)](https://www.cochrane.org/authors/handbooks-and-manuals/handbook/current/chapter-10) |
| `chart.draw_funnel_plot()` | Each study against its standard error (0 at the top), the combined effect of combine_studies as the line, and pseudo 95 % limits; ratios on a log scale. | There are no options for the limits. | **P1.** Sterne & Egger 2001 recommend the standard error on the vertical axis; the printout says asymmetry is not proof of publication bias (Sterne et al. 2011). **Checks:** Refused for correlations (their standard error is on Fisher's z scale), meta-regressions and fewer than 3 studies. | [Sterne & Egger 2001](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0895-4356(01)00377-8); [Sterne et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.d4002) |

## Dates and time windows

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.make_run_of_dates()` | Month, quarter and year steps are counted from the first date, staying on its day or on the month's last day when the month is shorter. | every=, to= or how_many=. | **P5.** As pandas DateOffset and Polars "1mo" counted from the start; R's seq(by = "month") runs over into the next month instead. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `sdata.make_run_of_dates()` `every="working day"` | Monday to Friday (pandas bdate_range); a first date on a weekend starts on the Monday after; no public holidays are left out. | how_many= gives that many working days instead of to=; every="day" for every day. | **P3.** Public holidays differ from country to country; pandas bdate_range without holidays is the package default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Either to= or how_many=; at least one working day between the dates. |  |
| `table.add_missing_dates()` `every=` | Without every=, the most common step between the dates (snapped to months, quarters or years when close), printed. | every= the step. | **P5.** Data preparation, not a statistical method: the step found is always printed so it can be checked. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Dates with no nameable step are refused. |  |
| `table.add_missing_dates()` | Each group gets its own run of dates from its first to its last date; the table comes back sorted by the groups and the dates; rows with an empty group cell are a group of their own. | between= to add dates before and after; every= the step. | **P5.** Data preparation: the new rows have empty cells, never made-up values, and the sentence says how many were added. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Repeated dates and dates off the step are refused. |  |

## Smoothing and trend

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.add_smoothed_column()` `test_method="loess", interval=` | R loess's own defaults: span 0.75, local curves of degree 2, no robustness steps; the band uses loess's own degrees of freedom. | smoothness= another span; interval= the level. | **P3.** These are the defaults of Cleveland's loess as implemented in R (Cleveland & Devlin 1988). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least 5 readings. | [Cleveland & Devlin 1988](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1988.10478639) |
| `table.add_smoothed_column()` | A centred moving average of 5 readings, the ends left empty (as R stats::filter(x, rep(1/5, 5), sides = 2)). | test_method="median", "savitzky-golay", "exponential", "gaussian", "lowess", "loess", "spline", "whittaker", "wiener"; window=, ends=. | **P5.** The moving average is the simplest smoother and repeats R exactly; the window of 5 is a starting value, not a published rule. The other methods keep their packages' defaults (Savitzky & Golay 1964; Cleveland 1979). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Even or too large windows are refused; empty cells and uneven spacing are refused for window methods. | [Savitzky & Golay 1964](https://doi.org/10.1021/ac60214a047); [Cleveland 1979](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1979.10481038) |
| `numbers.smooth()` | A centred moving average of 5 numbers, the ends left empty. | As table.add_smoothed_column. | **P5.** As table.add_smoothed_column. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As table.add_smoothed_column. |  |
| `table.find_trend_of()` | Sen's slope with its 95 % interval and the Mann-Kendall test with continuity correction (R trend); with repeats_every=, the seasonal Mann-Kendall test and seasonal Sen slope. | repeats_every=. | **P2.** Sen's slope and the Mann-Kendall test need no bell shape and resist outliers (Sen 1968; Mann 1945); the seasonal form compares the same season across years (Hirsch, Slack & Smith 1982). The test assumes independent readings; autocorrelation inflates its false alarms (Hamed & Rao 1998; Yue et al. 2002). simplydata checks for it but does not offer the modified test. **Checks:** Fewer than 4 readings are refused; without R only the slope is given, with a note. Without repeats_every= the readings left after Sen's line are tested for lag-1 autocorrelation (statsmodels acf and Ljung-Box at lag 1, lag 1 being the reading before); when it is positive with p < 0.05 a note says the p-value is too small and names the modified test (R modifiedmk). With repeats_every= the method line says independence is assumed, not checked. | [Sen 1968](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1968.10480934); [Mann 1945](https://doi.org/10.2307/1907187); [Hirsch et al. 1982](https://doi.org/10.1029/WR018i001p00107); [Hamed & Ramachandra Rao 1998](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0022-1694(97)00125-X); [Yue et al. 2002](https://doi.org/10.1002/hyp.1095) |
| `numbers.remove_trend()` | Removes a least-squares straight line (SciPy detrend, as residuals(lm(x ~ seq_along(x)))). | test_method="average"; breaks= (separate lines per piece, as SciPy). | **P3.** A straight line is the simplest trend; the help says that MATLAB and R pracma join the pieces, SciPy does not. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty values and fewer than 3 numbers are refused. |  |
| `table.add_trend_and_season()` | STL (season and trend by loess) with a season window of 7, as R stl(s.window = 7), not robust. | season_window="fixed" or another odd number; robust=True; test_method="moving averages" (R decompose) or "straight line". | **P5.** STL handles any season length and resists outliers when robust (Cleveland et al. 1990). R stl() has no default season window; 7 is simplydata's starting value. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Fewer than 2 full cycles and even or small windows are refused. | [Cleveland et al. 1990](https://www.scb.se/contentassets/ca21efb41fee47d293bbee5bf7be7fb3/stl-a-seasonal-trend-decomposition-procedure-based-on-loess.pdf) |
| `table.find_autocorrelation_of()` | The autocorrelation up to 10 x log10(n) rows back (as R acf()), the white-noise band +-1.96 / sqrt(n), and the Ljung-Box test. | show="partial" or "covariance"; band="bartlett"; rows_back=. | **P3.** These are R acf()'s defaults; the Bartlett band (Bartlett 1946) widens with the lag for moving-average processes; the Ljung-Box test checks many lags at once (Ljung & Box 1978). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 4 readings, a constant series and uneven spacing are refused. | [Bartlett 1946](https://doi.org/10.2307/2983611); [Ljung & Box 1978](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/65.2.297) |
| `table.find_autocorrelation_of()` `x=` | Moran's I with every pair of places as neighbours, weights 1 / distance, each row's weights summing to 1, and a permutation p-value from 999 shuffles after set.seed(1), one-sided (nearby places more alike) (R spdep). | nearest=8 for the 8 nearest places; on_globe=True for longitude and latitude; permutations=, with_seed=. | **P5.** Moran's I (Moran 1950) depends on the weights, which must be reported (Bivand & Wong 2018). Inverse-distance weights between all pairs need no distance band chosen by hand; row-standardising and the one-sided permutation test are spdep's defaults (Bivand & Wong 2018). No study fixes the weights for every field, so this is a starting value, printed every time. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Two rows at one place refused, with the line that averages them; fewer than 10 places noted; longitude and latitude without on_globe=True noted; sorted_by= or rows_back= with x= refused. | [Moran 1950](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/37.1-2.17); [Bivand & Wong 2018](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11749-018-0599-x) |
| `table.find_autocorrelation_of()` `nearest=` | Each place's k nearest places as neighbours, alike, each row's weights summing to 1 (R spdep knearneigh). | Leave nearest= out for 1 / distance weights between all pairs. | **P5.** k-nearest-neighbour weights give every place the same number of neighbours, useful when places are unevenly spread (Bivand & Wong 2018). Moran's I changes with the weights (on meuse 0.12 with 1 / distance, 0.49 with the 8 nearest), so the printout names them. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** nearest= must be below the number of places. | [Bivand & Wong 2018](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11749-018-0599-x) |
| `table.find_autocorrelation_of()` `on_globe=` | Longitude and latitude read as places on the earth (crs 4326): great-circle distances in km. | Leave on_globe= out for straight-line distances on x and y. | **P4.** Straight-line distances on degrees are wrong away from the equator, because a degree east shortens with latitude; spdep uses great-circle distances for geographic coordinates (Bivand & Wong 2018). simplydata does not guess: it notes columns that look like longitude and latitude. **Checks:** Refused when x= is beyond -180 to 180 or y= beyond -90 to 90. | [Bivand & Wong 2018](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11749-018-0599-x) |
| `numbers.find_autocorrelation()` | As table.find_autocorrelation_of: 10 x log10(n) rows back, the white-noise band, Ljung-Box. | show=, band=, rows_back=. | **P3.** As table.find_autocorrelation_of. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.find_autocorrelation_of. | [Bartlett 1946](https://doi.org/10.2307/2983611); [Ljung & Box 1978](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/65.2.297) |
| `table.check_steady_over_time()` | Steady around one level: the ADF test with a constant only (R urca::ur.df(type = "drift"), MacKinnon's p-value from urca::punitroot()) and the KPSS test of a steady level (tseries::kpss.test(null = "Level")), read together. | test_method="adf" or "kpss"; around="straight line": tseries::adf.test() (constant and trend) and kpss.test(null = "Trend"). | **P3.** The two tests have opposite null hypotheses, so reading them together separates 'steady', 'wanders' and 'cannot tell' (Dickey & Fuller 1979; Kwiatkowski et al. 1992); that only works when both ask the same question, so the ADF regression follows around= (tseries::adf.test always includes a trend, which would pass a steadily rising series as steady around a level). tseries has no constant-only ADF test, so that one is urca's, with MacKinnon's (1996) response-surface p-value. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 10 readings are refused; needs R; the printout says which question both tests ask. | [Dickey & Fuller 1979](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1979.10482531); [Kwiatkowski et al. 1992](https://doi.org/10.1016/0304-4076(92)90104-Y); [MacKinnon 1996](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1099-1255(199611)11:6<601::AID-JAE417>3.0.CO;2-T) |
| `table.find_change_points_in()` | Changes in the average by PELT with the MBIC penalty (R changepoint::cpt.mean(method = "PELT")), after dividing by the noise estimated as the median absolute deviation of neighbouring differences / sqrt(2). | change="average and spread"; penalty="bic", "aic", "hannan-quinn". | **P2.** PELT finds the best set of changes exactly and fast (Killick, Fearnhead & Eckley 2012); cpt.mean's own default method finds at most one change. MBIC is changepoint's default penalty (Zhang & Siegmund 2007). cpt.mean assumes a noise of 1, so simplydata scales by a robust difference-based noise estimate (in the spirit of Hall, Kay & Titterington 1990), its own step, said in the method line. **Checks:** Fewer than 4 readings and readings that do not vary are refused. | [Killick et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.2012.737745); [Zhang & Siegmund 2007](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1541-0420.2006.00662.x); [Killick & Eckley 2014](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v058.i03); [Hall et al. 1990](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/77.3.521) |
| `chart.draw_trend_and_season()` | STL as table.add_trend_and_season. | test_method=, repeats_every=. | **P5.** As table.add_trend_and_season (Cleveland et al. 1990). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As table.add_trend_and_season. | [Cleveland et al. 1990](https://www.scb.se/contentassets/ca21efb41fee47d293bbee5bf7be7fb3/stl-a-seasonal-trend-decomposition-procedure-based-on-loess.pdf) |
| `chart.draw_autocorrelation_bars()` | As table.find_autocorrelation_of, drawn with the dashed white-noise band of R plot(acf()). | show=, band="bartlett", rows_back=. | **P3.** As table.find_autocorrelation_of. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.find_autocorrelation_of. | [Bartlett 1946](https://doi.org/10.2307/2983611) |
| `chart.draw_cross_correlation_bars()` | The cross-correlation of two columns (as R ccf(), with the lag sign turned round) for 10 rows back each way, with the band +-1.96 / sqrt(n). | rows_back=. table.find_correlation_between(rows_back=) uses R's 10 x log10(n) instead. | **P5.** These are R ccf()'s band and a fixed starting number of lags; the printout says a correlation does not show that one thing causes the other. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Exactly two columns, filled and evenly spaced. |  |
| `chart.draw_lag_dots()` | Each value against the value rows_back=1 earlier, in the order of sorted_by=, with a dashed 1:1 line from the smallest to the biggest value. | rows_back= a number or a list (one colour each), transparency=. | **P3.** As R lag.plot: dots near the 1:1 line show values like the ones before them. One row back is R's default. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** rows_back= must be whole numbers of 1 or more. |  |

## Forecasts

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.forecast()` | Holt-Winters exponential smoothing (R HoltWinters()) with an undamped trend, and a season added when repeats_every= is given; 12 steps ahead with 95 % intervals; a Ljung-Box check of the residuals. | test_method="last value", "same season last time", "average", "straight on", "arima" (order= required); steps_ahead=, interval=, trend=False, season="multiplied". | **P3.** Holt-Winters is the classic exponential smoothing (Winters 1960). The evidence favours other defaults: modern practice chooses among exponential smoothing models by AICc (Hyndman et al. 2002; Hyndman & Athanasopoulos 2021), and damped trends usually forecast better than straight ones (Gardner & McKenzie 1985); simplydata offers neither yet. The residual check uses the lag rule of Hyndman & Athanasopoulos. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A season needs 2 full cycles; ARIMA orders are chosen only when asked (order="automatic"), and the order chosen is printed; residuals that still hold a pattern get a note. | [Winters 1960](https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.6.3.324); [Hyndman et al. 2002](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0169-2070(01)00110-8); [Hyndman et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-71918-2); [Hyndman & Athanasopoulos 2021](https://otexts.com/fpp3/); [Gardner & McKenzie 1985](https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.31.10.1237); [Ljung & Box 1978](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/65.2.297) |
| `table.forecast()` `order="automatic"` | R forecast::auto.arima chooses the order and the seasonal order by AICc, trying every order (stepwise = FALSE, approximation = FALSE) for series of up to 1,000 readings. | order=(p, d, q) and seasonal_order= given by you; methods other than "arima". | **P2.** auto.arima is the published automatic procedure (Hyndman & Khandakar 2008); its quicker stepwise search and the approximation can pick another order, so simplydata searches fully where that is fast. An order chosen from the data makes the intervals a little too narrow (Hyndman & Athanasopoulos 2021), which the printout says. **Checks:** seasonal_order= with order="automatic" is refused; the chosen orders are printed; above 1,000 readings the quicker search is used and said. | [Hyndman & Khandakar 2008](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v027.i03); [Hyndman & Athanasopoulos 2021](https://otexts.com/fpp3/) |
| `numbers.forecast()` | As table.forecast. | As table.forecast. | **P3.** As table.forecast. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.forecast. | [Winters 1960](https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.6.3.324); [Hyndman & Athanasopoulos 2021](https://otexts.com/fpp3/) |
| `chart.draw_forecast()` | Holt-Winters exponential smoothing as table.forecast, 12 steps ahead, the 95 % interval as a band. | As table.forecast. | **P3.** As table.forecast. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.forecast. | [Winters 1960](https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.6.3.324); [Hyndman & Athanasopoulos 2021](https://otexts.com/fpp3/) |
| `table.compare_forecasts_for()` | The last 12 readings held back; last value, same season last time (or the average), straight on and exponential smoothing compared by MASE. | test_method=, check_on_last=. | **P2.** MASE compares forecasts across series and is defined even with zeros (Hyndman & Koehler 2006). **Checks:** At least 2 methods; ARIMA needs order= (three numbers, or "automatic"). | [Hyndman & Koehler 2006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijforecast.2006.03.001); [Hyndman & Athanasopoulos 2021](https://otexts.com/fpp3/) |

## Cycles and filters

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.find_each_peak_that()` | Every local maximum (SciPy signal.find_peaks() with no thresholds unless you give them); flat tops counted once; the prominence and the width at half prominence of each. | is_at_least=, apart=, stands_out_by=, wide_at_least=. | **P3.** No threshold is a neutral start: which peaks matter is your question, and the conditions say it in words. These are SciPy's defaults. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty cells are never peaks; apart= and wide_at_least= need even spacing. |  |
| `numbers.find_each_peak_that()` | Every local maximum, as table.find_each_peak_that. | As table.find_each_peak_that. | **P3.** As table.find_each_peak_that. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `chart.draw_peak_dots()` | Every local maximum marked, as table.find_each_peak_that. | is_at_least=, apart=, stands_out_by=, dips=. | **P3.** As table.find_each_peak_that. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.find_each_dip_that()` | Every local minimum, as numbers.find_each_dip_that; apart= and wide_at_least= in units of sorted_by, turned into readings rounded to 9 decimals. | is_at_most=, apart=, stands_out_by=, wide_at_least=, for_each_group_in=. | **P3.** As table.find_each_peak_that. Rounding the number of readings keeps floating point from making SciPy's distance one reading longer. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty cells are never dips; apart= and wide_at_least= need even spacing. |  |
| `numbers.find_each_dip_that()` | Every local minimum: SciPy signal.find_peaks on minus the numbers (as R pracma::findpeaks(-x) for dips without flat bottoms); positions from 1, widths halfway down each dip. | is_at_most=, apart=, stands_out_by=, wide_at_least=, time_step=. | **P3.** A dip is a peak turned upside down, so the same neutral start as find_each_peak_that: no threshold unless you give one. These are SciPy's definitions. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.find_spectrum_of()` | The raw periodogram (no taper, mean removed), scaled so the powers add up to the variance (simplydata's choice of scaling); the 3 strongest peaks named. | test_method="welch" (averaged pieces of 256 readings, Hann window), "lomb-scargle" (uneven times); show=. | **P3.** The periodogram shows every frequency the data allow; its values scatter widely and do not settle with more data, which Welch's averaged periodogram reduces (Welch 1967), so test_method="welch" is offered. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Fewer than 4 readings, empty cells and uneven spacing are refused (Lomb-Scargle takes uneven times). | [Welch 1967](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAU.1967.1161901) |
| `numbers.find_spectrum()` | As table.find_spectrum_of. | As table.find_spectrum_of. | **P3.** As table.find_spectrum_of. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.find_spectrum_of. | [Welch 1967](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAU.1967.1161901) |
| `numbers.find_spectrum()` `over_time=` | As chart.draw_spectrum_over_time: SciPy spectrogram, pieces of min(256, n) readings, half overlap, Hann weights, each piece's mean removed. | window=, window_shape=, show="decibels". | **P3.** As chart.draw_spectrum_over_time. *Basis: convention.* | [Welch 1967](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAU.1967.1161901) |
| `chart.draw_spectrum()` | The periodogram as table.find_spectrum_of, on a log scale, the 3 strongest peaks labelled. | test_method="welch", show=, log_scale=False. | **P3.** As table.find_spectrum_of (Welch 1967 for the averaged form). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As table.find_spectrum_of. | [Welch 1967](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAU.1967.1161901) |
| `chart.draw_spectrum_over_time()` | Pieces of 256 readings with a Hann window and half overlap (SciPy spectrogram), in decibels. | window=, window_shape=. | **P5.** SciPy's spectrogram with its usual window; 256 is a starting value that trades time against frequency detail. *Basis: starting value.* | [Welch 1967](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAU.1967.1161901) |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` | A Butterworth filter of order 4, run both ways, as table.add_filtered_column. | As table.add_filtered_column. | **P5.** As table.add_filtered_column. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As table.add_filtered_column. | [Gustafsson 1996](https://doi.org/10.1109/78.492552) |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `test_method="fir"` | SciPy firwin: 101 weights and a Hamming window, run both ways. | how_many_weights=, window_shape=; test_method="fir least squares", "fir equiripple", "fir frequency sampling"; find_order_for= picks the length by the Kaiser rule. | **P3.** The window method is SciPy's and MATLAB's first FIR design; the Hamming window is firwin's default. 101 weights is a starting length, not a published rule; find_order_for= works it out from the losses asked. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** An even number of weights is refused (an odd one works for every kind of band). |  |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `test_method="fir least squares"` | SciPy firls with the cutoff in the middle of transition_width=, equal weight in each band. | transition_width=, how_many_weights=. | **P3.** SciPy firls with its default weights, as R gsignal::firls; the cutoff in the middle of the change follows SciPy kaiserord's documentation. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** transition_width= is needed, and bands that would overlap or leave 0 to half the readings per unit of time are refused. |  |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `test_method="fir equiripple"` | SciPy remez (Parks-McClellan) with the band edges of "fir least squares", equal weight in each band. | transition_width=, how_many_weights=. | **P3.** SciPy remez with its defaults, as R signal::remez (the same numbers). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As "fir least squares". |  |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `test_method="fir frequency sampling"` | SciPy firwin2 with a jump at the cutoff (or the change of transition_width=), SciPy's frequency grid and a Hamming window. | transition_width=, window_shape=, how_many_weights=. | **P3.** SciPy firwin2's defaults; R gsignal::fir2 uses a finer grid (512 points) by default, so its weights differ slightly unless the grids are the same. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** As "fir least squares". |  |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `test_method="gammatone"` | SciPy gammatone(f, "iir"): its width is fixed by the frequency (Slaney's design), as in models of hearing. | is_equal_to= with the other designs: a peak filter of width=. | **P3.** SciPy's only gammatone design; width= does not apply. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Only with keep_each_frequency_that(is_equal_to=). |  |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `find_order_for=` | The smallest steepness that loses at most ripple= dB (0.5) where it keeps and removes at least removed_by= dB (40) from "loss_at" on: SciPy buttord, cheb1ord, cheb2ord, ellipord (kaiserord for "fir"). | steepness= instead; ripple= and removed_by= for other losses. | **P5.** SciPy's order selection, as R gsignal::buttord and the others (the same numbers); 0.5 dB and 40 dB are simplydata's starting values for ripple= and removed_by=. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** "loss_at" on the wrong side of the cutoff, a design with no order rule (bessel) and more than steepness 20 are refused. |  |
