| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `start_from="first value"` | Forwards only, starting in the steady state for the first reading: SciPy sosfilt_zi (lfilter_zi) times it. | start_from="rest" (the default), both_ways=True. | **P3.** What SciPy's documentation shows for a filter that should not swing up from 0 at the start, as R gsignal::filter_zi. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Only with both_ways=False. |  |
| `numbers.keep_each_frequency_that()` `with_least_delay=` | SciPy minimum_phase(method="homomorphic", half=False): the same gain, the least delay. | both_ways=True for no delay at all. | **P5.** half=False keeps the gain at every frequency (SciPy's default half=True takes its square root). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Only for FIR designs run forwards (both_ways=False). |  |
| `numbers.remove_each_frequency_that()` | A Butterworth filter of order 4, run both ways; is_equal_to= removes one frequency with a notch filter. | As table.add_filtered_column; and_multiples=True for a notch at each multiple (SciPy iirnotch each; the average stays). | **P5.** As table.add_filtered_column. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As table.add_filtered_column. | [Gustafsson 1996](https://doi.org/10.1109/78.492552) |
| `table.add_filtered_column()` | A Butterworth filter of order 4, run forwards and backwards (zero phase, SciPy sosfiltfilt), the ends padded by odd reflection (SciPy's default). | test_method="chebyshev", "chebyshev 2", "elliptic", "bessel"; steepness=; both_ways=False; remove_at=. | **P5.** The Butterworth filter has no ripple in the band kept; running it both ways removes the shift in time. The ends are handled by SciPy's padding (Gustafsson 1996 gives another way to start the filter). Order 4 is a common engineering starting value, not a published rule. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Frequencies outside (0, Nyquist), steepness outside 1 to 20 and too few readings for two-way filtering are refused. | [Gustafsson 1996](https://doi.org/10.1109/78.492552) |
| `sdata.frequency_filter()` | A Butterworth filter of order 4 run both ways, as table.add_filtered_column. | As table.add_filtered_column. | **P5.** As table.add_filtered_column. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As table.add_filtered_column. | [Gustafsson 1996](https://doi.org/10.1109/78.492552) |
| `sdata.frequency_filter()` `analog=` | SciPy's design with analog=True at 2 pi x the cutoff: the prototype (buttap ...) moved to the cutoff by lp2lp, lp2hp, lp2bp or lp2bs. | test_method=, steepness=, find_order_for=. | **P3.** SciPy's analog design, as R gsignal::butter(..., "s"); cutoffs stay in cycles per unit of time like the filter words. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** time_step= and remove_at= are refused with analog=True. |  |
| `sdata.frequency_filter()` `find_order_for=` | As numbers.keep_each_frequency_that(find_order_for=). | steepness=. | **P5.** As numbers.keep_each_frequency_that(find_order_for=). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** As numbers.keep_each_frequency_that(find_order_for=). |  |
| `table.add_envelope_column()` | The envelope: the size of the analytic signal from the Hilbert transform (SciPy signal.hilbert, as R gsignal::hilbert), in order of sorted_by=. | show="phase" (degrees, unwrapped) or "frequency"; for_each_group_in=. | **P3.** The analytic signal is the standard way to read the size of a wave at each moment; it is SciPy's and R gsignal's definition. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The readings must be evenly spaced and filled in. |  |
| `numbers.take_fourier_transform()` | The half spectrum (SciPy rfft, frequency 0 up to the highest the readings can show), sizes and phases in degrees. | show="all" (SciPy fft, negative frequencies too); time_step=. | **P3.** For real numbers the negative frequencies mirror the positive ones, so the half spectrum holds everything. Degrees are easier to read than radians. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty values are refused. |  |
| `numbers.take_fourier_transform()` `over_time=` | SciPy ShortTimeFFT without scaling (scale_to=None): the plain Fourier transform of each weighted piece of min(256, n) readings, half overlap, periodic Hann weights, the pieces at the ends padded with zeros. | window=, window_shape=. | **P3.** The piece length, overlap and window are those of SciPy's legacy stft, but the sizes are not divided by the sum of the weights as stft does: they are the sizes of the plain transform, as take_fourier_transform gives for the whole series. The padding lets istft give every reading back. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** window= from 4 to n. |  |
| `numbers.take_fourier_transform()` `between=` | SciPy zoom_fft at how_many=100 frequencies from low to high, both ends included. | how_many=. | **P5.** endpoint=True so both ends asked for are shown; 100 frequencies is a starting value. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** between= must lie within 0 and half the readings per unit of time. |  |
| `sdata.find_inverse_fourier_transform()` | SciPy irfft with the number of readings remembered from the transform (ifft for a full spectrum). | None: the table says which transform it came from. | **P3.** An odd number of readings cannot be told from the half spectrum, so the count travels with the table and a round trip gives the numbers back. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A table that did not come from take_fourier_transform is refused. |  |
| `numbers.find_coherence_with()` | Welch's method (SciPy signal.coherence: Hann window, half-overlapping segments of 256 readings); a recording shorter than 1152 readings is cut into eight half-overlapping segments (as MATLAB mscohere). | time_step= or sampling_rate= for the frequencies. | **P4.** Coherence is an average over segments (Welch 1967): with one segment it is 1 at every frequency, so short recordings take MATLAB's default of eight segments instead of SciPy's fixed 256 readings. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The two recordings must have the same length, at least 16 readings and no empty values. | [Welch 1967](https://doi.org/10.1109/TAU.1967.1161901) |
| `numbers.take_cosine_transform()` | The type-2 discrete cosine transform with orthonormal scaling (SciPy fft.dct(type=2, norm="ortho")). | None; SciPy's other types and scalings stay in SciPy (the manual shows the call). | **P3.** Type 2 is the DCT of Ahmed et al. (1974) and of image and sound compression; the orthonormal scaling keeps the sum of squares, so the sizes can be compared with the numbers. **Checks:** Empty values are refused. | [Ahmed et al. 1974](https://doi.org/10.1109/T-C.1974.223784) |

## Planning a study

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` | 80 % power, significance 0.05, two-sided, two groups of equal size; the two-sample t-test formula (as R power.t.test(), counting only the right direction for a two-sided test); the answer rounded up. Three or more groups: one-way ANOVA (Cohen's f); counts: chi-square (Cohen's w), or two percents (R power.prop.test). | power=, significance=, sides=, groups=, for_test=, percents=, same_within=, interval_width=, based_on=, using=, outcome=, test_method=, in_clusters_of=, drawing_from=. | **P1.** 80 % power at a two-sided 5 % level is the convention of Cohen 1988 and G*Power (Faul et al. 2009); two-sided tests are the regulatory norm (ICH E9). The effect to plan for is the smallest that matters (Lenth 2001), never the one a finished study found (Hoenig & Heisey 2001). **Checks:** Power not above the significance, effects of 0, and one-sided F and chi-square tests are refused; notes for the equal-spread formula (Welch in the test) and for no Yates correction. | [Cohen 1988](https://doi.org/10.4324/9780203771587); [Faul et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.3758/BRM.41.4.1149); [ICH E9 1998](https://database.ich.org/sites/default/files/E9_Guideline.pdf); [Lenth 2001](https://doi.org/10.1198/000313001317098149); [Hoenig & Heisey 2001](https://doi.org/10.1198/000313001300339897) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `interval_width=` | The Wilson interval, named explicitly (R presize::prec_prop(method = "wilson")); for an average presize::prec_mean. | There is no other interval here. | **P2.** Wilson's interval keeps its coverage better than the Wald interval (Brown, Cai & DasGupta 2001); presize's own method= is a list of four and warns when none is chosen (Haynes et al. 2021). **Checks:** spread= is required for an average. | [Brown et al. 2001](https://doi.org/10.1214/ss/1009213286); [Haynes et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.21105/joss.03118) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `for_test=` | For for_test="compare_survival": the events needed by Schoenfeld's formula for the log-rank test (R gsDesign::nEvents()), then the people from the share expected to have the event. | test_method="freedman". | **P2.** Survival power depends on the number of events, not of people (Schoenfeld 1983; Hsieh & Lavori 2000), so the events are printed first. **Checks:** A hazard ratio of 1 or below 0 is refused; the printout says it assumes proportional hazards and plans no recruitment time or drop-out. | [Schoenfeld 1983](https://doi.org/10.2307/2531021); [Hsieh & Lavori 2000](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0197-2456(00)00104-5) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `test_method=` | With test_method="freedman": Freedman's (1982) formula, two groups of the same size. | Leave test_method= out for Schoenfeld's formula. | **P3.** Freedman 1982 is the other classic log-rank formula; it gives slightly larger numbers than Schoenfeld's. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Needs percents=. | [Freedman 1982](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.4780010204) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `using=` | Regression power on Cohen's f squared, statsmodels FTestPowerF2 (as R pwr::pwr.f2.test()); the rows are the denominator degrees of freedom plus the columns plus 1. | for_test="compare_models" with against=. | **P3.** Cohen 1988, chapter 9; the newer statsmodels class names the degrees of freedom the right way round. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One-sided tests are refused; each name counts as 1 degree of freedom, with a note. | [Cohen 1988](https://doi.org/10.4324/9780203771587); [Faul et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.3758/BRM.41.4.1149) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `outcome=` | For outcome="yes or no": Demidenko's (2007) formula with the variance correction (R pwrss), one bell-shaped column, the odds ratio per standard deviation. | by_running= on find_chance_of_finding for several columns. | **P2.** Demidenko 2007 showed that Hsieh's formula (Hsieh, Bloch & Larsen 1998) is inaccurate; the corrected formula gives G*Power's answer (337 in the G*Power manual's example). **Checks:** Several columns are refused. | [Demidenko 2007](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2771); [Hsieh et al. 1998](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19980730)17:14<1623::AID-SIM871>3.0.CO;2-S); [Faul et al. 2009](https://doi.org/10.3758/BRM.41.4.1149) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `in_clusters_of=` | The independent number times the design effect 1 + (m - 1) x ICC, rounded up, then whole clusters. | alike_within_clusters= the ICC. | **P1.** The design effect is the standard allowance for clustering (Donner, Birkett & Buck 1981; CONSORT for cluster trials, Campbell et al. 2012); unequal cluster sizes need more (Eldridge, Ashby & Kerry 2006), which a note says. **Checks:** An ICC outside 0 to 1 and clusters below 2 are refused; fewer than 10 clusters get a note. | [Donner et al. 1981](https://doi.org/10.1093/oxfordjournals.aje.a113261); [Campbell et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj.e5661); [Eldridge et al. 2006](https://doi.org/10.1093/ije/dyl129) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `based_on=` | Power by simulation with the very test compare_averages_of(based_on="ranks") runs (Mann-Whitney), 10000 tries (seed 1), normal data shifted by the effect; the smallest number that reaches the power. | tries=, with_seed=, drawing_from=. | **P2.** Simulation is the general answer when no formula fits the test that will be run (Arnold et al. 2011); the rank-test formulas need P(X < Y), which is rarely known (Noether 1987). SciPy's own significance default is 0.01, so simplydata passes its own. **Checks:** Two-sided only; the answer moves a little with the seed, which is said. | [Arnold et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-11-94); [Noether 1987](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1987.10478478) |
| `sdata.find_how_many_needed()` `same_within=` | Power of the TOST equivalence test (R TOSTER::power_t_TOST()), assuming a true difference of 0 unless difference= says otherwise. | difference=. | **P2.** Equivalence must be planned with its own test (Schuirmann 1987; Lakens 2017). A true difference of 0 is the most hopeful case; plan with difference= if you expect any difference. **Checks:** spread= is required; the difference must lie inside the margin. | [Schuirmann 1987](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01068419); [Lakens 2017](https://doi.org/10.1177/1948550617697177) |
| `sdata.find_chance_of_finding()` | Significance 0.05, two-sided, as R power.t.test() (strict = FALSE). | As find_how_many_needed. | **P1.** As find_how_many_needed (Cohen 1988; ICH E9). **Checks:** values_per_group= is required; a note when the power is below 80 %. | [Cohen 1988](https://doi.org/10.4324/9780203771587); [ICH E9 1998](https://database.ich.org/sites/default/files/E9_Guideline.pdf) |
| `sdata.find_chance_of_finding()` `drawing_from=` | Seeded simulation (seed 1, 10000 tries, drawn 1000 at a time) with the test compare_averages_of runs: Welch's t-test (based_on="ranks" gives Mann-Whitney). | tries=, with_seed=. | **P2.** Simulation answers the power of the test actually run for the distributions you expect (Arnold et al. 2011); the seed makes the answer repeat and the Monte Carlo interval is printed. **Checks:** Distribution objects only; effect options are refused with it. | [Arnold et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-11-94) |
| `sdata.find_chance_of_finding()` `by_running=` | Your function, run once per seed (1, 2, ... 500 by default); the p-values below significance= are counted. | tries=, with_seed=. | **P2.** Simulation of any study design (Arnold et al. 2011); the seeds make the answer repeat. **Checks:** A function that gives a different p for the same seed, or p outside 0 to 1, is refused. | [Arnold et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-11-94) |

## Planning and surveys

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.make_design_table()` | Every combination of the factors (a full factorial) in one random order (completely random), from R agricolae with R's own generator (kinds = "Mersenne-Twister", not agricolae's "Super-Duper"), and the standard order of R expand.grid(). | of_kind="randomised blocks", "latin square" or "split plot" (with whole_plots=). | **P2.** Randomising the order of the plots spreads unknown differences evenly over the treatments, and true repeats of each combination are needed to measure the noise (Hurlbert 1984; Casler 2015). Completely random is the simplest layout; blocks are better when the plots differ in a known way, which the word cannot know, so it is offered, not chosen. **Checks:** Factors need at least 2 levels and a name that is not one of the table's own columns; repeats= is required (at least 2 for blocks). | [Hurlbert 1984](https://doi.org/10.2307/1942661); [Casler 2015](https://doi.org/10.2134/agronj2013.0114) |
| `sdata.make_design_table()` `of_kind="randomised blocks"` | Each repeat is a block that holds every combination once, in its own random order (agricolae design.rcbd, or design.ab(design = "rcbd") for several factors). | of_kind="completely random" (the default), "latin square", "split plot". | **P2.** Blocks take known differences between groups of plots (benches, fields, days) out of the comparison (Casler 2015). **Checks:** repeats= is the number of blocks and must be at least 2. | [Casler 2015](https://doi.org/10.2134/agronj2013.0114) |
| `sdata.make_design_table()` `of_kind="latin square"` | One factor laid out in rows and columns so that each level is once in every row and every column (agricolae design.lsd). | of_kind="randomised blocks" when only one direction differs. | **P2.** A latin square removes differences in two directions at once (rows and columns of a field or a greenhouse) (Casler 2015). **Checks:** Exactly one factor with at least 3 levels; repeats= and whole_plots= are refused. | [Casler 2015](https://doi.org/10.2134/agronj2013.0114) |
| `sdata.make_design_table()` `of_kind="split plot"` | Whole plots in randomised blocks get the factor named in whole_plots=, and each whole plot is split into parts for the other factor (agricolae design.split(design = "rcbd")). | of_kind="randomised blocks" when both factors can change plot by plot. | **P2.** A split plot is the layout for a factor that is hard to change on small plots, such as irrigation; it has two sizes of error, so it must be analysed as a split plot (Casler 2015). **Checks:** Exactly two factors, and whole_plots= naming one of them. | [Casler 2015](https://doi.org/10.2134/agronj2013.0114) |
| `sdata.make_random_assignments()` | Permuted blocks from R blockrand::blockrand(): each block holds every group equally often, and without in_blocks_of= the block sizes are 2 and 3 times the number of groups, chosen at random block by block; R's generator is named in full (Mersenne-Twister, Inversion, Rejection) so that with_seed= repeats the list. | in_blocks_of= (any multiples of the number of groups, one size or several); for_each_of= (one list per centre); groups= (names or a number). | **P1.** Blocks keep the groups about the same size all the way through, and random block sizes stop recruiters from guessing the last places of a block (Schulz & Grimes 2002a, 2002b; Efird 2011). The sizes 2 and 3 times the number of groups are simplydata's own starting value: no study fixes them. **Checks:** Block sizes that are not a multiple of the number of groups are refused; the printout says the last block is completed and that the list must be kept from the people who recruit. | [Schulz & Grimes 2002a](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(02)07683-3); [Schulz & Grimes 2002b](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(02)07750-4); [Efird 2011](https://doi.org/10.3390/ijerph8010015) |
| `table.use_survey_design()` | R survey::svydesign() with the columns given; options(survey.lonely.psu = "adjust") and nest = TRUE; from then on the survey words give design-based answers and the other statistics words refuse. | table.use_survey_design() with no options turns it off. | **P1.** Analysing a complex sample as if it were a simple random sample gives standard errors that are too small (Lumley 2010; Korn & Graubard 1999). survey stops by default when a stratum has one cluster; "adjust" is the conservative option Lumley describes (Lumley 2010). **Checks:** Empty design cells and weights of 0 or less are refused; the printout says how the population sizes were read; a survey word refuses after rows were removed. | [Lumley 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/9780470580066); [Korn & Graubard 1999](https://doi.org/10.1002/9781118032619); [Lumley 2004](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v009.i08) |

## Join and reshape

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.join_with()` | keep="matching rows" (an inner join); a column in both tables gets "_other" in the other table's copy. | keep="all rows from this table" or "all rows from both". | **P4.** Data preparation: the sentence says how many rows found a match and warns when a key is repeated in the other table, so rows are not doubled unnoticed. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Keys of different kinds (text and numbers) are refused; repeated keys in the other table get a note. |  |
| `table.join_with()` `using=` | using={"name": "student"} pairs this table's column with the other table's; the key keeps this table's name only; check_that= runs pandas' and Polars' own validate= before anything is joined. | check_that= "each row has one match", "each key appears once in both" or "each key appears once here"; keep="all rows from the other table"; with_match_column=True. | **P4.** Data preparation: a key that repeats by mistake multiplies rows without a word; a check that names the repeated values stops it before the table changes. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A failed check names the repeated values and the table they are in; keys of different kinds are refused. |  |
| `table.join_with()` `match=` | "nearest before" (pandas merge_asof direction="backward"), both tables sorted by the date first and the rows put back in order; within= as a time such as "2 days". | "nearest after", "nearest"; keep="all rows from this table" keeps rows with nothing near enough. | **P4.** Data preparation: measurements at different times (a river and a rain gauge) are matched to the last reading before each one, which is what was known then; a limit stops a match to a reading weeks away. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty dates are refused, and dates or numbers are needed in both tables. |  |
| `table.join_with()` `where=` | Every pair of rows that meets the condition (Polars join_where); the rows keep this table's order, each followed by its partners, on both engines; a row without a partner is left out. | using= for joins on equal columns; match= for the nearest date; then keep_rows_where() for other conditions. | **P5.** Data handling: Polars join_where gives no order of its own, so simplydata makes every pair (a cross join) and keeps the ones that meet the condition, which keeps the table's order and gives the same rows on both engines. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** No using=, keep=, match=, within=, check_that= or with_match_column=; at most 20,000,000 pairs of rows. |  |
| `table.add_rows_from()` | Columns matched by their names; the new rows go under. | join_with() to add columns instead of rows. | **P4.** Data preparation: matching by name stops values landing in the wrong column when two files list them in another order. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Columns found in only one table, or holding different kinds of values, are refused with their names. |  |
| `table.gather_into_rows()` | The other columns stay beside each value; the new columns are called "column" and "value". | names_to=, values_to=. | **P5.** Data preparation: a long table is what charts and models take; plain names say what each new column holds. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Mixing text and number columns, or a name already in the table, is refused. |  |
| `table.gather_into_rows()` `starts_with=` | starts_with=, ends_with= and matches_pattern= choose the columns by their names as keep_columns does, in table order (capital letters matter). | A list of columns; one way at a time. | **P3.** Data preparation: wide files name repeated measures with a shared start (pain_week_0, pain_week_4); choosing them by it avoids typing every name. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** No match is refused with the column names there are. |  |
| `table.spread_into_columns()` | Rows and new columns stay in the order they first appear; all the other columns say which row is which. | names_from=, values_from=. | **P4.** Data preparation: keeping the table's own order (pandas would sort) and refusing to choose between two values for one cell keep the result honest. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** When two rows would fill one cell simplydata stops and suggests an average first; when another column differs between rows that would merge, so that the new columns get empty cells, a note names that column. |  |
| `table.spread_into_columns()` `show=` | Rows and new columns stay in the order they first appear; a pair that never appears stays an empty cell for every show= (pandas pivot_table(aggfunc=), Polars pivot(aggregate_function=)). | show= "average", "total", "median", "smallest", "biggest" or "count". | **P4.** Data preparation: Polars gives 0 for a total of no rows, which reads as a measurement; an empty cell keeps "not measured" apart from "measured zero". *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Without show=, repeated pairs stop the word with their names, so nothing is combined without your word. |  |
| `table.swap_rows_and_columns()` | The first column names the new columns when it holds text with no repeat and no empty cell (pandas set_index(first).T); otherwise the new columns are numbered "1", "2" ... and the old column names go in "column". | Keep or reorder the columns first (keep_columns) to choose which column names the rows. | **P5.** Data preparation: a table read with its measurements as rows becomes one row per thing measured; using the first text column as names keeps the row labels, as in pandas and R t() on a data frame with row names. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A table with no rows or no columns is refused; a first column that cannot name columns gives a note. |  |
| `table.keep_rows_found_in()` | A semi join: the rows whose key is in the other table stay, in their order; no column comes in and no row is repeated (pandas isin, Polars join(how="semi")). | join_with() to bring the other table's columns in; remove_rows_found_in() for the rows that are not there. | **P4.** Data preparation: learners who only want to keep the matching rows reach for a join and get repeated rows when a key appears twice; a word that cannot repeat a row keeps the count of rows honest. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Keys of different kinds (text and numbers) are refused with the step that fixes them; an empty key is never found, and a note says how many rows had one. |  |
| `table.remove_rows_found_in()` | An anti join: the rows whose key is not in the other table stay, in their order (pandas ~isin, Polars join(how="anti")). | keep_rows_found_in() for the opposite. | **P4.** Data preparation: finding what one table lacks (samples with no lab result) is the usual check before a join. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Keys of different kinds are refused; a row with an empty key is never found, so it stays, and a note says so. |  |
| `table.add_missing_combinations_of()` | Every combination of the values found in the columns gets a row; the rows come out sorted by those columns, a combination that is there twice keeps both rows, and a row with an empty key stays, at the end. | add_missing_dates() for a run of dates. | **P5.** Data preparation: pandas reindex refuses repeated keys and R tidyr::complete() keeps them; keeping every row already there means the word only ever adds rows. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sentence says how many rows were added and how many combinations there are. |  |
| `table.spread_into_yes_no_columns()` | One True/False column for each value, named "column: value", A to Z, after the other columns; the column itself stays; empty cells get a column of their own, "column: (empty)", last, named as the cross tables of count_rows name them (pandas get_dummies, Polars to_dummies). | separator= for cells that hold several values (pandas str.get_dummies). | **P5.** Data preparation: pandas names the column of empty cells "nan" and Polars "null"; one plain name on both engines keeps the same table on both (approved by Manish 2026-11-25). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Text columns only; a column that holds the value "(empty)" next to empty cells is refused, with the fill_empty_cells_in line that fixes it; a new name that already exists is refused. |  |
| `table.make_one_table_for_each_group_in()` | Groups in the order they are first seen, rows in their order, an empty value a group of its own; one column's tables are found by the value itself. | for_each_group_in= on most words works group by group without splitting the table. | **P3.** Data preparation: pandas groupby sorts the groups and drops empty keys by default, and Polars partition_by keeps them; one rule for both keeps every row. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.run_sql()` | DuckDB, with the table called data; no extensions loaded or installed on their own, no files, the settings locked, and one SELECT only. Without DuckDB the word stops with the install line. | sdata.run_sql() for queries on files; the table words for the same jobs without SQL. | **P4.** Privacy: simplydata is used on sensitive data, so a query must never fetch from or send to the web, open a file, install anything or write a file; DuckDB's own settings and its own parser's statement type enforce it. Polars SQL is not used as a stand-in: its read_csv and read_parquet table functions can open any file. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A query with a web or cloud address, more than one statement, or anything but a SELECT is refused before it runs. |  |
| `sdata.run_sql()` | DuckDB on the files of this computer; no extensions loaded or installed on their own, the settings locked, and one SELECT only. | table.run_sql() on a table already read; sdata.table() to read a file whole. | **P4.** Privacy: the query reads local files only, so data never leave the computer; DuckDB reads only the rows and columns a query needs, which keeps big files quick. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** An address such as https:// or s3:// is refused before DuckDB sees it; so are statements that are not a SELECT. |  |

## Undo and finish

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `table.save()` | The file holds only the columns, without row labels. | The end of the file name chooses the type (.xlsx, .csv, .tsv, .parquet, .json). | **P5.** Data handling: row labels are pandas' own and not part of the data. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `table.save()` `in_sheet=` | The sheet is added to the Excel file and the other sheets are kept; a sheet of the same name is replaced; a file that does not exist is made with that one sheet. | Without in_sheet=, the whole file is replaced by one sheet. | **P5.** Data handling: pandas' own append mode (ExcelWriter mode="a", if_sheet_exists="replace"); replacing the named sheet, not the file, keeps the other sheets, and simplydata says when it replaced one. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Sheet names are checked with Excel's rules before the file is opened (at most 31 characters, none of : \ / ? * [ ], no apostrophe at the start or end, not History, not the same name in other capitals), so a refused name leaves the file as it was. |  |
| `table.save()` `in_table=` | Replace that one table and keep the other tables of the database file (pandas to_sql(if_exists="replace"), no row labels); DuckDB files through DuckDB with no extensions. | in_sheet= for Excel; sdata.table(table_name=) to read it back. | **P4.** Data preparation: mirrors in_sheet=, so several tables go into one file and saving again never removes the others. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A database file without in_table= is refused with an example. |  |
| `table.make_copy()` | An independent copy that keeps the steps so far (backup.undo() and backup.show_steps() work on it); steps on one table never change the other. pandas df.copy() (free until one changes under copy-on-write), Polars df.clone(). | sdata.table(table) starts a new table from this one without its steps; table.start_again() goes back to the table as it was read. | **P5.** A learner who writes backup = table expects a copy and gets a second name for the same table (the Python behaviour that surprised users in the 2026-11-23 study); a word that says copy makes the difference visible, as pandas copy() and Polars clone() do. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** copy.copy(table) and copy.deepcopy(table) give the same copy. |  |

## Numbers: make and look

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.number_list()` | With to=, the end is included; the count of steps allows a little room for rounding. | step=, how_many= (evenly spaced, as np.linspace). | **P5.** Making numbers: "1 to 10" includes 10 in everyday speech and in R's seq(); NumPy's arange leaves the end out and can overshoot with a decimal step. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `sdata.make_random_numbers()` | NumPy's random generator (default_rng) with the seed given; whole numbers include both ends. | with_seed= for the same numbers every time. | **P3.** NumPy recommends its Generator over the old global seed; "between 1 and 6" includes 6, like a dice. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.show()` | Every number, with commas between them, however many there are. | show_first_numbers() and show_last_numbers() for the ends only. | **P5.** Showing results, not a method: a word called show hides nothing, and commas make the list read like the one you typed. NumPy's print shortens long arrays to their ends. *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `numbers.show_first_numbers()` | The first 5 numbers, with commas, none hidden. | how_many= any whole number. | **P5.** Showing results: the same look as show(). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** how_many= must be a whole number. |  |
| `numbers.show_last_numbers()` | The last 5 numbers, with commas, none hidden. | how_many= any whole number. | **P5.** Showing results: the same look as show(); 0 shows none. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** how_many= must be a whole number. |  |
| `numbers.get_number_at()` | Positions count from 1; "last" is the last number. | None: Python's [ ] is not used on simplydata numbers. | **P5.** People count from 1, and so do charts' x axes and R; counting from 0 is a frequent beginner's error. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A position outside 1 to the count stops with a message that gives the count. |  |
| `numbers.count()` | How many places there are, empty values included. | count() minus count_empty_numbers() for the filled ones. | **P3.** The same as len() in Python and length() in R; the empty values are counted on their own. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Numbers: maths in words

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `numbers.divide_by()` | Dividing by 0 gives an empty value (nan) and a note saying how many, not inf. | None; fill the empty values afterwards if a number is wanted there. | **P4.** There is no answer to dividing by 0. An empty value is skipped by the summary words, while inf would make a total or an average inf. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** The note counts the numbers that became empty. |  |
| `numbers.raise_to_power()` | Whole numbers to a minus power are worked out as decimals; 0 to a minus power and a negative number to a power with decimals become empty, with a note. | None. | **P4.** Neither has an answer among ordinary numbers; NumPy stops with an error for whole numbers to a minus power and gives inf for 0 to a minus power. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** The note says how many numbers became empty and why. |  |
| `numbers.round_to()` | A number exactly halfway goes to the even neighbour (0.5 to 0, 2.5 to 2). | None; with_decimals= sets the places (negative rounds to tens, hundreds). | **P3.** The same as NumPy, pandas and R round(): halves do not all go up, so a total of rounded numbers is not pushed up. Excel rounds halves away from 0. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** with_decimals= must be a whole number. |  |
| `numbers.take_log()` | The natural log; 0 and negative numbers become empty, with a note. | base=10, base=2 or any number above 0 but not 1. | **P4.** The log only exists above 0; an empty value is skipped by later summaries, while -inf would spoil them. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The note says how many numbers became empty. |  |
| `numbers.turn_into_running_total()` | An empty value stays empty and is skipped in the total. | fill_empty_numbers() first to count it as a number. | **P3.** The same as pandas cumsum(): one empty value does not empty every total after it, and its place stays visible. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.take_function()` | The plain name comes from one name table ("bessel J" is SciPy special.jv, and "jv" works too); the numbers go where SciPy's x goes and the other inputs are options with plain names (order=). | SciPy's or NumPy's own name; take_function() with no name lists every function. | **P5.** One name table for take_function and formula text; the numbers are SciPy's own. SciPy's own default (scipy.special), so the numbers agree with SciPy's; no study fixes this choice. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Unknown options and missing inputs are refused, naming the options; where a function has no value the answer is empty and a note says how many. |  |
| `numbers.take_function()` `part=` | simplydata numbers are real numbers: a complex answer gives its real part unless part= asks for "imaginary", "size" (np.abs) or "angle" (np.angle); the sentence says which. | part="imaginary", "size" or "angle". | **P5.** The real part is the plain reading of a complex answer, and the sentence names it so nothing is lost unknowingly. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** part= only for functions with complex answers; other values are refused. |  |
| `numbers.find_prime_factors()` | Each prime factor with how many times it divides the number (SymPy factorint); a number with a decimal part is the fraction it stands for, its bottom's primes with minus times (factorrat); 1 has no prime factor, and a number below 0 has the primes of its size, its minus sign said and not listed as a prime; 0 is refused, as every prime divides it. | show="different primes" (primefactors) lists each prime once. | **P3.** factorint is exact for every whole number a list of numbers holds (up to 2**53). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** No empty numbers, none above 2**53. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |

## Numbers: choose, sort and fill

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `numbers.keep_each_number_that()` | is_above= is strictly above; empty values match only is_empty=True. | is_at_least=, is_between= (both ends included) and the other conditions. | **P5.** The same conditions as table.keep_rows_where, so one rule holds everywhere. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.remove_each_number_that()` | is_below= is strictly below; an empty value matches no condition, so it stays. | is_at_most= for <=; is_empty=True removes the empty values. | **P5.** The same rule as table.remove_rows_where: a condition on a number says nothing about a missing number. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One condition at a time. |  |
| `numbers.keep_random_numbers()` | NumPy's default_rng with the seed given, picking without repeats; the numbers kept stay in their order. | with_seed= any whole number; leave it out for a new pick each time. | **P3.** default_rng is the generator NumPy recommends; keeping the order keeps a series a series. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Asking for more numbers than there are keeps them all, with a note. |  |
| `numbers.remove_duplicate_numbers()` | Each number once, in its first place; empty values count as one value. | sort() afterwards for the numbers in order. | **P3.** The same as pandas duplicated(keep="first"): the order of the list is kept, where np.unique sorts. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.keep_numbers_by_position()` | Positions count from 1 and both ends are kept. | to=, step= or a list of positions. | **P5.** People count from 1 and say "2 to 4" for three numbers, as R's 2:4 does. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Positions outside the list stop with a message; to= past the end is cut, with a note. |  |
| `numbers.remove_numbers_by_position()` | Positions count from 1 and both ends go. | to=, step=, a list of positions or "last". | **P5.** The same positions as keep_numbers_by_position. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Removing every number is refused. |  |
| `numbers.replace_each_number_that()` | is_above= is strictly above; an empty value matches no condition, so it stays empty. | is_empty=True to replace the empty values; with_value=None makes numbers empty. | **P5.** The same rule as the other condition words: a condition on a number says nothing about a missing number. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One condition, or one old value, at a time. |  |
| `numbers.fill_empty_numbers()` | with_the="line between neighbours" fills each gap on the straight line between its neighbours, spaced by position; empty values at the start or end stay empty, with a note. | with_value=, or with_the="average", "median", "value above" or "value below". | **P3.** The same as pandas interpolate(limit_area="inside"): a value outside the filled ones would be a guess beyond the data. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Exactly one of with_value= and with_the=. |  |
| `numbers.sort()` | Smallest first; empty values last, also with biggest_first=True. | biggest_first=True. | **P3.** The same as pandas sort_values(na_position="last") and R sort(na.last = TRUE): the numbers come first either way. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Numbers: summaries

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `numbers.find_average()` | The mean, empty values skipped. | remove_empty_numbers() first, to see how many there were. | **P3.** As R mean(na.rm = TRUE) and pandas: the sentence says how many numbers were used. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.find_median()` | The middle value, empty values skipped (np.nanmedian); NumPy's np.median would give nan. | remove_empty_numbers() first if you want them gone for later steps too. | **P3.** Skipping empty values and saying so is what pandas and R's median(na.rm = TRUE) do. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.find_total()` | Empty values skipped (np.nansum), with a note saying how many. | remove_empty_numbers() first if you want them gone for later steps too. | **P3.** pandas and R's sum(na.rm = TRUE) skip empty values too; simplydata also says how many. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.find_smallest()` | Empty values skipped (np.nanmin), with a note saying how many. | remove_empty_numbers() first if you want them gone for later steps too. | **P3.** pandas and R's min(na.rm = TRUE) skip empty values too; simplydata also says how many. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.find_biggest()` | Empty values skipped (np.nanmax), with a note saying how many. | remove_empty_numbers() first if you want them gone for later steps too. | **P3.** pandas and R's max(na.rm = TRUE) skip empty values too; simplydata also says how many. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `numbers.find_standard_deviation()` | The sample standard deviation, dividing by n - 1, empty values skipped. | There is no other kind here. | **P1.** As table.find_standard_deviation_of: the n - 1 form of the GUM, R and pandas (NumPy's own default divides by n). **Checks:** Fewer than 2 values give an empty answer, with a note. | [JCGM 100:2008 (GUM)](https://www.bipm.org/documents/20126/2071204/JCGM_100_2008_E.pdf) |
| `numbers.find_percentile()` | Linear interpolation (R quantile(type = 7)), empty values skipped. | table.find_percentile_of(test_method=) for the other types. | **P3.** As table.find_percentile_of (Hyndman & Fan 1996). *Basis: convention.* | [Hyndman & Fan 1996](https://doi.org/10.1080/00031305.1996.10473566) |
| `numbers.count_each()` | Empty values counted as a value of their own; percents out of all numbers, rounded to 1 decimal; biggest count first, equal counts smallest number first, empty last. | remove_empty_numbers() first to leave the empty values out. | **P5.** Showing results: every number is accounted for, so the percents add up to 100 before rounding, and the order is the same every time. *Basis: starting value.* |  |

## Grids of numbers

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.number_grid()` | A list is cut into rows, row after row. | rows= or columns=; a list of rows for a grid as it is. | **P3.** As NumPy's reshape (order "C"), which reads a list the way text is read; R's matrix() fills by column unless byrow = TRUE. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `sdata.number_grid()` `exactly=` | A grid of exact cells (SymPy Matrix): a typed decimal is the exact fraction it stands for (0.2 is 1/5), text is read as a formula ("1/3", "a", "x**2"); up to 12 x 12. | Without exactly=True: a grid of decimals (NumPy). | **P3.** As in the other exact words (exact_number): the fraction a decimal stands for is what the researcher typed; 12 x 12 keeps exact determinants and inverses within the time limit. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Every row the same length; numbers and formula text only. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `numbers.make_number_grid()` | Filled row after row (NumPy's order "C"). | rows= or columns= (or both). | **P3.** Rows are read the way text is read; it is NumPy's own order. R's matrix() fills column by column unless byrow = TRUE. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The numbers must fill the grid exactly. |  |
| `grid.show_first_rows()` | The first 5 rows, with commas, none hidden. | rows= any whole number. | **P5.** Showing results: the same look as show(). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** rows= must be a whole number. |  |
| `grid.show_last_rows()` | The last 5 rows, with commas, none hidden. | rows= any whole number. | **P5.** Showing results: the same look as show(). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** rows= must be a whole number. |  |
| `grid.get_number_at()` | Rows and columns count from 1; "last" is the last one. | None: Python's [ ] is not used on grids. | **P5.** As numbers.get_number_at: people count from 1, and so does R. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A row or column outside the grid stops with a message. |  |
| `grid.get_row()` | Rows count from 1; the row comes back as new numbers (a copy). | "last" for the bottom row. | **P5.** As numbers.get_number_at; a copy means a later step on the row never changes the grid. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A row outside the grid stops with a message. |  |
| `grid.get_column()` | Columns count from 1 at the left; the column comes back as new numbers (a copy). | "last" for the right-hand column. | **P5.** As grid.get_row. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A column outside the grid stops with a message. |  |
| `grid.get_all_numbers()` | Row by row (NumPy's order "C"). | swap_rows_and_columns() first for column by column. | **P3.** The same order as make_number_grid fills, so the two undo each other. *Basis: convention.* |  |
| `grid.keep_rows_by_position()` | Rows count from 1 and both ends are kept; the result stays a grid. | to=, step= or a list of rows. | **P5.** As numbers.keep_numbers_by_position. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Value conditions are refused: rows are chosen by place. |  |
| `grid.keep_columns_by_position()` | Columns count from 1 at the left and both ends are kept. | to=, step= or a list of columns. | **P5.** As numbers.keep_numbers_by_position. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Value conditions are refused: columns are chosen by place. |  |
| `grid.remove_rows_by_position()` | Rows count from 1. | to=, step= or a list of rows. | **P5.** As grid.keep_rows_by_position. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Removing every row is refused. |  |
| `grid.remove_columns_by_position()` | Columns count from 1 at the left. | to=, step= or a list of columns. | **P5.** As grid.keep_columns_by_position. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Removing every column is refused. |  |
| `grid.replace_each_number_that()` | Numbers are changed where they are, so the grid keeps its rows and columns; an empty value matches no condition. | is_empty=True for the empty values; with_value=None makes numbers empty. | **P5.** A grid cannot lose single numbers (its rows would differ in length), so the numbers are replaced, never dropped. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One condition at a time; keep_each_number_that is refused on a grid, with what to use. |  |

## Grids: pairs, patches and matrices

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `grid.get_numbers_at_pairs()` | Positions count from 1, as everywhere in simplydata; 1 is taken off for NumPy's indexing. | keep_rows_by_position then keep_columns_by_position for the rectangle of the rows and columns. | **P5.** One way of counting in every word; the sentence gives NumPy's own positions too. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Whole numbers inside the grid, as many rows as columns. |  |
| `grid.get_numbers_from_each_row_at()` | Positions count from 1, as everywhere in simplydata; 1 is taken off for NumPy's take_along_axis. | A list (one position for each row) or a grid (several). | **P5.** One way of counting in every word. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Whole numbers inside the grid, one row of positions for each row. |  |
| `grid.find_each_patch_that()` | Cells that share a side touch (SciPy ndimage.label's own default); the centre of a patch counts each cell the same, from 1. | touching="corners" lets a shared corner count too. | **P3.** Side-touching (4-connectivity) is the default of SciPy label and of image analysis; an unweighted centre is where the patch lies, whatever its values. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty cells are never in a patch. |  |
| `grid.find_each_patch_that()` `show=` | Positions count from 1; the standard deviation and variance have n in the bottom (SciPy ndimage, as NumPy). | table.find_summary_of on the cells of one patch for the sample standard deviation. | **P5.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** show= values from the list; bins= with "counts in bins" only. |  |
| `grid.change_size_to()` | Straight lines between neighbouring cells (SciPy ndimage.zoom, order=1). | test_method="nearest" or "cubic" (SciPy's own default, order=3). | **P5.** Linear interpolation stays between the neighbouring values; a cubic spline can overshoot them (for example below 0 next to a 0), which is easy to miss in a map of concentrations. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Empty cells are refused. |  |
| `grid.rotate()` | The grid keeps its size; straight lines between neighbouring cells (order=1); cells from outside the old grid are empty (SciPy ndimage.rotate, reshape=False, cval=nan). | test_method="nearest" or "cubic". | **P4.** SciPy fills the outside with 0, which would read as a measured 0; empty says nothing was there. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Empty cells are refused; the sentence says how many became empty. |  |
| `grid.take_matrix_exp()` | SciPy linalg.expm (scaling and squaring with a Pade approximation). | None: R Matrix::expm gives the same answer. | **P3.** The method of Al-Mohy & Higham (2009) is the standard in SciPy and MATLAB. **Checks:** A square grid without empty cells. | [Al-Mohy & Higham 2009](https://doi.org/10.1137/09074721X) |
| `grid.find_null_space()` `exactly=` | SymPy Matrix.nullspace on the numbers as fractions; each direction is one column of the new grid, as without exactly=True. | Without exactly=True: SciPy linalg.null_space (columns of length 1 at right angles). | **P5.** SymPy's directions are its simplest whole numbers and fractions, easy to read; the sentence says they are not of length 1, so they are not mistaken for SciPy's. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Grids up to 12 x 12 with a number in every cell. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `grid.find_determinant()` `exactly=` | Each number of the grid becomes the simplest fraction whose decimal is exactly that number (SymPy nsimplify, rational=True: 0.2 is 1/5); a number no short fraction gives back exactly (4 + 1e-15) keeps its exact binary value instead of being rounded to 15 digits. Then SymPy Matrix.det works exactly. | Without exactly=True: SciPy linalg.det (LU decomposition, LAPACK). | **P5.** A decimal such as 0.2 cannot be held exactly by a float; the fraction it was typed as is what the reader means. Rounding every number to 15 digits first would change a number such as 4 + 1e-15 and could turn a grid with an inverse into one without, so such a number is kept exactly as stored. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Grids up to 12 x 12 with a number in every cell. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `grid.take_inverse()` `exactly=` | The exact inverse from SymPy (the numbers as fractions) is printed as fractions; the grid keeps their decimals, so the other grid words work on it. | Without exactly=True: SciPy linalg.inv (LAPACK). | **P5.** A grid holds numbers, so the fractions are shown for reading and the decimals kept for working on. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A grid whose determinant is exactly 0 is refused; grids up to 12 x 12. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `grid.find_eigenvalues()` | A grid symmetric to within R isSymmetric's tolerance (100 x machine epsilon) uses SciPy linalg.eigvalsh (real eigenvalues always), biggest first; any other grid linalg.eigvals, biggest size (modulus) first: the order of R eigen(). | exactly=True: SymPy eigenvals, for grids up to 6 x 6. | **P3.** LAPACK's symmetric eigensolver (Anderson et al. 1999) is faster and more accurate for symmetric grids and never gives a tiny imaginary part by rounding; biggest first matches R and the order readers expect. **Checks:** A square grid with no empty cells. | [Anderson et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898719604) |
| `grid.find_eigenvalues()` `exactly=` | SymPy Matrix.eigenvals on the numbers as exact fractions, in the order of R eigen() (as without exactly=True), with how many times each comes; a real eigenvalue has no imaginary part, not a rounding remainder. | Without exactly=True: SciPy linalg.eigvalsh or eigvals. | **P5.** SymPy gives the eigenvalues as a dict without an order; biggest first is the order of the numeric word and R eigen(). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Grids up to 6 x 6: the roots of a polynomial of higher degree seldom have an exact formula. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `grid.find_eigenvalues()` `structure=` | Biggest first, as R eigen() for a symmetric grid; "banded" uses SciPy eigvals_banded, "tridiagonal" eigvalsh_tridiagonal. | Without structure=: linalg.eigvalsh or eigvals on the whole grid. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A symmetric grid; "tridiagonal" needs one diagonal each side at most. |  |
| `grid.solve_for()` | A square grid: SciPy linalg.solve (the LU decomposition, LAPACK gesv). A grid that is not square: least squares, SciPy linalg.lstsq (LAPACK gelsd), the shortest answer when the columns depend on each other; the sentence says which. | with_best_fit=True for least squares on a square grid too; between= for limits; exactly=True for SymPy's exact answer. | **P3.** LAPACK's LU solver is what R solve(), MATLAB a \ b and NumPy use (Anderson et al. 1999); a grid with more rows than columns has no exact answer, and least squares is the standard best fit (Lawson & Hanson 1995), as MATLAB a \ b gives for a grid that is not square. **Checks:** A grid with no empty cells; b needs one number per row; a square grid with determinant 0 is refused with with_best_fit=True named. | [Anderson et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898719604); [Lawson & Hanson 1995](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611971217) |
| `grid.solve_for()` `between=` | between=[0, None]: non-negative least squares, SciPy optimize.nnls (the active-set method of Lawson & Hanson). Other limits: SciPy optimize.lsq_linear with method="bvls" (bounded-variable least squares). | Without between=: least squares with no limits (linalg.lstsq). | **P3.** The active-set methods end at the exact answer of the problem with limits (no iteration tolerance to choose), as R nnls and MATLAB lsqnonneg (Lawson & Hanson 1995). **Checks:** Two values, each a number or None, the low one first; one right side only; not with exactly=True. | [Lawson & Hanson 1995](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611971217) |
| `grid.solve_for()` `exactly=` | The numbers become the fractions they stand for (SymPy nsimplify, rational=True: 0.2 is 1/5), then SymPy LUsolve (a square grid) or solve_least_squares (a grid that is not square); x keeps the decimals of the exact answer. | Without exactly=True: SciPy linalg.solve or linalg.lstsq. | **P5.** The same reading of typed decimals as find_determinant(exactly=True); the fractions are printed for reading and the decimals kept for working on. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Grids up to 12 x 12; a square grid with determinant exactly 0, or a grid whose columns depend on each other, is refused. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `grid.solve_for()` `structure=` | The band is read with SciPy linalg.bandwidth and put in LAPACK's banded storage; a symmetric grid with positive eigenvalues uses Cholesky on the band (cholesky_banded, cho_solve_banded), any other banded grid solve_banded. | Without structure=: SciPy linalg.solve on the whole grid. | **P3.** Cholesky is the standard method for a symmetric positive definite band, LU with pivoting (gbsv) otherwise (Anderson et al. 1999). **Checks:** A square grid; not with between=, with_best_fit= or exactly=. | [Anderson et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898719604) |
| `grid.break_down()` | The singular value decomposition (SciPy linalg.svd), in its short (economic) form: U has as many columns as the grid, as R svd(). | test_method="qr", "lu", "cholesky", "schur", "polar", "ldl", "hessenberg" or "qz". | **P3.** Every grid, square or not, has a singular value decomposition, so the word works without a choice; the short form leaves out columns that multiply only zeros (Anderson et al. 1999). **Checks:** A grid with no empty cells. | [Anderson et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898719604) |
| `grid.break_down()` `test_method=` | Each method is SciPy linalg's own call with its own defaults: lu gives grid = P L U, cholesky the upper triangle (as R chol() and MATLAB chol), schur the real form; qr is the short (economic) form, as R qr(); ldl's row order counts from 1, as every position in simplydata. | Without test_method=: the singular value decomposition. | **P3.** SciPy's defaults are LAPACK's (Anderson et al. 1999), which R and MATLAB also call, so the parts agree with theirs up to the signs of columns where a decomposition leaves them free. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** cholesky and ldl need a symmetric grid; cholesky needs eigenvalues above 0; schur, polar, ldl, hessenberg and qz need a square grid. | [Anderson et al. 1999](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9780898719604) |
| `grid.break_down()` `test_method="rq"` | The short (economic) form of SciPy linalg.rq, as for qr. | test_method="qr" for Q times R. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A grid with no empty cells. |  |
| `grid.break_down()` `test_method="cs"` | The four blocks are cut after half the rows and half the columns, unless split_at= says where (SciPy cossin's p and q). | split_at=[rows, columns]. | **P5.** SciPy cossin needs p and q; half of each is the even split. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A square grid whose columns have length 1 and are at right angles. |  |
| `grid.break_down()` `test_method="real eigen blocks"` | The eigenvalues and eigenvectors of SciPy linalg.eig, turned into real 2 x 2 blocks by linalg.cdf2rdf. | test_method="schur" for the real Schur form, find_eigenvalues() for the eigenvalues alone. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A square grid. |  |
| `grid.break_down()` `test_method="jordan"` | The numbers become exact fractions (SymPy nsimplify), then SymPy Matrix.jordan_form, so the Jordan form is exact. | test_method="real eigen blocks" or "schur" in decimals. | **P5.** The Jordan form cannot be worked out reliably in decimals (a small change in the numbers changes its blocks), so it is exact or not at all. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Real eigenvalues only; SymPy's one time limit. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |
| `grid.take_matrix_function()` `power=` | A whole power uses NumPy linalg.matrix_power (repeated matrix products); any other power SciPy linalg.fractional_matrix_power. | take_matrix_function("square root") for the power 0.5 by SciPy sqrtm. | **P3.** Repeated products are exact for a whole power; a fractional power needs the Schur-Pade method of fractional_matrix_power. SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A square grid; an answer with complex numbers is refused. |  |
| `grid.take_matrix_function()` `function="a formula of x"` | Any formula of x goes to SciPy linalg.funm (the Schur-Parlett method), read safely by simplydata's formula reader. | The named functions ("log", "square root", "cos" ...) use SciPy's dedicated routines, which are more accurate. | **P3.** funm is the general matrix function of SciPy; the dedicated routines are preferred where they exist. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A formula of x; an answer with complex numbers is refused. |  |
| `grid.check_if_grid_is()` `within=` | Without within=, the grid must be exactly symmetric (SciPy issymmetric's own default); within= is an absolute tolerance. | within=1e-12 (or any number) to allow rounding. | **P5.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** within= is a number of 0 or more. |  |
| `grid.find_angles_to()` | The angles in degrees, biggest first (SciPy subspace_angles' order). | in_radians=True keeps SciPy's radians. | **P5.** Degrees are the everyday unit, as in grid.rotate(by_degrees=). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Both grids need the same number of rows. |  |
| `grid.find_edges()` `along=` | Without along=, the slope size in both directions (SciPy generic_gradient_magnitude); along= gives the signed change in one direction. | along="rows" or "columns". | **P5.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** test_method="sobel" or "prewitt". |  |
| `grid.change_patch_shapes()` | The neighbours touch by their sides (SciPy generate_binary_structure(2, 1), the default of binary_dilation), by= cells are by= steps (iterations=), and at the edges the edge cells are repeated (the grid padded 2 x by cells with NumPy pad "symmetric", SciPy's mode "reflect"), as for the grey picture, so closing never removes a cell. | touching="corners" lets a shared corner count too. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Patches from a condition, a yes or no grid or a grid of 0 and 1; any other grid changes as a grey picture. |  |
| `grid.change_patch_shapes()` `is_above=` | The patches are the cells that meet the condition; the grid becomes yes or no. | No condition: a yes or no grid uses its yes cells, a grid of other numbers changes as a grey picture. | **P5.** The same conditions as find_each_patch_that. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** One condition at a time. |  |
| `grid.change_patch_shapes()` `how="grow" on numbers` | Without a condition, a grid of numbers changes as a grey picture: the neighbours touch by their sides, grown to by= cells by SciPy iterate_structure, as the footprint of grey_dilation and the others. | A condition (is_above= ...) works on patches instead. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** No empty cells. |  |
| `grid.find_distance_to_nearest()` | The straight-line (Euclidean) distance, exact (SciPy distance_transform_edt), from every cell that does not meet the condition to the nearest one that does. | test_method="city block", "chessboard" or "brute force"; cell_size= for cells that are not squares of size 1. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** At least one cell meets the condition. |  |
| `grid.split_touching_patches()` | Each starting point (from 1) gets its own patch number, in the order given; the neighbours touch by their sides. | touching="corners". | **P5.** SciPy watershed_ift with the markers in the order given. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Whole numbers from 0 to 65535 (grey levels). |  |
| `grid.move_and_stretch_by()` | Straight lines between neighbouring cells (order 1), and cells from outside the old grid are empty. | test_method="nearest" or "cubic", as change_size_to. | **P5.** Straight lines never overshoot the numbers; empty cells say plainly where the old grid ended (as grid.rotate). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** matrix= is a 2 x 2 grid; no empty cells. |  |
| `grid.move_and_stretch_by()` `rule=` | The rule's row and column count from 1, as every position in simplydata. | matrix= and shift= for straight-line moves. | **P5.** Positions count from 1 everywhere in simplydata. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Formulas of row and column only. |  |
| `grid.move_and_stretch_by()` `test_method="fourier"` | shift= says where each new cell takes its number from, as for matrix= (SciPy affine_transform's offset), so SciPy fourier_shift is given minus shift=. | test_method="linear" without wrapping round. | **P5.** One meaning of shift= for every test_method=. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** shift= alone. |  |
| `grid.convolve_with()` | Cells outside the grid count as 0 (SciPy convolve2d's boundary "fill"), and keep="all" keeps every overlap, as numbers.convolve_with. | keep="same" or "overlap"; flip=False for a correlation. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A kernel of numbers; no empty cells. |  |
| `grid.convolve_with()` `row_weights=` | At the edges the edge numbers are repeated (as SciPy mode "reflect") and the grid keeps its size, as SciPy sepfir2d. | A kernel grid with convolve2d (cells outside count as 0). | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Two lists of an odd number of weights. |  |
| `grid.find_permanent()` | SymPy per on the grid's numbers as exact fractions, given as a decimal (exactly=True gives the exact answer), for grids up to 12 x 12. | exactly=True for the exact answer as a formula. | **P3.** SciPy and NumPy have no permanent; exact fractions add no rounding on the way, and the permanent takes about n! steps, so 12 x 12 is the limit (as for the exact determinant). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A square grid with a number in every cell. | [Meurer et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.7717/peerj-cs.103) |

## Numbers: undo and finish

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `numbers.turn_into_table()` | One column called "value". | name= any column name. | **P5.** A plain name that says what the column holds when nothing else is known. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** name= must be text. |  |
| `numbers.start_chart()` | The numbers become a table with one column called "value" (a grid: columns "1", "2" ... and rows numbered from 1). | turn_into_table(name=) first, then sdata.chart(), for another column name. | **P5.** The chart words name columns, so the numbers need a column name; the same as turn_into_table(). *Basis: starting value.* |  |
| `numbers.save()` | One column called "value", without row numbers. | turn_into_table(name=), then the table's own save word, for another name; .wav with time_step= and bits=. | **P5.** A file without pandas' row labels reads back as it was with pandas read_csv or R read.csv; simplydata's own reader needs separator="," for a file of one column (a current limitation). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** time_step= and bits= only go with a .wav file. |  |
| `numbers.as_numpy()` | A copy of the numbers as they are now. | None. | **P5.** A copy means changing the array never changes the simplydata numbers or their steps. *Basis: convention.* |  |

## Numbers: yes or no

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `numbers.turn_into_yes_or_no()` | An empty number is no; the numbers themselves do not change (new yes or no values come back). | is_empty=True for yes where the numbers are empty; & \| ~ to join tests. | **P3.** As NumPy, where nan > 20 is False, so the answer matches the NumPy line; keeping the numbers lets the same values make several tests. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** One condition at a time, as keep_each_number_that. |  |
| `numbers.check_if_all_numbers()` | Empty numbers are skipped, and the sentence says how many were skipped. | count_empty_numbers() and replace_each_number_that(is_empty=True, with_value=...) first, to decide them yourself. | **P5.** The same rule as every simplydata summary (empty values are skipped, with a note); NumPy's np.all would say False for any nan. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** One condition at a time. |  |

## Numbers: rounding, angles and positions

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `numbers.take_sin()` | Angles in radians, as NumPy; degrees=True for degrees. | degrees=True; change_angles_to("degrees") or ("radians") to change the numbers themselves. | **P3.** Radians keep the printed NumPy line exactly the same as the word (owner decision 2026-11-25); the sentence says which unit was used every time. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** degrees= is True or False. |  |
| `numbers.find_where_biggest()` | The position counted from 1 (NumPy's index plus 1); empty values skipped (np.nanargmax); on a tie, the first. | over= for one position along a direction of a grid or block. | **P5.** Positions count from 1 everywhere in simplydata (approved 2026-11-14); the sentence gives the NumPy index too, so the bridge to NumPy is exact. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** All-empty numbers are refused. |  |

## Blocks of numbers

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sdata.number_block()` `keep_on_disk=` | Off: the numbers of a file are read at once. keep_on_disk=True leaves them in the file; keep_where, keep_by_position_in and get_numbers_nearest_to cut it there (xarray isel), and any other word reads them once, with a note. | keep_on_disk=True for a file bigger than memory; cut it before the word that reads it. | **P5.** Reading at once is what a beginner expects (every word works at once); xarray's lazy opening is kept for big files without needing dask. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Several files (a pattern) are read at once; keep_on_disk= takes one file. |  |
| `block.find_average()` | over= names the directions that go and in_each= the ones kept; empty values are skipped (np.nanmean). | over=[...] for several; no option for one number for the whole block. | **P5.** Named directions remove NumPy's axis numbers and keepdims=True: the result keeps its names and lines up with the block by name (NumPy brief D4, approved 2026-11-15). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Unknown names are refused with the names the block has. |  |
| `block.keep_where()` | The range is taken in either order, and turned round when the labels run downward (xarray sel). | is_one_of= for exact labels; keep_by_position_in for positions. | **P4.** Many ocean and climate files store latitude from north to south; xarray then gives nothing for a range written low to high, with no error. Turning it round removes that trap. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** A direction without labels is refused, with the position word to use. |  |

## Blocks: shape and directions

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `block.change_shape_to()` | The numbers are read row by row (NumPy's C order), and one_row_per= must be the first direction. | move_direction() first, to put another direction first; fill="repeat" for a new size (np.resize). | **P4.** NumPy's own order, so the result equals reshape; a reshape that would mix directions is refused instead of scrambling them silently (NumPy brief D5). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** The sizes must multiply to the count of numbers. |  |
| `block.rotate()` `by_degrees=` | The size and labels are kept (reshape=False) and the corners that come from outside are empty; straight lines fill in between the numbers. | test_method="nearest" or "cubic"; by_quarter_turns= for quarter turns without filling in. | **P4.** An empty corner is honest about places with no data, where SciPy's default would put 0, a number that was never measured. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Empty values are refused. |  |

## Blocks: files

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `block.save()` | A .nc file is written as netCDF-4 with the h5netcdf engine, every variable compressed (zlib, level 4), with the directions' names and labels. | Another ending (.csv, .xlsx, .parquet ...) writes the block's long table instead. | **P3.** netCDF-4 is the usual file of ocean and climate grids and keeps names, labels and units with the numbers; compression loses nothing (the numbers come back exactly) and level 4 is the middle of zlib's range. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Web addresses are refused: simplydata opens only the file you name; it never downloads or sends anything. |  |

## Blocks: labels, dates and places

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `block.find_values_at()` | Straight lines between the neighbouring labels (test_method="linear"), along each direction named; places outside the labels are empty. | test_method="nearest" or "cubic". | **P3.** The same rule as table.find_values_at and xarray interp (SciPy interpn underneath), so the numbers agree with both; nothing is extended beyond the data without being asked. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Text labels are refused; the labels must run steadily up or down. |  |
| `block.smooth()` | A moving average of window=5 places along each direction named; the edges without a full window are empty (ends="empty"), as numbers.smooth. | ends="reflect" or "mirror" (SciPy's modes of those names); test_method="gaussian" (width=2) or "median". | **P3.** The same starting values as numbers.smooth (R stats::filter leaves the ends empty), so a block smoothed along one direction gives what each list of numbers would give. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** window= is odd, so it is centred; empty values are refused. |  |
| `block.change_size_to()` | Straight lines between the numbers (SciPy ndimage.zoom, order 1); number labels are filled in the same way. | test_method="nearest" (order 0) or "cubic" (order 3). | **P5.** Linear filling in makes no new highs or lows (cubic can overshoot), so the new numbers stay within the old ones. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** Empty values are refused; sizes are whole numbers. |  |
| `grid.smooth()` | Along the rows and the columns together (a picture blurred); for "gaussian" and "median" the edge number is repeated (SciPy mode "reflect"), and the bell curve is cut at 4 standard deviations. | over="row" or "column" for one direction; window=, width=; ends="mirror" (SciPy mode "mirror"). | **P3.** SciPy ndimage's own defaults, so the grid equals gaussian_filter and median_filter in scipy.ndimage. *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Empty values are refused. |  |
| `grid.smooth()` `test_method="biggest"` | A square window of window= cells; at the edges the edge number is repeated (SciPy mode "reflect"). | ends="mirror" or "nearest"; over="row" or "column" for one direction. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** An odd window of at least 3 cells; no empty cells. |  |
| `grid.smooth()` `test_method="smallest"` | Along one direction (over=), the 1d filter (SciPy minimum_filter1d), with the edge number repeated. | Without over=: a square window (minimum_filter). | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** An odd window of at least 3 cells. |  |
| `grid.smooth()` `test_method="rank"` | rank= counts from 1 in the sorted window (SciPy's rank from 0). | test_method="percentile" with percentile= for a share of the window. | **P5.** Positions count from 1 everywhere in simplydata. *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** rank= from 1 to the number of cells in the window. |  |
| `grid.smooth()` `test_method="fourier gaussian"` | The smoothing is done in the frequencies (NumPy fft2, then the real part of ifft2), so the picture wraps round at its edges. | test_method="gaussian" smooths in the cells, with ends=. | **P5.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Both directions; no ends=. |  |
| `grid.smooth()` `test_method="spline"` | A cubic smoothing spline surface with smoothness=5, SciPy signal.spline_filter's own default (lmbda=5.0). | smoothness=0 goes through every number; bigger values smooth more. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** A grid big enough for SciPy's edge sums to settle (about 30 x 30 at smoothness=5). |  |
| `grid.smooth()` `ends="zeros"` | The cells outside the grid count as 0, as SciPy signal.order_filter. | ends="reflect" (the default for test_method="rank") repeats the edge number. | **P3.** SciPy's own default, so the numbers agree with SciPy (and with R and MATLAB, which call the same routines). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** test_method="rank" over both directions. |  |

## Numbers: chances and random picks

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `numbers.make_random_picks()` | Each pick is put back (putting_back=True), each number equally likely; with_chances= must add up to 1. | putting_back=False; with_chances=[...]; with_seed= to repeat the picks. | **P3.** Putting back is NumPy's choice default and what a bootstrap sample needs. Chances that do not add up to 1 are refused rather than divided by their total, so no weight is changed silently (decision 5). *Basis: convention.* **Checks:** Without putting back, at most as many picks as numbers. |  |
| `numbers.take_log_odds()` | 0, 1 and numbers outside 0 to 1 become empty, with a note. | replace_each_number_that first, to move 0 and 1 a little inside, if your field does that. | **P5.** SciPy gives -inf and inf for 0 and 1; simplydata's rule for a sum with no answer is an empty value and a note, never infinity (the same as take_log). *Basis: starting value.* **Checks:** The note counts the numbers that became empty. |  |

## Waves and systems

| Word or option | Default | Alternatives | Why | References |
|---|---|---|---|---|
